  **Real gap found while checking data-sourcing readiness: `scripts/prepare_data.py --source` only supports `fineweb-edu`.** None of DCLM-Edu, Stack-Edu, FineMath, or Cosmopedia V2 have any existing loading path in `data.py`/`prepare_data.py` -- each would need real, separate work (a new HF streaming-dataset integration, its own schema/field mapping, and a license/provenance check per `AGENTS.md`'s data-manifest discipline), not a config change. This is a substantial, multi-dataset undertaking distinct from the mechanism work above, and is the next real step before this task's own comparison can run for real -- not started yet, flagged here rather than assumed quick.
  **Three of four new sources implemented and tested (2026-09-10), verified against each dataset's own real HuggingFace schema before writing any loader code (not assumed from a similarly-named dataset).** A verification pass confirmed real schema for all four proposed sources, with one real blocker found (Stack-Edu, see below). New `src/minifrontier/data.py` loaders, each mirroring `iter_fineweb_edu`'s exact shape and pinned to a real commit SHA fetched directly from the HF Hub API (not guessed): `iter_dclm_edu` (`HuggingFaceTB/dclm-edu`, filters on the real `edu_int_score` field, range 2-5 confirmed -- `start`/`limit` deliberately count only post-filter admitted rows, a documented divergence from `iter_fineweb_edu`'s raw-stream-position convention, since a caller asking for N documents from a heavily-filtered source wants N real usable ones, not an unpredictable smaller count); `iter_finemath` (`HuggingFaceTB/finemath`, real four-config choice -- `finemath-3plus`/`finemath-4plus`/`infiwebmath-3plus`/`infiwebmath-4plus` -- defaulted to `finemath-4plus`, a real recorded decision, not silently assumed); `iter_cosmopedia_v2` (lives inside `HuggingFaceTB/smollm-corpus`'s `cosmopedia-v2` config, not the differently-named standalone `HuggingFaceTB/cosmopedia` repo -- confirmed before use). Wired into `scripts/prepare_data.py --source {dclm-edu,finemath,cosmopedia-v2}` (plus `--dclm-min-score`/`--finemath-config`), not left as a library-only gap. 5 new tests in `tests/test_data.py` (provenance/config-selection correctness per source, plus the DCLM-Edu post-filter counting semantics specifically). Full fast suite: 422 passed, `ruff check`/`format --check` clean.
  **Stack-Edu (the code component) is a real, verified blocker, resolved by user decision (2026-09-10).** Its actual HuggingFace schema (confirmed directly, not assumed) holds only Software Heritage blob metadata (`repo_name`, `path`, `license_type`, `blob_id`) -- no code text at all. Real content requires a separate blob-fetch pipeline from Software Heritage's S3 store (`boto3`, auth, gzip decompression per blob), infrastructure this project does not have. **Decision: do both, and both before [[MF-070]]'s launch, not staged** -- (1) substitute this project's own already-real, already-license-checked code corpus ([[MF-094]]'s 57-file pool, `data/mf094-code-approved/`) as an interim code component so the mixture comparison can start now; (2) also build the real Stack-Edu S3 pipeline (tracked as [[MF-110]]) and use genuine Stack-Edu content as the code component for [[MF-070]]'s actual run -- **user-clarified (2026-09-10) this is required before [[MF-070]], not deferred follow-up work**, superseding [[MF-110]]'s own initial "does not block MF-070" framing. [[MF-110]]'s status note below is corrected to match.
- **A third real precedent, verified (2026-09-10):** Qwen3's own technical report (arXiv:2505.09388, fetched directly, not assumed) confirms a real three-stage pretraining shape with exactly this structure -- general stage (30T tokens, seq_len 4096) → a reweighted stage upweighting STEM/code/reasoning/synthetic data (+5T tokens) → a long-context extension stage (hundreds of billions of tokens, seq_len 32,768, 75% of text 16K-32K tokens long). Joins SmolLM2/MiniCPM as a third independent, current, real-world confirmation of "different data composition in different training phases," at a scale/lab with no compute constraint forcing the choice -- not new evidence this project's own bounded budget requires it, but a stronger prior that the technique is real and worth the eventual comparison once [[MF-083]]/[[MF-094]] land.
- **A fourth, most directly relevant precedent, verified (2026-09-11): SmolLM3 (the direct successor to SmolLM2, whose fixed mixture [[MF-094]]'s own proposal was modeled on) abandoned the fixed-mixture approach entirely for a real 3-stage evolving one.** Fetched directly from HuggingFace's own SmolLM3 blog post, real quoted numbers across 11.2T total tokens: Stage 1 (0-8T) web 85%/code 12%/math 3%; Stage 2 (8-10T) web 75%/code 15%/math 10% (adds Stack-Edu); Stage 3 (10-11.1T) web 63%/code 24%/math 13% (adds OpenMathReasoning). The article's own words: "Following SmolLM2's multi-stage approach, we train SmolLM3... using a three-stage training strategy that mixes web, math, and code data with evolving proportions" -- an explicit, stated departure from SmolLM2's own fixed ratio. Real, useful cross-check: this task's already-proposed 5-source composition (DCLM-Edu 45% + FineWeb-Edu 30% = 75% web, code 15%, math 5%+5% synthetic) lands almost exactly on SmolLM3's own **Stage 2** point on web/code, close on math -- not claimed as validating evidence (coincidence is plausible at this resolution), but a real, notable alignment worth recording. Strengthens the case that this task's own real comparison should test a staged/decay-phase version against the fixed proposal, not just accept the fixed one once validated -- exactly this task's own original question, now with a fourth real precedent from the most directly comparable model family available.

### MF-096 — Benchmark SDPA-with-explicit-window-mask against eager FlexAttention and manual attention for the hybrid Modern preset's *training* throughput

- **Priority / state:** P1 / Done — real result: the current default already wins, no change made
- **Depends on:** none (all three implementations already exist and are already correctness-tested)
- **Deliverable:** a real training-throughput comparison (not just the existing prefill/decode inference numbers) of all three already-implemented local-layer attention paths on `150m-modern.toml`: eager FlexAttention (`auto`'s current choice for local layers), the manual teaching path, and — genuinely untested until now — `attention_impl="sdpa"` forced explicitly, which bypasses `auto`'s per-layer routing entirely and uses `F.scaled_dot_product_attention` with an explicit banded window mask (`src/minifrontier/attention.py` lines ~405-418) for *every* layer, local and global alike.
- **Acceptance:** a real matched-token or matched-wall-clock training comparison (same bounded-comparison discipline as every other `reports/mf070-*` experiment) reports tokens/second and peak VRAM for all three paths on the same hybrid config, real data, real hardware; if forced-SDPA wins, `ModelConfig.attention_impl_for_layer`'s `"auto"` resolution rule is revisited (currently always `"flex"` for local layers) with the change recorded as a decision, not applied silently.
- **Status note:** raised by a fourth round of external feedback ("your eager local FlexAttention path is painfully slow, find better solutions"), which is not new information on its own — this exact problem is already thoroughly documented (`reports/mf050-rtx2070s-profile-matrix.md` Finding 5/7: eager FlexAttention 6.4x slower than manual on prefill for this hybrid preset; [[MF-078]], Done, real Inductor bug confirmed on both CPU and CUDA that blocks compiling FlexAttention directly on this PyTorch build). But re-reading the actual comparisons already run turned up a genuine, previously-unexplored gap: every existing benchmark compares **flex vs manual** (or, for full-attention Edu models, **manual vs SDPA**) — never **flex vs SDPA** for the hybrid Modern preset's local layers specifically, even though `attention.py` already implements a correct, already-used (for global layers, and for local layers' ring-cache decode step) SDPA-with-explicit-mask path. Since MF-077's own report already found "manual is consistently slower than SDPA... on full-attention models" and SDPA's fused kernel typically beats a from-scratch matmul+softmax implementation, forced SDPA is a real, plausible (not yet confirmed) candidate to beat manual — and per Finding 7, manual already beats eager-flex by 6.4x. If SDPA beats manual too, this could be a meaningful, immediately-usable training-throughput win for MF-070's actual 350M run, with zero new kernel code required (only a config value change). Sequence-length note: SDPA's explicit mask is `O(sequence_length^2)` memory, unlike FlexAttention's `O(1)`-memory `mask_mod` description — negligible at this project's current `sequence_length` (1024-2048, a few MB at most) but a real reason `auto` might have preferred flex "in principle" for future longer-context work; this task's real measurement should settle whether that theoretical preference is worth today's real measured cost.
  **Done (2026-09-08): the hypothesis was wrong — measured, not assumed.** `reports/mf096-attention-impl-training-throughput.md`: real 200-update bounded runs (409,200 tokens/arm, `train/pretrain.py --attention-impl {auto,sdpa,manual} --no-checkpoint`, RTX 2070 Super, real data). **`auto` (the current default) is the fastest of all three, by a wide margin**: 4,306.4 tok/s at 5.63GB peak reserved VRAM, vs forced-SDPA's 2,109.7 tok/s (**2.04x slower**, and +1.48GB *more* memory, not less) and forced-manual's 1,290.3 tok/s (3.34x slower, +2.24GB more memory). The expected mechanism, not independently re-verified but consistent with well-documented PyTorch behavior: supplying SDPA an explicit custom `attn_mask` (rather than `is_causal=True` or no mask) disables its fastest fused/Flash-Attention-style kernel path, forcing a slower backend, while also paying the real (if modest at this project's sequence length) memory cost of materializing that mask. MF-050's real finding — manual beats eager FlexAttention *for prefill* — does not generalize to training throughput, where this project's existing `"auto"` heuristic (FlexAttention for local layers, SDPA for global layers) turns out to already be the right choice, empirically, not just a plausible default. **No code change made** — `attention_impl_for_layer`'s `"auto"` rule is confirmed correct as-is; MF-070's real 350M run proceeds with the unmodified default. A real, useful negative result: closes the question cheaply (all three runs together took well under 15 minutes of real GPU time) rather than leaving a plausible-sounding but wrong hypothesis unverified.

### MF-097 — Compare packing strategies: continuous-ribbon (current) vs. best-fit variants

- **Priority / state:** P2 / Done — real bounded comparison ran 2026-09-11, ribbon confirmed as [[MF-070]]'s default, see final status note
- **Depends on:** none structurally; complements rather than replaces [[MF-085]]
- **Deliverable:** two distinct alternative packing modes, not one — verified via `WebSearch` to be genuinely different algorithms, not two names for the same thing: (1) **Best-fit Packing** (Ding et al., arXiv:2404.10830, AWS AI Labs) — length-aware combinatorial optimization that groups documents into sequences to minimize truncation *without* discarding tokens or losing concatenation's training efficiency; (2) nanochat's **BOS-aligned best-fit-and-crop** — each row starts fresh with `<BOS>` plus one document, best-fit-filling remaining space with more whole documents and *cropping* (discarding) a document's tail when it doesn't fit, trading real token volume for guaranteed-clean boundaries. Both compared against the current continuous-ribbon default. (A third candidate, **Dataset Decomposition** (Meta, arXiv:2405.13226), was considered and split out to its own task, [[MF-111]], after real scoping showed it to be a structurally different, much larger undertaking — see the 2026-09-10 note below.)
- **Acceptance:** all packing modes produce valid shards passing the existing shard-integrity tests; a real bounded comparison (matched tokens/seed/tokenizer) measures each against the current ribbon default. Best-fit Packing (1) is the stronger candidate to test first — its own published results (real reading-comprehension/context-following/program-synthesis gains, real hallucination reduction, see status note) come with *no* stated token-efficiency cost, unlike nanochat's crop-based approach, whose real published trade (~35% of document tokens cropped at `sequence_length=2048`) this project should not accept without measuring against its own data/scale.
- **Status note:** raised by a fourth round of external feedback, citing both nanochat's design and, independently and more rigorously, Ding et al. (arXiv:2404.10830, "Fewer Truncations Improve Language Modeling") — verified via `WebSearch` before recording, real paper, real AWS AI Labs authorship, real reported results (+4.7% reading comprehension, +16.8% context following, +9.2% program synthesis, up to −58.3% closed-domain hallucination). This is a different mechanism from [[MF-085]]'s document-boundary masking — MF-085 keeps every token from the current ribbon-packing scheme but masks cross-document attention, while this task changes *which documents get packed together at all*. The two are complementary and could in principle be combined (best-fit packing plus intra-document masking within whatever multi-document rows remain) — worth comparing independently first per the reviewer's own suggestion. Raised from P3 to P2 after verifying Best-fit Packing's real published results are more substantial and less caveated (no stated efficiency cost) than the nanochat crop-based variant this task was originally scoped around alone. Not started; still requires reworking the packing/writer path more invasively than a config flag, same real cost as originally scoped.
- **Additional research pass (2026-09-10), before this task is picked up — real findings, each tagged with confidence.** (1) **Intra-document attention masking (i.e. [[MF-085]]'s own mechanism) is real, dominant industry practice, not a niche idea** — verified directly against Llama 3's primary source (arXiv:2407.21783, §3.2): *"We use an attention mask that prevents self-attention between different documents within the same sequence."* The paper adds a real, useful nuance MF-085 doesn't currently record: masking *"had limited impact during standard pre-training, but we find it to be important in continued pre-training on very long sequences"* — a real signal that the effect may be sequence-length-dependent, worth folding into MF-085's own eventual bounded test design (e.g. testing at more than one sequence length, not just this project's current 1024/2048). Verified against primary source. (2) **"Llama 3 uses Best-fit Packing" is a real, likely-incorrect attribution circulating in secondary sources (blogs, Amazon Science summaries) — not stated anywhere in the actual Llama 3 paper**, which documents only the causal cross-document mask above, not Ding et al.'s algorithm. Do not cite Llama 3 as Best-fit-Packing precedent without a primary-source citation actually supporting it. (3) **Dataset Decomposition (Meta, arXiv:2405.13226, NeurIPS 2024)** is a genuinely different, not-yet-recorded mechanism: bucket documents by length and train with a curriculum over variable sequence lengths/batch sizes instead of concatenating to one fixed length, so compute cost scales with real document length. Its own reported result is in this project's actual size regime, unlike Ding et al.'s larger-scale numbers — an 8k-context **1B-parameter** model trained at the same compute cost as a 2k-context baseline, up to 6x faster. Verified via a fetched paper summary (not the raw PDF text). (4) Two lower-relevance items, secondary-source-only, not independently verified: Cross-Lingual Document Attention/XLDA (Trillion 7B report, arXiv:2504.15431 — a refinement of intra-document masking for multilingual corpora, not applicable unless this project trains multilingual data) and a claim that DeepSeek-V3 also uses boundary-respecting packing (could not confirm from the primary PDF). (5) The mechanism traces back at least to Kosec et al. (Graphcore, arXiv:2107.02027, "Efficient Sequence Packing without Cross-contamination") as the original precedent, not a 2024+ invention — found via search, not independently fetched. **Net addition to this task's scope**: items (1) and (3) are real, verified-or-better candidates worth adding to whatever bounded comparison this task eventually runs (Dataset Decomposition as a third real alternative alongside the two already recorded; the sequence-length-dependence nuance from (1) as a reason to test more than one sequence length if [[MF-085]]'s masking is combined with this task's own comparison). Nothing here changes this task's still-not-started status or priority.
- **Progress (2026-09-10): both required packing algorithms implemented and unit-tested; wiring into `TokenShardWriter` and the real bounded GPU comparison remain open.** New `src/minifrontier/packing.py`: `pack_best_fit` (Best-Fit-Decreasing bin packing over a buffer of already-tokenized, EOS-terminated documents -- sorts longest-first, slots each into the tightest-fitting partially-filled row, opens a new row otherwise; an oversized document is pre-split into full-length chunks plus one packable remainder, so every token is preserved, verified by a dedicated test asserting total returned non-padding count equals the sum of input document lengths exactly) and `pack_bos_aligned_crop` (nanochat-style: each row starts fresh with BOS, best-fit-fills with whole documents in order, and crops the next document's head to fill any remaining room when nothing else fits -- the cropped tail is discarded for good; a document too long for even one row is cropped down to exactly one row rather than continued onto a second). 10 new tests in `tests/test_packing.py`, including a constructed crop scenario proving specific discarded tokens never appear anywhere in the output and that real content-preserved is strictly less than input volume, matching the paper's own disclosed trade. Deliberately paused here (real priority shift, 2026-09-10: the user wants [[MF-070]]'s actual 9-day run trained on the validated 5-source mixture from [[MF-095]], not launched on a schedule) to prioritize [[MF-095]]'s external-corpus sourcing work, which is now a hard prerequisite for [[MF-070]] rather than a nice-to-have -- resumed after that. Remaining for this task: wire both functions into `TokenShardWriter` (a `packing: Literal["ribbon","best_fit","bos_crop"]` mode buffering whole documents instead of ribboning immediately when non-default), and the real bounded matched-token GPU comparison this task's acceptance criterion requires.
- **`TokenShardWriter` wiring done (2026-09-10).** `packing`/`pack_buffer_documents` parameters added (default `"ribbon"`, byte-for-byte unchanged behavior when omitted); non-ribbon modes buffer whole tokenized (EOS-terminated) documents and run the chosen `packing.py` function once the buffer reaches `pack_buffer_documents`, flushing packed rows through the existing `_append_sequence`/shard-flush path unchanged. `finalize()` branches on mode: ribbon keeps its existing `drop_remainder` leftover-fragment semantics exactly as before; `best_fit`/`bos_crop` always flush their final partial batch regardless of `drop_remainder` (a padded row from either can still hold real, already-admitted document content, unlike ribbon's unambiguous incomplete fragment -- documented explicitly, not silently different). `ShardManifest` gained an additive `packing: str = "ribbon"` field (defaulted so a manifest written before this field existed still loads under the old default, verified by a dedicated backward-compatibility test) so a shard pool's own manifest records which mode produced it, not just the run command that built it -- `prepare_data.py --packing {ribbon,best_fit,bos_crop} --pack-buffer-documents N` wires this through the real CLI, not left library-only. 8 new tests (`tests/test_shards.py`): rejection of an unknown mode/non-positive buffer size, `best_fit` never discarding a token (verified against independently-recomputed expected totals), `bos_crop` prepending BOS to every real emitted row, a partial buffer still flushing at `finalize()` rather than being silently dropped, and the manifest backward-compatibility default. Full fast suite: **427 passed** (up from 422), `ruff check`/`format --check` clean.
- **Dataset Decomposition split out to its own task, [[MF-111]], and explicitly deferred until after [[MF-070]] (user decision, 2026-09-10).** The real algorithm was verified against the paper's own HTML (arXiv:2405.13226) before this decision, not assumed from the abstract alone -- see [[MF-111]] for the full precise mechanism and the real scoping estimate that drove the deferral (comparable in size to everything else built for this task combined: a new bucket-decomposition shard writer, a variable-shape batch provider, and a curriculum schedule, none of which exist yet). **This task's own real bounded comparison now covers ribbon (baseline) vs. `best_fit` vs. `bos_crop` only** -- both already implemented and tested above -- which is what actually runs before [[MF-070]].

  **Real result (2026-09-11), `reports/mf097-packing-comparison.md`, full detail there.** All three arms ran for real overnight (150m-modern, RTX 2070 Super, FP16, seed 42, 5000 updates/~10.2M tokens each). First evaluation pass (each checkpoint against its own packing-dependent validation split) showed best_fit +0.114% relative CE vs. ribbon (weak) and bos_crop an apparent **−1.424%** win (31.0x [[MF-088]]'s noise floor, large) -- but bos_crop's own validation split was a genuinely different, ~half-sized sample of held-out bytes (packing changes which packed rows land in validation), a real confound. **Corrected (2026-09-11): re-evaluated all three checkpoints against one shared validation set** (`data/shards/mf064-150m-train/validation`, since a model's forward pass doesn't care how its training data was packed) -- **the apparent bos_crop win completely disappears and reverses**: best_fit +0.297% relative CE vs. ribbon (6.4x noise floor), bos_crop +0.308% (6.7x noise floor) -- both real, small, consistent *losses*, not gains, at nearly identical magnitude. The entire earlier "win" was purely the validation-set-size artifact, not a training-quality effect -- a real, concrete demonstration of exactly the kind of confound this project's own methodology exists to catch (same class as the MF-087 tokenizer confound). **Decision: ribbon stays [[MF-070]]'s default, now with clean, trustworthy evidence and no open follow-up.** best_fit's throughput is also slightly worse (3,971.8 vs 4,057.9 tok/s); bos_crop's is roughly on par but discards over half the real training tokens for a real loss, not a gain. This task is fully Done -- the earlier-recorded follow-up (bos_crop on a matched validation split) has now been run and answered negatively, closing the question rather than leaving it open.

- **MF-110 dropped, superseded by a real philosophical decision (2026-09-10): this project will not require paid/credentialed cloud access for anyone to reproduce its data pipeline.** MF-110's own real blocker (verified directly against BigCode's official `the-stack-v2` documentation) is that fetching real Stack-Edu content requires a real, credentialed AWS account (`AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID`/`AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY`) even though the S3 bucket is nominally "public" -- every official example signs requests with a real account. See the research pass immediately below.
  **Real replacement implemented and tested (2026-09-10): `codeparrot/github-code`.** Research (real dataset-card verification) found it fully ungated, no credentials needed, with a real per-file `code`/`license`/`language`/`repo_name` field -- confirmed directly, not assumed, including a real empirical correction to the initial research: the dataset's own `languages=`/`licenses=` `load_dataset` kwargs do **not** actually filter under `streaming=True` on the pinned revision used here (tested directly: requesting `languages=["Python"], licenses=["mit"]` still returned JavaScript/GPL-2.0 rows) -- `iter_github_code` (new, `src/minifrontier/data.py`) filters client-side instead, normalizing real lowercase SPDX-style license strings (verified via a real shuffled sample: `apache-2.0, cc0-1.0, mit, unlicense` directly observed and correctly normalized, plus many non-permissive ones like `gpl-3.0`/`lgpl-2.1`/`mpl-2.0`/`epl-1.0`/`agpl-3.0` correctly rejected before `Document.create` ever sees them, not after -- letting the license-gate exception handle filtering would have crashed the whole stream on the first bad row; `bsd-2-clause`/`bsd-3-clause` were not directly observed in this sample but map through the identical normalization logic, not a separate unverified code path). Supports an optional `languages` filter and an optional `repo_names` allowlist (the mechanism for restricting to only the curated repos below, rather than an unfiltered crawl of this dataset's full 115M files/873GB). Wired into `scripts/prepare_data.py --source github-code` with `--github-languages`/`--github-repo-allowlist`. 4 new tests in `tests/test_data.py` (license-gate filtering, language/repo-allowlist filtering, post-filter `start`/`limit` counting semantics matching `iter_dclm_edu`'s own convention, real kwargs sent to `load_dataset`). **Real, live smoke test against the actual dataset** (not just mocked): the full pipeline (161-repo curated allowlist below, real streaming, real license/language attribution) pulled 5 real files from `spring-projects/spring-framework`, `apache/flink`, `eslint/eslint` with correct metadata -- confirmed working end to end, not just unit-tested. Full fast suite: **431 passed** (up from 427), `ruff check`/`format --check` clean.
  **Real curated repo allowlist compiled (2026-09-10), `configs/code-repo-allowlist.txt`, 161 repos across 12 languages** (JS, TS, Python, Go, C#, C++, Rust, Java, Kotlin, Elixir, HTML, CSS), weighted by real popularity evidence (GitHub Octoverse 2025, TIOBE Sept 2026 -- Python/JS/TS get the most entries, Java/C++/C#/Go/Rust a solid middle tier, Kotlin/Elixir/HTML/CSS fewer) rather than a flat per-language count, per explicit user direction. Compiled from two independent real research passes, cross-checked against each other -- **a real, important catch surfaced along the way**: GitHub's own unauthenticated search results included several repos with implausible star counts for their visibility (a known star-farming abuse pattern, e.g. an obscure-looking repo credited with 244k★) -- these were explicitly excluded, not included on trust, and every entry actually in the allowlist is either independently verified or a widely-recognized, long-established real project (React, Django, Kubernetes, Spring Boot, etc.). Several popular-but-non-permissive near-misses were also correctly excluded rather than silently included: HashiCorp Terraform (MPL-2.0), JUnit5 (EPL-2.0), Boost/Catch2 (BSL-1.0), `animate.css` (Hippocratic License, not OSI-approved), various ISC-licensed npm packages (ISC is not in this project's allowed set). A real, useful structural finding: Rust's ecosystem convention of MIT/Apache-2.0 dual-licensing means most popular Rust crates clear this project's allowlist by default, a real advantage over other language ecosystems. This is recorded as a real starting list, not exhaustive -- extendable the same way later.
  **Real commands, ready for the user's own terminal (2026-09-10) -- data prep for all five real sources, then a real 3-arm bounded comparison.** Same reliability rationale as every other real GPU/long-running-prep command this session (both multi-hour GPU training and long CPU/network-bound data prep have each been unreliable inside this session's own background tooling at least once). FineWeb-Edu and the 70/30 web/code split both already have real prepared shards (`data/shards/mf064-150m-train`, `data/shards/mf094-code-train`) and need no new prep; the four other sources are new:
    ```

    ./.venv/Scripts/python.exe scripts/prepare_data.py --source finemath --finemath-config finemath-4plus --limit 2500 --shuffle-seed 123 --tokenizer data/tokenizer --output data/shards/mf095-finemath-train --sequence-length 1024 --validation-fraction 0.01
    ./.venv/Scripts/python.exe scripts/prepare_data.py --source cosmopedia-v2 --limit 2500 --shuffle-seed 123 --tokenizer data/tokenizer --output data/shards/mf095-cosmopedia-v2-train --sequence-length 1024 --validation-fraction 0.01
    ./.venv/Scripts/python.exe scripts/prepare_data.py --source github-code --github-repo-allowlist configs/code-repo-allowlist.txt --limit 3000 --shuffle-seed 123 --tokenizer data/tokenizer --output data/shards/mf095-github-code-train --sequence-length 1024 --validation-fraction 0.01

    # Arm A: the proposed SmolLM2-modeled 5-source mixture (DCLM-Edu 45 / FineWeb-Edu 30 / code 15 / FineMath 5 / Cosmopedia-v2 5)
    ./.venv/Scripts/python.exe train/pretrain.py --config configs/150m-modern.toml --output artifacts/mf095-mixture/proposed-5-source --updates 5000 --batch-size 2 --seed 42 --device cuda --keep-last-n-checkpoints 2 --mixture "dclm;data/shards/mf095-dclm-edu-train/train;0.45" --mixture "web;data/shards/mf064-150m-train/train;0.30" --mixture "code;data/shards/mf095-github-code-train/train;0.15" --mixture "math;data/shards/mf095-finemath-train/train;0.05" --mixture "synthetic;data/shards/mf095-cosmopedia-v2-train/train;0.05"

    # Arm A2: same proposed mixture, WSD schedule, no decay-phase reweight -- the clean baseline Arm A3 is compared against (since --decay-mixture requires --schedule wsd), so the curriculum's own effect isn't confounded with the schedule-type change
    ./.venv/Scripts/python.exe train/pretrain.py --config configs/150m-modern.toml --output artifacts/mf095-mixture/wsd-fixed --updates 5000 --batch-size 2 --seed 42 --device cuda --keep-last-n-checkpoints 2 --schedule wsd --mixture "dclm;data/shards/mf095-dclm-edu-train/train;0.45" --mixture "web;data/shards/mf064-150m-train/train;0.30" --mixture "code;data/shards/mf095-github-code-train/train;0.15" --mixture "math;data/shards/mf095-finemath-train/train;0.05" --mixture "synthetic;data/shards/mf095-cosmopedia-v2-train/train;0.05"

    # Arm A3: MF-095's own real core question -- same mixture, WSD schedule, reweighted toward the highest-quality sources (Cosmopedia-v2, FineMath) once the decay phase starts
    ./.venv/Scripts/python.exe train/pretrain.py --config configs/150m-modern.toml --output artifacts/mf095-mixture/wsd-decay-curriculum --updates 5000 --batch-size 2 --seed 42 --device cuda --keep-last-n-checkpoints 2 --schedule wsd --mixture "dclm;data/shards/mf095-dclm-edu-train/train;0.45" --mixture "web;data/shards/mf064-150m-train/train;0.30" --mixture "code;data/shards/mf095-github-code-train/train;0.15" --mixture "math;data/shards/mf095-finemath-train/train;0.05" --mixture "synthetic;data/shards/mf095-cosmopedia-v2-train/train;0.05" --decay-mixture "dclm;0.35" --decay-mixture "web;0.15" --decay-mixture "math;0.15" --decay-mixture "synthetic;0.20"

    # Arm B: plain FineWeb-Edu single-source baseline (already real, no new prep)
    ./.venv/Scripts/python.exe train/pretrain.py --config configs/150m-modern.toml --train-shards data/shards/mf064-150m-train/train --output artifacts/mf095-mixture/fineweb-only --updates 5000 --batch-size 2 --seed 42 --device cuda --keep-last-n-checkpoints 2

    # Arm C: the already-tested MF-094 70/30 web/code split (already real, no new prep)
    ./.venv/Scripts/python.exe train/pretrain.py --config configs/150m-modern.toml --output artifacts/mf095-mixture/web-code-70-30 --updates 5000 --batch-size 2 --seed 42 --device cuda --keep-last-n-checkpoints 2 --mixture "web;data/shards/mf064-150m-train/train;0.7" --mixture "code;data/shards/mf094-code-train/train;0.3"
    ```
    Then evaluate all five via `scripts/eval_checkpoint.py` against `data/shards/mf064-150m-train/validation` (real held-out FineWeb-Edu validation, the one split common to all five arms, appropriate since the question is "which training mix generalizes best to real held-out text," not source-specific held-out performance) for real CE/PPL/BPB, and record in a new `reports/mf095-mixture-comparison.md`. If Arm A doesn't clearly beat both B and C, that is itself a real, useful result -- not a reason to assume the SmolLM2-modeled proposal transfers to this project's own data/scale untested. Compare Arm A3 (`wsd-decay-curriculum`) against Arm A2 (`wsd-fixed`) specifically to answer this task's own core question -- the decay-phase reweight's real effect, isolated from the schedule-type change both share.
  **Real gap found and fixed (2026-09-11): the comparison as originally queued never actually exercised this task's own core question.** All three original arms (A/B/C) used a plain fixed-ratio `--mixture`, none used `--decay-mixture` -- so the queued run would have settled "which fixed ratio wins" while leaving "does decay-phase reweighting help" (this task's actual title and stated question) completely untested, despite the `CurriculumMixtureProvider` mechanism already being built and tested. Caught by the user directly. Fixed by adding Arms A2/A3 above: a real, isolated WSD pair (fixed-mixture-under-WSD vs. decay-phase-reweighted-under-WSD) rather than naively comparing the curriculum arm against the existing cosine-schedule arms, which would have confounded the curriculum's real effect with an unrelated schedule-type change -- the same class of confound this project already caught and avoided once before (MF-087's tokenizer/digit-splitting confound). Decay-phase weights (`dclm;0.35 web;0.15 math;0.15 synthetic;0.20`, code unlisted so it keeps its stable-phase 0.15) shift toward the two most curated/highest-quality sources (Cosmopedia-v2, FineMath) and away from raw web text, per the real MiniCPM/SmolLM2/SmolLM3 "curated data in the decay phase" pattern this task was modeled on -- a real, reasoned choice, not arbitrary numbers. Both new arms added to the overnight orchestration scripts (`.ps1`/`.sh`) and the standalone commands above.
  **Scope decision (2026-09-11): 2-stage (stable/decay) curriculum confirmed sufficient, not SmolLM3's full N-stage (3-stage) approach.** Asked directly by the user after the SmolLM3 finding above. SmolLM3's 3 stages are proportioned for an **11.2-trillion**-token run; this project's real release target is **3 billion** tokens -- roughly 3,700x smaller, meaning a proportionally-scaled third stage would be a few hundred million tokens at most, likely too short to show a measurable effect against this project's own established seed-to-seed noise floor ([[MF-088]], ~0.046% relative CE). The 2-stage design already built also matches the actual papers this task was modeled on (MiniCPM/SmolLM2's own decay-phase framing is itself 2-stage). Generalizing `CurriculumMixtureProvider` to N stages later is a moderate, not huge, addition if this task's own real 2-stage result ever shows an effect worth pushing further -- not built speculatively now.
  **All three real command sets pressure-tested end to end before being handed to the user (2026-09-10), not just unit-tested.** Real (tiny-scale, CPU) smoke tests, not mocked: `prepare_data.py --source {dclm-edu,finemath,cosmopedia-v2,github-code,fineweb-edu}` each ran against the real live dataset and produced real admitted documents/shards (5/5 admitted each); `--packing best_fit`/`bos_crop` both ran against real FineWeb-Edu documents and produced real, correctly-shaped shards (a real, useful confirmation of the expected qualitative difference: `best_fit` preserved nearly all real tokens across 20 real documents, `bos_crop` used visibly less of the real admitted volume, matching its documented crop trade-off); a real `train/pretrain.py --mixture` run with all five real tiny pools trained 2 real updates without error; separate real runs confirmed `--packing best_fit`/`bos_crop` shards and `--optimizer cautious_adamw` all train cleanly through the real CLI. All scratch/smoke-test artifacts deleted afterward (confirmed untracked via `git status` before deletion, nothing real lost). This is the reason the exact commands recorded above and in [[MF-097]]/[[MF-083]] are trusted to actually work tonight, not just plausible-looking.
  **Real result (2026-09-11), `reports/mf095-mixture-comparison.md`, full detail there -- a real, decisive, and genuinely surprising result that overturns the working assumption this task started from.** All five arms ran for real overnight (150m-modern, RTX 2070 Super, FP16, seed 42, 5000 updates/~10.23M tokens each). **Every mixture arm scored worse than plain single-source FineWeb-Edu** on real held-out CE: `wsd-fixed` +0.954% relative CE (20.7x [[MF-088]]'s noise floor), `wsd-decay-curriculum` +1.717% (37.3x), `proposed-5-source` +2.811% (61.1x), `web-code-70-30` +4.002% (87.0x) -- all real, decisive effects, none plausibly noise. **This task's own actual core question, answered by the isolated pair** (`wsd-decay-curriculum` vs. `wsd-fixed`, both WSD, differing only in the decay-phase reweight): decay-phase reweighting toward Cosmopedia-v2/FineMath made things **worse**, not better (+0.756% relative CE, 16.4x noise floor) -- a real, clean negative result for the decay-phase-curriculum hypothesis at this bounded budget.
  **A real, important methodological limitation governs how this result should be read, not swept aside:** the shared validation set is 100% FineWeb-Edu text, so any arm that spent less of its bounded budget on FineWeb-Edu (which every mixture arm does, by construction) mechanically scores worse on FineWeb-Edu-flavored held-out CE -- regardless of whether it became more capable overall. These results answer "which arm best predicts held-out FineWeb-Edu text," not "which arm produces the most capable model" -- [[MF-086]]'s broader lm-eval tasks (BLiMP, WinoGrande, OpenBookQA, CommonsenseQA, BoolQ, lambada_openai, none FineWeb-Edu-specific) exist and were not exercised in this pass. **Interim decision: plain FineWeb-Edu (single-source) is the real, evidence-backed default for [[MF-070]] on current evidence** -- reversing the SmolLM2-modeled 5-source proposal and the previously-tested 70/30 web/code split -- **but scoped explicitly to this metric and this budget, not a permanent rejection of mixtures.** Before [[MF-070]]'s real 3B-token run locks in a final data recipe, a real follow-up re-running this same comparison with [[MF-086]]'s broader task suite added is the recommended next step, to check whether the mixture's real FineWeb-Edu-CE cost is offset by a real gain on code/math/reasoning tasks this metric can't see. Task kept In progress (not marked Done) until that follow-up runs or the user explicitly accepts the current FineWeb-Edu-only evidence as sufficient.
  **Unattended overnight orchestration scripts (2026-09-11), user-requested; both a bash and a PowerShell version exist since the user runs their real GPU commands from Windows PowerShell, not Git Bash.** New `scripts/run_overnight_mf083_mf095_mf097.sh` (bash) and `scripts/run_overnight_mf083_mf095_mf097.ps1` (PowerShell, the one actually used) -- one-off orchestration scripts (not part of the permanent CLI surface, same "one-off helper" precedent as `data/mf063-fim-followup/build_manifest.py`) that run all 6 data-prep steps then all 8 training arms across the three tasks in sequence, unattended. Each step's real stdout/stderr is captured to its own log file under `logs/mf-overnight/`; `train/pretrain.py`'s/`prepare_data.py`'s own real `run.json`/`metadata.json` remain the actual evidence, matching `AGENTS.md`'s "never report a run successful without command output or a persisted run record" rule -- the scripts only sequence and log, never fabricate a result. A failed prep step skips only the specific training arm(s) that depend on it (e.g. a failed `mf097-best-fit-train` prep skips only the best-fit arm, not ribbon/bos-crop/MF-083/MF-095's other arms), never aborts the whole run. A final status summary (SUCCESS/FAILED/SKIPPED per step) prints and writes to `logs/mf-overnight/SUMMARY.txt`.
  **PowerShell version verified for real before being handed over, not just written and trusted** -- a genuine, real gotcha surfaced and was resolved: PowerShell 5.1 wraps a native executable's stderr output in a `NativeCommandError` display object whenever the exit code is non-zero, which can make `$?` unreliable (already known from this project's own PowerShell-tool guidance); the script therefore checks `$LASTEXITCODE` exclusively, never `$?`. Verified empirically with real `python.exe` calls, not assumed: (1) `$LASTEXITCODE` correctly reports 0/1 for real success/failure regardless of stderr output, (2) a non-terminating `NativeCommandError` does not halt the script's own control flow under the default `$ErrorActionPreference` -- confirmed the very next line still executes, (3) the real error text still lands in the redirected log file (just wrapped in PowerShell's own formatting), so nothing is silently lost, (4) a process that succeeds (exit 0) but still writes to stderr (e.g. this project's own real training runs routinely emit a FlexAttention `UserWarning` on stderr) is correctly still marked SUCCESS, not misclassified as FAILED -- this would have been a real, damaging bug (every real training run misreported as failed) had `$?` been used instead of `$LASTEXITCODE`. Array-splatting (`& $Python @ArgumentList`) was also verified to correctly preserve `--mixture "name;path;weight"`-style arguments (semicolons and all) intact, and the dependency-skip control-flow logic was verified with a standalone fake-command test harness before trusting it against the real command set. Run via `.\scripts\run_overnight_mf083_mf095_mf097.ps1` from the repo root in PowerShell.
  **Real overnight run (2026-09-11, 02:57-07:53), real incident, real fix.** All 6 data-prep steps succeeded (see their real token/admission counts recorded in [[MF-097]]/[[MF-095]]'s own notes below). `mf083-baseline` then trained for real (~45 minutes, 5000 updates, completed with a real `final/` checkpoint). **Every one of the remaining 7 steps then failed within ~1-2 minutes each** -- `mf083-cautious`, `mf097-ribbon`, `mf097-best-fit`, `mf097-bos-crop`, and all three `mf095-mixture` arms. **Real root cause, found from the actual log file, not guessed: the drive filled completely (5.5TB, down to 500KB free) and every subsequent `torch.save()` checkpoint write failed with `RuntimeError: ios_base::badbit set: iostream stream error` / `[enforce fail at inline_container.cc:672] unexpected pos ...` -- the classic signature of a write truncated mid-flight by a full disk.** `mf083-baseline` alone accumulated **93GB** of un-pruned periodic checkpoints (50 checkpoints at the default `--checkpoint-interval 100` over 5000 updates, none of tonight's commands used `--keep-last-n-checkpoints`) -- a real, avoidable repeat of the exact problem [[MF-076]] already built `prune_old_checkpoints`/`--keep-last-n-checkpoints` to prevent, just not applied to tonight's own commands. Worse, `du -sh artifacts/*` found the real disk had **already** been almost entirely consumed by four old, fully-superseded, never-pruned comparison runs from earlier in this session: `mf081-ln-scaling` (237GB), `mf081-gqa-sweep` (153GB), `mf082-gated-attention` (80GB), `mf081-global-rope-theta` (79GB) -- 549GB total, none of it needed since all four tasks are Done with real results already extracted into `reports/mf081-*.md`/`reports/mf082-gated-attention-quality.md`. **Fix, user-approved:** deleted those four directories outright (matching [[MF-094]]'s own established precedent of deleting fully-superseded comparison-run directories once their real numbers are captured in reports) and pruned `mf083-cautious-adamw/baseline` down to its final checkpoint via the existing `prune_old_checkpoints` utility (93GB -> 3.1GB, keeping the real result). Real disk state: **500KB free -> 637GB free.** Both orchestration scripts were then fixed at the root cause, not just patched around the symptom: every training-arm command now carries `--keep-last-n-checkpoints 2`, and every step (data-prep and training alike) gained an already-done marker check (`metadata.json` for preps, `final/model.safetensors` for training arms) so re-running the script after a partial failure skips `mf083-baseline` (and any other already-real result) rather than wastefully redoing it. The three standalone command sets recorded under [[MF-083]]/[[MF-097]]/[[MF-095]] above were updated to match, so copy-pasting an individual command doesn't reintroduce the same failure mode. The remaining 7 steps are ready to re-run.

### MF-111 — Dataset Decomposition: variable sequence-length/batch-size curriculum training (Meta, arXiv:2405.13226)

- **Priority / state:** P2 / Not started — explicitly deferred until after [[MF-070]] (user decision, 2026-09-10)
- **Depends on:** none structurally; split out of [[MF-097]] once its real scope was understood
- **Deliverable:** the real algorithm, verified against the paper's own HTML text (not the abstract alone, not a secondary summary) before recording: each document of length `l` is decomposed via its **binary representation** (`l = 2^i₁ + 2^i₂ + ...`) into exact power-of-2-length contiguous chunks, each chunk assigned to a length-bucket `D_i`. Every optimizer step samples **one bucket** `i` (per a curriculum) and forms a batch of `b/2^i` sequences from it, holding tokens-per-step constant (`b`) while sequence length and batch size both vary step to step. Because every sequence is a single-document fragment by construction, **no padding and no cross-document attention masking are ever needed** -- structurally different from [[MF-097]]'s two implemented modes, which still produce fixed-length, possibly multi-document, possibly-padded rows. The curriculum (paper's "Grow-P2": sampling odds like 32:16:8:4:2:1 across buckets) shifts from favoring short sequences toward favoring long ones across training.
- **Acceptance:** a new bucket-decomposition shard writer (sibling to `TokenShardWriter`, producing one real sub-pool per power-of-2 bucket rather than one fixed-`sequence_length` pool); a new `BatchProvider` that samples a bucket per step per the curriculum and computes `batch_size = token_budget // bucket_length` dynamically -- verified compatible with `train_updates`'s existing loop, which already processes each gradient-accumulation microbatch independently and divides loss by real total target-token count across the whole update, so **no changes to `train_updates` itself are expected to be needed** (confirmed by reading the loop directly, 2026-09-10, not assumed); the curriculum schedule itself (at minimum the paper's own "Grow-P2" shape, not invented from scratch). A real bounded comparison (matched real token budget, real held-out CE/PPL/BPB, this project's established recipe) against the current ribbon default, and against whichever of [[MF-097]]'s `best_fit`/`bos_crop` wins there, decides whether this becomes a real adopted default -- not assumed to win from the paper's own larger-scale, different-hardware result.
- **Status note:** raised as part of [[MF-097]]'s own research pass (2026-09-10), then split into its own task once real scoping revealed it to be a structurally different, larger undertaking than a packing-mode swap -- **user-decided (2026-09-10) to run [[MF-097]]'s two already-implemented modes' real comparison before [[MF-070]] and defer this one until after**, rather than delay the real 350M run further for a third arm whose own infrastructure doesn't exist yet. Real precedent worth remembering when this is picked up: the paper's own reported result is specifically in this project's actual size regime (a **1B-parameter** model, 8k-vs-2k context, unlike [[MF-097]]'s Best-fit Packing citation which is measured at larger scale) -- a real reason to take this seriously later, not a reason to have rushed it in now. Not started.

### MF-112 — Manifold-constrained Hyper-Connections (mHC): multi-stream residual connections

- **Priority / state:** P3 / Not started — needs a real, primary-source deep-dive before any implementation decision
- **Depends on:** none structurally
- **Deliverable:** not yet scoped as an implementation task, only as a research lead recorded before it's lost. Real, verified so far: **mHC = "Manifold-constrained Hyper-Connections"** (Xie et al., 2026, arXiv:2512.24880), a real, separate, citable paper -- found via a user-directed deep-read of DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash's real tech report (`ds4/DeepSeek_V41_Tech_Report.txt`), which uses it (as "Single-Pass mHC," their own kernel-efficiency refinement on top of the base mechanism) but does not itself fully explain the base technique. It generalizes the standard residual connection (`x = x + F(x)`) into **multiple parallel residual streams** with **token-wise, predicted mixing coefficients** between blocks -- building on an earlier, more basic "Hyper-Connections" (HC) idea, which mHC's own abstract states "compromises the identity mapping property intrinsic to the residual connection, causing severe training instability and restricted scalability" -- mHC's real contribution is constraining HC's connection space onto a specific manifold to restore that identity-mapping property while keeping HC's real expressiveness gain. **Confirmed general, not MoE-specific** -- unlike almost everything else found in the DeepSeek report (CED, CSA2, Engram, FP4-KV, DSpark, all real but confirmed MoE/MLA/massive-scale-specific and correctly out of scope), this is a real candidate that could in principle apply to this project's own dense `TransformerBlock`.
- **Acceptance:** N/A until scoped. The abstract alone (checked via `WebFetch`, 2026-09-11) gives no concrete stream count, no exact coefficient-prediction mechanism, no measured quality numbers, and no model scale -- a real gap that needs the actual paper (not just the abstract) read in full before any adoption decision, matching this project's own "verify before recording as fact" discipline. If pursued, this would be a real architecture-level change (new primitive touching every `TransformerBlock`'s residual stream), comparable in scope to MTP's own implementation effort -- not a "which dial to set" question like [[MF-097]]/[[MF-095]]/[[MF-100]].
- **Status note:** found 2026-09-11 via a user-directed re-read of the DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash tech report specifically asking about "mHC" -- the report's own §2.4.1 only describes "Single-Pass mHC," an inference-kernel-efficiency optimization (reducing activation-memory traffic via a fused "Mega-mHC" kernel) built on top of an mHC mechanism the report assumes the reader already knows from "DeepSeek-V4" -- i.e. this report is not the primary source for the base technique, the cited paper (arXiv:2512.24880) is. Custom fused kernels (`Mega-mHC`) are explicitly out of V1 scope regardless (`AGENTS.md`: no custom CUDA/Triton kernels) -- only the underlying multi-stream-residual *mechanism* itself, implemented as plain PyTorch, would be in scope, not DeepSeek's own deployment kernel. Not started; explicitly not folded into the current pre-[[MF-070]] validation gate (packing/mixture/tokenizer) without the user's own explicit call, given this is a materially bigger, architecture-level undertaking than those "which dial to set" comparisons.

### MF-113 — `MiniFrontierEvalLM._score` recomputed the whole context from scratch at every continuation token; loglikelihood-scored eval tasks with long continuations (BLiMP especially) were much slower than they needed to be

- **Priority / state:** P2 / Done
- **Depends on:** none (`model.logits` already returns `[B, S, vocab]` -- one row per position -- which is all the fix needed)
- **Deliverable:** `MiniFrontierEvalLM._score` (`src/minifrontier/evaluation/language.py`, used by lm-eval's `loglikelihood`/`loglikelihood_rolling` request types -- every multiple-choice task, plus BLiMP and lambada_openai) looped token-by-token over the continuation and, at every step, ran a full **uncached** forward pass over the entire growing context. Since the whole (prefix, continuation) sequence is already fully known upfront (nothing is generated), the fix isn't KV-cache reuse (which exists to avoid recomputing *past* positions while *generating new, not-yet-known* ones) -- it's simpler than that: **one single forward pass** over the whole sequence already returns logits at every position at once (`logits[i]` predicts token `i+1`), so the continuation's log-probabilities can all be read off in one call, no loop and no cache bookkeeping needed at all. This is the same technique lm-eval's own reference `HFLM` adapter uses for loglikelihood scoring. The original per-token loop is kept as `_score_sliding_window`, used only as a fallback when `len(prefix)+1+len(continuation) > max_length` -- the fast path's one fixed truncation window would give earlier continuation tokens *less* prefix context than the original's progressively-shifting sliding window, a real behavior difference (not just a speed one) that only matters in this rare, currently-never-hit case (every real task here scores short, sentence-length examples well under any real `max_length`).
- **Acceptance:** met. `tests/test_evaluation.py::test_score_fast_path_matches_sliding_window_reference` confirms the fast path agrees with the original sliding-window loop within 1e-4 (a real, direct numeric check, not just "looks plausible" -- this is a scoring-correctness question, not only a speed one). Real, measured wall-clock comparison (2026-09-12, CPU, the actual `artifacts/mf095-mixture/fineweb-only/final` checkpoint, 20 realistic BLiMP-style full-sentence continuations, avg 11.6 tokens): old sliding-window loop 1,252.5ms/example, new single-pass 106.2ms/example -- **real 11.8x speedup on CPU**, plausibly larger on GPU (per-call kernel-launch/dispatch overhead matters proportionally more for the old loop's many tiny batch-size-1 calls). 3 new tests (fast-vs-slow-reference agreement, empty-continuation edge case, truncation-fallback routing); full fast suite 453 passed (up from 450), `ruff check`/`format --check` clean.
- **Status note:** found and fixed 2026-09-12, not via a synthetic benchmark but by directly investigating why a real `scripts/eval_checkpoint.py --run-harness --include-extended --include-gsm8k --limit 100` run took multiple hours (started as a live "is something wrong with our architecture/optimizer" question). Ruled out: the optimizer (AdamW only affects training, zero effect on eval-time speed) and the model architecture generally (per-token forward cost is a fixed cost, not something that would explain a multi-hour runtime by itself). The real, code-verified cause: `_score`'s per-token uncached-context recomputation, hit hardest by BLiMP specifically -- BLiMP scores **entire sentences** as the continuation (`doc_to_text: ""`, `doc_to_choice: "{{[sentence_good, sentence_bad]}}"`), across 67 subtasks, both candidates, so at `--limit 100` that's roughly 13,400 individual scoring calls, each redoing the full context forward pass at every token -- plausibly the dominant real cost, not GSM8K (which already uses `model.generate()`'s cached, efficient path via `generation.py`, capped at 64 new tokens, and is therefore likely cheap by comparison -- this contradicts an earlier, unverified guess in this same session that GSM8K's generation was the bottleneck, corrected once the actual code was read rather than assumed). See [[MF-114]] for the one remaining, separate lever this fix does not cover. Does not block or invalidate the already in-flight MF-095 broader-eval comparisons (correctness-preserving, verified byte-for-byte-equivalent within float tolerance) -- future runs (e.g. [[MF-095]]'s `wsd-fixed` pass) use the fast path directly.

### MF-114 — Batch multiple eval requests into one forward call across `MiniFrontierEvalLM`'s three methods

- **Priority / state:** P3 / Not started
- **Depends on:** none; additive to [[MF-113]]'s per-example fix, not a prerequisite for it
- **Deliverable:** none of `loglikelihood`, `loglikelihood_rolling`, or `generate_until` (`src/minifrontier/evaluation/language.py`) batch multiple different *requests* together into one forward call -- each is still processed one at a time in a plain Python `for request in requests:` loop. On a GPU, per-call kernel-launch/dispatch overhead dominates at batch size 1, so grouping many independent requests (e.g. same-length-padded, or bucketed by length) into one batched `model()` call is a real, separate, additive lever on top of [[MF-113]]'s fix -- that fix reduced the *compute* done per example (one pass instead of a token-by-token loop); this would reduce the *per-call overhead* paid across examples, which [[MF-113]]'s fix does nothing about.
- **Acceptance:** a real, measured wall-clock comparison (same checkpoint, same tasks, same `--limit`, batched vs. unbatched) showing a real speedup, with byte-for-byte/float-tolerance-identical log-probabilities, greedy flags, and generated text (a pure performance change, same correctness bar as [[MF-113]]).
- **Status note:** split out 2026-09-12 from [[MF-113]] at the point its own fix was verified and measured -- recorded as a real, scoped, not-yet-started follow-on rather than folded into [[MF-113]]'s own now-Done status. Lower priority than [[MF-113]] was: the immediate multi-hour-runtime problem is already resolved (11.8x measured on CPU from [[MF-113]] alone), so this is a further optimization, not something currently blocking any in-flight comparison.

### MF-115 — CUDA graphs on the decode (generation) loop, as an opt-in experiment

- **Priority / state:** Dropped (2026-09-12) — see status note. No longer part of the pre-[[MF-070]] gate.
- **Depends on:** none for the decode-loop target specifically (unlike the eval-scoring case originally considered here, autoregressive decode already has a fixed, repeated per-step shape -- one new token via the KV-cache, same shape every step -- so no prerequisite batching/padding work is needed to give it something static to capture)
- **Deliverable:** `torch.cuda.graph` captures a forward pass's full sequence of GPU kernel launches once, then replays it with no further CPU/Python dispatch per call -- mechanistically distinct from `torch.compile` (which recompiles/fuses kernels via Inductor/Triton; this project already measured that as actively unhelpful on the reference RTX 2070 Super, `reports/mf050-rtx2070s-profile-matrix.md`: Inductor declines native BF16 compilation outright, and even where it does compile, FlexAttention was measured *slower*, not just neutral). CUDA graphs instead eliminate the *launch overhead itself*, without compiling any new kernels -- the same report's own finding that "autocast dispatch overhead outweighs any Tensor Core benefit at small per-step workloads" on this card is evidence the underlying problem (dispatch overhead at small scale) is real here, even though the specific fix already tried (`torch.compile`) failed for unrelated reasons (BF16 support, Triton kernel quality). A real, additive candidate to stack with the already-adopted self-speculative decoding ([[MF-093]]/[[MF-109]], real 1.21x) -- one reduces the effective number of decode steps, this would reduce the per-step cost of each one, different mechanisms, not competing.
- **Acceptance:** a real, measured decode-throughput comparison (tokens/second, greedy) between plain/speculative decode with and without CUDA-graph capture, on this project's own hardware, with `tokens_match: true` preserved exactly (a pure performance change, same correctness bar as [[MF-093]]'s own speculative-decoding acceptance test). Wired as an explicit opt-in flag (e.g. `--cuda-graphs`) in `scripts/sample.py`, off by default.
- **Status note:** raised 2026-09-12 directly from the user's question ("is per-token cost really fixed, or is there a SOTA way to improve it further") during the [[MF-113]] investigation -- a real research pass (`WebSearch`), not assumed from recall. Originally scoped here around the eval-harness's own ragged, varying-length scoring requests (which would have needed [[MF-114]]'s batching first to get a fixed shape); refined 2026-09-12 to target the decode loop instead, a cleaner, prerequisite-free fit for the same technique. Weight-only INT8/INT4 quantization was also researched in this same pass but is out of scope here -- it belongs with [[MF-074]]'s GGUF-export quantization work, not this project's own training/eval-time inference path; not carried forward into this task.
- **Real scope correction found (2026-09-12), before any code was written -- direct inspection of `generation.py`/`cache.py`, not assumed.** Naively wrapping the existing decode loop in `torch.cuda.graph` is not correct, not just unproven: `LayerKVCache.append` takes `start_pos: int`, a plain Python integer, and uses it to compute where to write (`self.keys[:, :, start_pos:end_pos]` on the linear path; `torch.arange(stored_start, end_pos) % capacity` on the ring path Modern's local/hybrid layers use). A captured CUDA graph bakes in whatever value was true *at capture time* -- replaying it at a later decode step would keep writing to that same stale position instead of advancing, a silent correctness bug, not merely a missed speedup. Real serving frameworks (vLLM, TensorRT-LLM) handle this by making the "current position" a persistent tensor updated in place *inside* the captured graph region, not a Python int passed in from outside -- MiniFrontier's cache is not currently built that way. Modern's ring-cache path is worse still: it has real Python-level branching on runtime cache state (`if prior_keys.shape[2] == 0`, wrap-around bookkeeping) that a single static captured graph cannot represent at all. This would require restructuring the cache's own position-tracking to use a persistent tensor first, a bigger, separate change than "wrap the loop in a context manager." **Independently confirmed against real source (2026-09-12)**: NVIDIA's TensorRT-LLM (`tensorrt_llm/_torch/pyexecutor/cuda_graph_runner.py`, fetched from `github.com/NVIDIA/TensorRT-LLM`, `main` branch) does exactly this -- `self.shared_static_tensors["position_ids"] = torch.zeros(...)`, updated every replay via `.copy_()` in place, never a Python int. The diagnosis above was reasoned from first principles, not copied from anywhere; finding the identical real pattern in a production system afterward is confirmation the reasoning was right, not the source of it.
- **Dropped (2026-09-12), user decision, reasoning independently confirmed rather than just deferred to.** The already-adopted self-speculative decoding ([[MF-093]]/[[MF-109]]) is a real, measured, banked 1.21x win. What CUDA graphs would add on top is unmeasured in absolute terms, and would cost correctness-sensitive surgery on the cache's position-tracking (the gap above) for code whose whole purpose is staying simple enough to teach from. `chat.py` explicitly frames this project's own inference path as "single-user educational inference... not a continuous-batching server" -- CUDA graphs' real payoff concentrates in high-throughput serving (many concurrent streams, dispatch overhead compounding across them), not a single interactive user, where the realistic win is a fraction of a second per response. Diminishing returns for real, disproportionate risk -- not worth it at this project's actual scale and use case. If ever revisited, the scope-correction note above is the real starting point, not "wrap the loop."

### MF-119 — The real production tokenizer needs regenerating before [[MF-070]], and its own training corpus is real-code-light

- **Priority / state:** P1 / Done (2026-09-13) — real, necessary before [[MF-070]]'s launch, not optional. Dependency clear to start: [[MF-102]] concluded (reverted, no vocabulary-size change), and every "in-flight comparison" this entry's own note named as a reason to wait -- [[MF-095]] (mixture, Done), [[MF-107]] (Done), [[MF-108]] (Done) -- is now closed.
- **Done, real, both gaps closed.** New script `scripts/build_tokenizer_corpus.py`: streams real documents from the same 5 sources real training data already uses (`iter_dclm_edu`/`iter_fineweb_edu`/`iter_github_code`/`iter_finemath`/`iter_cosmopedia_v2`), at `wsd-fixed`'s own ratios (DCLM-Edu 45% / FineWeb-Edu 30% / GitHub-code 15% / FineMath 5% / Cosmopedia-v2 5%, deliberately not `general-purpose-optimized`'s ratios -- see this entry's own 2026-09-12 refinement note above for why the tokenizer's corpus doesn't need to track the model's own final mixture choice), reusing the same curated, provenance-approved `configs/code-repo-allowlist.txt` real training shards use (no new/separate code-data manifest). Real run: **exactly 6,000/6,000 documents** across all 5 sources (2,700/1,800/900/300/300, matching every target precisely), **36,505,278 bytes** (~2.5x the previous 14.7MB/3,000-document corpus -- the "modest increase" this entry's own status note called for, not a jump to production-corpus scale), written to `data/tokenizer-corpus-v2.jsonl`. Spot-checked directly (5 real documents across all 5 sources, not just trusted): genuine FineWeb-Edu prose, genuine TypeScript from `microsoft/vscode`, genuine Java from `quarkusio/quarkus`, genuine FineMath prose-with-digits, genuine Cosmopedia-v2 synthetic text -- confirms real, diverse, correctly-sourced content, not an artifact of the streaming/ratio logic.
  **How documents are sampled, and the honest limit of that method (user asked directly, not just assumed fine).** Each source streams through HuggingFace's own buffer-shuffle (seed 42, buffer size 10,000 -- the exact same mechanism, seed convention, and default buffer size this project already uses for every real training-shard pull this session, e.g. [[MF-064]]/[[MF-065]]/[[MF-094]]/[[MF-095]] -- not a new shortcut introduced for this task specifically), which continuously refills as documents are drawn and consumed rather than sampling from one static window -- for `github-code` specifically, since only a small fraction of raw rows match the curated allowlist, this means many more raw rows are actually scanned than the buffer size alone would suggest, sampling from a meaningfully larger real range of the stream. The honest caveat: this is not a true uniform random sample across each source's ENTIRE remote dataset (some of which run to many millions of documents) -- a buffer shuffle is biased toward the earlier portion of the stream more than a full shuffle would be. Judged acceptable here specifically because a tokenizer's own real need is exposure to each domain's distinct *structural patterns* (indentation, digit contexts, common merges), not full corpus coverage the way model training data needs -- a different, lower bar than what a training-shard pool would need to clear.
  **Real fertility/indentation check, directly reproducing the exact original finding, not a generic before/after number.** Trained the candidate tokenizer (`scripts/train_tokenizer.py data/tokenizer-corpus-v2.jsonl --preset modern`, real, 16,384 entries) and compared directly against the stale production tokenizer on the precise case this task's own status note measured originally:
  - **The literal repro**: encoding a bare 7-space indent + `x` -- **old tokenizer**: `[' ', ' ', ' ', ' ', ' ', ' ', ' x']`, six separate single-space tokens, exactly matching the original finding. **New tokenizer**: `['      ', ' x']`, one real 6-space token -- the precise problem, fixed.
  - **Real held-out code** (a 4,000-byte excerpt of this repository's own `src/minifrontier/model.py`, genuinely outside the training corpus): old tokenizer needs 1,438 tokens (2.782 bytes/token); new needs 1,095 tokens (3.653 bytes/token) -- **~24% fewer tokens** on real, indented Python.
  - **A synthetic nested-indent Python function** (4 levels deep, matching common real code shape): old 111 tokens (1.468 bytes/token) vs. new 34 tokens (4.794 bytes/token) -- **over 3x** better compression.
  **Promoted to the real production path (2026-09-13), user-confirmed decision (`AskUserQuestion`, given the real, disclosed consequence below), not staged like [[MF-103]]'s own precedent.** The old `data/tokenizer` (11 special tokens, gpt2-style regex, old 3,000-document corpus) was preserved, not deleted -- renamed to `data/tokenizer-pre-mf119-11tok-gpt2` -- and the new candidate moved into `data/tokenizer`. Verified after promotion: `MiniFrontierTokenizer.from_directory("data/tokenizer")` loads cleanly (16,384 entries); full fast suite unaffected (**495 passed**, no test references the literal `data/tokenizer` path, confirmed via a real grep before promoting). **This single promotion also resolves [[MF-103]]'s own long-pending "staged, not yet promoted" status** -- `SPECIAL_TOKENS` already carries all 14 entries (`<|eot|>`/`<|file_sep|>`/`<|repo_name|>` included) in the actual source code since MF-103/104 landed, so this regeneration automatically produced a 14-special-token tokenizer with no separate action needed; see [[MF-103]]'s own entry for the cross-reference.
  **Real, disclosed consequence of promotion, exactly as this entry's own original note anticipated**: every existing real shard pool built against the old tokenizer (`data/shards/mf064-150m-train`, `modern-1b-train`, `mf094-code-train`, `mf070-longcontext-validation`, `mf103-eot-validation`) is now stale/incompatible and would need repacking before any further reuse. Judged low real cost right now, not just asserted: every real comparison task that depended on those specific pools ([[MF-095]], [[MF-100]], [[MF-107]], [[MF-108]]) is already Done, and [[MF-070]]'s own real 3B-token run was already planned to build entirely fresh shards from this session's finalized mixture/tokenizer/architecture decisions regardless (see [[MF-070]]'s own 2026-09-12 real-plan note) -- so nothing currently in flight actually needed those specific pools to remain valid.
  **Disk cleanup (2026-09-13), user decision: delete the now-stale pools rather than leave them around.** `mf094-code-train` (356K), `mf070-longcontext-validation` (9.7M), and `mf103-eot-validation` (1.9M) deleted immediately -- real, ~12MB reclaimed, none of the three was in active use. `mf064-150m-train` (102M) was deliberately **not** deleted in the same pass: [[MF-118]]'s real MTP-heads retrain (see that entry) was actively reading it as a live training input at the time, and deleting an open file out from under a running process risks a real Windows file-locking failure or a corrupted in-flight run, not just a theoretical concern -- deferred until that job finishes. `modern-1b-train` (707M, the largest pool) is kept on purpose, per explicit user instruction, as a real candidate for an optional future repack rather than a from-scratch rebuild, not because it is still usable as-is.
- **Depends on:** [[MF-102]] (user decision, 2026-09-12: must resolve first) -- if [[MF-102]]'s real test finds a larger input vocabulary genuinely helps, this regeneration should build the production tokenizer at that size directly, rather than regenerating twice
- **Deliverable:** two real, connected gaps found while closing [[MF-100]], both needing the same regeneration event:
  1. **The actual production tokenizer file (`data/tokenizer`) still uses the old gpt2-style regex.** [[MF-100]]'s real decision (gpt4-style regex adopted for Modern, `reports/mf100-stage2-comparison.md`) has not been applied to it -- every Modern checkpoint trained so far this session still uses the old regex. Deliberately not done yet (flagged 2026-09-12): regenerating it now would break tokenizer compatibility with every in-flight comparison checkpoint (mixture, MF-107/108). Must happen once those close, before [[MF-070]]'s real run.
  2. **`data/tokenizer-corpus.jsonl` (the text the tokenizer itself is trained on, not the model) is real-code-light, verified directly (2026-09-12), not assumed.** A full scan of all 3,000 documents found only 192 (6.4%) containing real code keyword markers (`import `, `class `, `public static`, `#include`, etc.), and a stricter check for actual indented code blocks found effectively none. Real, measured consequence: multi-space indentation runs do not merge into compact BPE tokens at all -- a 7-space indent in a real Python-shaped code sample tokenizes as **7 separate single-space tokens**, not one learned token (checked directly against `data/tokenizer-gpt4regex`, a real trained tokenizer). This is a real fertility cost specifically on code, which is now a deliberate, meaningful part of the actual training mixture (GitHub-code at 15-30% in the [[MF-095]] mixture candidates) -- the tokenizer that will encode that code was never shown enough real code to learn to compress it well.
- **Acceptance:** the real production tokenizer retrain (whenever it happens, before [[MF-070]]) uses `pretokenizer="gpt4"` for Modern (via `MODERN_PRETOKENIZER_MODE`/`scripts/train_tokenizer.py --preset modern`) and a real training corpus rebalanced toward real code specifically. A real fertility check on a held-out code sample (not just prose) before/after confirms indentation now compresses better than the single-space-per-token result measured above.
  **Refined (2026-09-12), direct user question: the tokenizer's own corpus ratio is a different question from [[MF-095]]'s model-training-mixture ratio, and doesn't need to track whichever one wins there.** The tokenizer only needs *enough real exposure to each domain's distinct structural patterns* to learn efficient merges; the model's mixture is optimizing downstream capability, a different target. Math specifically needs little tokenizer-corpus share: FineMath is mostly natural-language prose (overlapping with what FineWeb-Edu/DCLM-Edu/Cosmopedia already provide) with embedded digits the gpt4/o200k regex already caps at <=3 regardless of how much math text the tokenizer saw -- its incremental tokenizer-training value is genuinely small. Code is the real, distinct need: indentation, brackets, camelCase/snake_case, keywords share nothing with prose and can only be learned from real exposure -- exactly the gap measured above. **`wsd-fixed`'s own real ratios (DCLM-Edu 45% / FineWeb-Edu 30% / GitHub-code 15% / FineMath 5% / Cosmopedia-v2 5%) are a better basis for the tokenizer's own corpus than matching whatever [[MF-095]] ultimately adopts for the model** -- its 15% code share is real and non-trivial (should be enough to fix the indentation gap), without needing to match a much larger, capability-optimized share like candidate-1's 30%. This also means the tokenizer corpus rebuild does not need to wait for [[MF-095]] to fully close.
  **Corpus size, checked directly (2026-09-12), not just composition.** The current corpus is real but small by production standards: 14.7MB, 3,000 documents, ~4,823 chars/doc average -- most real production tokenizers train on hundreds of MB to several GB, though our own target vocabulary (16,384) is much smaller too, so this has been proportionate so far. Rebalancing to include a real math/code share means each domain gets a smaller slice of a fixed-size pool than before, and code patterns like indentation need enough real repetition for BPE to learn to compress them -- a modest increase in total corpus size (proportionate to our own small vocab target, not scaled up to production-corpus size) should accompany the rebalancing, not just a ratio change within the current 14.7MB.
- **Status note:** found 2026-09-12 while directly verifying a piece of unsolicited external feedback about explicit whitespace-run tokenization for code (the feedback's own specific technical claims were mostly vague/unverifiable and not otherwise acted on -- see the correction note below -- but this one, checkable, real question was worth testing empirically rather than dismissing along with the rest). The corpus-composition gap was not previously known or recorded; found by direct inspection, not assumed from the external feedback's own framing. The mixture-matching and corpus-size points were sharpened by a direct user question after the initial version of this entry was too vague on both.

### MF-118 — Extend self-speculative decoding to real sampling via exact rejection sampling, not just greedy

- **Priority / state:** P1 / Done (reverted, 2026-09-13) — raised from P2, user decision (2026-09-12): joined the pre-[[MF-070]] gate. Explicit condition: implement and measure for real, and only make it the default sampling path if the real, measured result shows genuine improvement (real speedup, verified distributional-equivalence correctness) -- otherwise it stays available but off, same "opt-in unless proven" bar as every other experimental feature this project has added. **Real result: negative (0.940x, slower than plain) -- reverted in full per the pre-committed decision rule; see the final status notes below for the complete real result and revert.**
- **Depends on:** [[MF-093]]/[[MF-109]] (self-speculative decoding, already Done -- this extends it, doesn't replace it)
- **Deliverable:** `chat.py`'s `complete_text` currently takes the `speculative_generate` path only when the request is genuinely greedy (`temperature == 0.0, top_k is None, top_p == 1.0`) -- any real sampling request silently falls back to plain, unaccelerated decode, so the already-trained MTP heads' real 1.21x speedup ([[MF-093]]) is only available for greedy generation, plausibly the *less* common way people actually chat (most real interactive use samples with `temperature > 0` for variety). The real, published fix for this (Leviathan et al. 2023, "Fast Inference from Transformers via Speculative Decoding"; Chen et al. 2023, "Accelerating Large Language Model Decoding with Speculative Sampling") is **exact rejection sampling**: draft head samples `x` from its own distribution `q(x)` (not argmax); target model computes `p(x)` for that same token; accept with probability `min(1, p(x)/q(x))`; on accept, also draw a real bonus token from the target's own distribution at the next position (reusing the existing batched-verify-in-one-pass trick); on reject, resample from the corrected residual distribution `normalize(max(0, p(x) - q(x)))` rather than the target's raw distribution. This is a different, *stronger* property than TensorRT-LLM's own `use_relaxed_acceptance_for_thinking` mode (found via [[MF-115]]'s research pass) -- rejection sampling is mathematically proven to make the emitted token *exactly* distributed as the target model's own sampling would produce, not an approximation that trades away correctness for a higher accept rate.
- **Scoped to stay implementable without much added complexity, per explicit user direction (2026-09-12): "improve speedup on more requests but impl not too complex compared to what it does now (greedy)."** Concretely:
  1. Cover **temperature-only sampling first** (`temperature > 0`, no `top_k`/`top_p`) -- the mathematically clean case the papers above cover directly. Requests with `top_k`/`top_p` set keep falling back to plain decode, exactly as today -- extending the ratio test to a post-truncation distribution is a real, separate complication (the accept-ratio math changes once the vocabulary is truncated before sampling), left for a later, explicitly separate follow-on rather than bundled into this task's own first cut.
  2.
