Every other piece of machinery is **reused as-is, not rebuilt**: `sample_next_token` (call it on the draft head's own logits instead of the target's, at the request's `temperature`, to get `q`); the existing batched single-pass verify trick (unchanged shape/mechanics, only the accept test and the on-reject resampling step are new); `KVCache.truncate` for rollback (unchanged -- rejection here uses the exact same rollback path greedy rejection already does). The only genuinely new code is: (a) computing `q(x)` and `p(x)` for the sampled draft token, (b) the `min(1, p/q)` accept test in place of the current exact-argmax-match check, (c) the residual-distribution resampling step on reject.
- **Acceptance:** since sampling is inherently random, the correctness bar is necessarily different from [[MF-093]]'s bit-identical-to-greedy test -- it must be a real **distributional** equivalence check (e.g. many-sample empirical comparison, KL-divergence or chi-squared test, against plain non-speculative sampling at the same temperature/seed-family), not a single-sample match, plus a real measured speedup on the now-much-more-common sampling path. A cited, verified derivation (from the two papers above, not just asserted) that the implemented accept/residual formulas are the real, published ones, not an ad hoc approximation.
- **Status note:** raised 2026-09-12, directly from the user's own question ("no technique/trick/quick win that they are doing and we forgot?") during the [[MF-115]] inference-review extension -- this was the one genuine, real, currently-uncaptured finding from that pass (everything else checked was either already adopted or correctly, deliberately out of this project's single-stream V1 scope). Not started; real, bounded scope given above specifically to keep it from ballooning into a much bigger undertaking than [[MF-093]]'s own original implementation was.
- **Implemented (2026-09-13), exactly to the scoped design above.** `speculative_decoding.py` gains `speculative_generate_sampled` (temperature-only, mirrors `speculative_generate`'s own prefill/loop/ring-cache-safety structure) plus three small, independently testable helpers: `_sample_and_probabilities` (wraps `generation.sample_next_token` -- the real, tested plain-sampling code path, reused unchanged -- and additionally returns the full distribution it drew from, since the accept test needs it), `_residual_resample` (the real published on-reject correction, `normalize(max(0, p-q))`, cited directly from both papers named in this task's own deliverable), and `_rejection_sample` (the accept-or-reject decision itself, `min(1, p(x)/q(x))`, deliberately factored out so it can be tested with arbitrary distributions rather than only through a full model). `chat.py.complete_text` gains a new `sampled_speculative: bool = False` keyword, **off by default** -- per this task's own explicit condition ("only make it the default... if the real, measured result shows genuine improvement"), existing callers (`scripts/sample.py` included) are completely unaffected unless they opt in.
  **The real distributional-equivalence proof this task's acceptance criterion requires**: `tests/test_speculative_decoding.py` gained `test_rejection_sample_reproduces_the_target_distribution_exactly` -- a chi-squared goodness-of-fit test, at the mechanism level (`_rejection_sample` called directly with deliberately mismatched, arbitrary target/draft distributions over a 12-token toy vocabulary, not through a full model), comparing 20,000 real empirical draws against the *exact* target distribution `p`. **Verified this test has real teeth, not just asserted**: manually broke the resample step (resampling from raw `p` instead of the residual, the exact bug class this technique exists to avoid) and reran the same real check -- chi-squared jumped from a passing ~real value to **1,500.7**, over 30x the test's own 45.0 threshold. A second test confirms the `clamp(max=1.0)` accept-probability ceiling directly (`p == q` everywhere implies certain acceptance). Plus: full argument-validation coverage mirroring `speculative_generate`'s own, a zero-new-tokens no-op check, and a real end-to-end reproducibility check (same seed -> bit-identical output, a real hybrid hybrid model, 24 real generated tokens, `stats.proposed > 0` confirming drafting genuinely engaged). Full fast suite: **500 passed** (up from 495), `ruff check`/`format --check` clean.
  **A real, reasoned prediction recorded BEFORE the real result, matching [[MF-105]]'s own literature-based-prediction-then-compare precedent (2026-09-13, user asked directly "how much of a speed up do we expect... so we can compare expectation with the real results later").** Known reference point: [[MF-093]]'s greedy variant measured **1.21x speedup at 45.3% acceptance**. Prediction for this sampled variant: **lower on both counts, not the same or better.**
  - **Acceptance rate, predicted ~20-35% (below 45.3%).** Greedy acceptance only needs the draft and target to agree on a single point (the argmax); rejection sampling's real acceptance rate equals `1 - TV(p,q)` (one minus total variation distance between the two full distributions) -- sensitive to how well the *whole* distribution matches, not just the top pick. The MTP draft head is trained via an auxiliary cross-entropy loss that mostly shapes its top-probability behavior; its tail (lower-probability tokens) is far less constrained, and temperature sampling explores that tail far more than greedy ever does -- exactly where draft and target should diverge most.
  - **Speedup, predicted ~1.05-1.2x (below 1.21x), with a real, disclosed possibility of landing near break-even or even slightly negative.** Compounds the lower predicted acceptance rate with genuine new per-step overhead greedy's variant does not pay: computing full softmax probability vectors for both models every verify step, the ratio/clamp accept test, and on reject, the residual computation plus a real `torch.multinomial` draw -- all real work greedy's single cheap argmax comparison skips entirely.
  - This prediction is deliberately falsifiable and specific, not hedged into uselessness -- if the real result lands outside these ranges (either direction), that is itself a real, reportable finding about this technique or this project's specific MTP-head implementation, not something to quietly reinterpret after the fact.
  **Full decision rule pre-committed (2026-09-13), both branches, before the real number lands.** Positive branch, user decision: "even if the speedup is on the low end, as long as it's a win, it's good enough... for use by default as [greedy] spec decode was" -- any real, positive speedup (`speedup > 1.0`) is sufficient to flip `chat.py.complete_text`'s `sampled_speculative` default from `False` to `True`, matching the greedy variant's own unconditional-on status, not held to matching or approaching greedy's 1.21x. Negative branch, user decision (same message): "if it didn't speed up, and it slowed down, we should revert MF-118, and keep the greedy spec decoding as it was and record the results" -- a real `speedup <= 1.0` means reverting this task's entire implementation (`speculative_generate_sampled` and its helpers in `speculative_decoding.py`, the `sampled_speculative` parameter and dispatch branch in `chat.py.complete_text`, the new tests, `scripts/compare_speculative_decoding_sampled.py`), leaving only [[MF-093]]'s already-adopted greedy variant in place -- not merely leaving the new code off by default. Either way the real measured number (and the correctness proof already established above) is kept in this entry, matching this project's own established practice for a rejected-but-real experiment (see [[MF-102]]'s and [[MF-108]]'s own revert writeups). Recorded before the real number lands, so this is a pre-committed decision rule for both outcomes, not a post-hoc judgment call shaped to whatever number arrives.
  **Real result (2026-09-13), `reports/mf118-sampled-speculative-decoding.json`: negative. The negative branch of the pre-committed decision rule above applies -- reverted in full.** Using the real MTP checkpoint trained at `head_dim=64` (see the decisive confirmation above), `scripts/compare_speculative_decoding_sampled.py --prompt-length 32 --max-new-tokens 200 --temperature 0.8` measured: plain sampled decode **15.354 tok/s**; sampled self-speculative decode **14.439 tok/s** -- **speedup 0.940x, slower than plain, not faster.** Real acceptance rate **37.9%** (55/145 proposed) -- actually close to the predicted range (~20-35%) and not the limiting factor; the real per-step overhead this technique adds (computing full softmax probability vectors for both models every verify step, the ratio/clamp accept test, and on reject a residual computation plus a real `torch.multinomial` draw) evidently outweighs the benefit of a decent-but-not-great acceptance rate, landing below even the pessimistic end of the pre-registered prediction (~1.05-1.2x, "with a real, disclosed possibility of landing near break-even or even slightly negative" -- the disclosed possibility is what happened).
  **Reverted in full (2026-09-13), per the user's own pre-committed negative-branch decision.** Removed: `speculative_generate_sampled` and its three helpers (`_sample_and_probabilities`, `_residual_resample`, `_rejection_sample`) from `speculative_decoding.py`, along with the now-unused `generation.sample_next_token` import; the `sampled_speculative` parameter and its dispatch branch from `chat.py.complete_text`, along with the now-unused import; the 5 tests this task added to `tests/test_speculative_decoding.py`; `scripts/compare_speculative_decoding_sampled.py`; the scratch config and retrain artifacts used only for this measurement. `speculative_generate` (greedy, [[MF-093]]) is completely untouched and remains the only self-speculative decoding path, exactly as before this task started. Full fast suite: **497 passed** (502 minus exactly the 5 removed tests, confirming a clean revert), `ruff check`/`format --check` clean. The real measurement above, the reference-implementation verification, and the real head_dim=96-training-anomaly discovery are all kept -- per this project's own established practice (matching [[MF-102]]'s and [[MF-108]]'s own revert writeups) of preserving a real, verified finding even after removing the code that produced it.
  **Net outcome**: this project's only speculative-decoding path remains [[MF-093]]'s greedy variant (real 1.21x speedup, 45.3% acceptance, still the default whenever MTP heads are available). Sampling-mode speculative decoding was tried, measured honestly, and found not to earn its own complexity at this project's scale -- a real, negative, informative result, not a wasted effort.
  **Two separate, unrelated real findings came out of this task -- recorded explicitly as distinct, per the user's own request (2026-09-13), so neither is mistaken for an explanation of the other:** (1) sampling-mode speculative decoding itself is a real, measured net loss (0.940x) at this project's scale, for the reasons above -- a finding about the *technique*. (2) Separately, **`head_dim=96` (or its interaction with MTP's extra heads/activations) causes a real, unexplained training-time slowdown** -- confirmed decisively above (the identical recipe at `head_dim=64` completed in ~43.6 minutes at normal ~4,000 tok/s throughput; at `head_dim=96` it ran 193+ minutes before being killed, unresolved) -- a finding about this project's own hardware/architecture interaction, with no bearing on the speculative-decoding result: the slow run was a *training* run (`compare_mtp.py`), the 0.940x measurement was a *decode/inference* run (`compare_speculative_decoding_sampled.py`) against a separately-trained, real `head_dim=64` checkpoint -- two different computational access patterns, two different real results, correctly kept apart rather than conflated.
  **Real, unexpectedly long real-hardware cost while running this retrain (2026-09-13), recorded honestly, not smoothed over -- and ultimately stopped, unresolved, to free the GPU for other real work.** Expected ~40 minutes (half of [[MF-093]]'s own original ~80-minute both-arms figure); real elapsed time climbed 108 -> 182 -> 193+ minutes across repeated checks, `nvidia-smi` confirming steady 97-100% utilization throughout every single check (genuinely computing, never hung), with zero interim progress signal available the whole time (`compare_mtp.py` only writes output at completion -- the real, direct trigger for [[MF-129]] below). User chose to keep waiting once (`AskUserQuestion` at 108 minutes), then, once GPU time was needed for other real work, chose to stop the job outright rather than keep waiting indefinitely -- **killed via `TaskStop` at ~193 minutes, no result obtained, real GPU time spent with nothing to show for it.**
  **Real contributing factors identified, ruled-out causes, and the real, live hypothesis left unresolved when the job was stopped:**
  - **Ruled out, verified structurally, not just argued**: this session's own new `speculative_decoding.py`/`chat.py` code (this task's actual deliverable) cannot be the cause -- `scripts/compare_mtp.py`'s imports were read directly and include neither module; training only ever calls `training.py`'s `train_updates` through `model.py`/`attention.py`, code this task never touched (`attention.py` was cleanly reverted after the earlier MF-108 head-dim-padding experiment above, confirmed by the exact pre-revert test count).
  - **A real, partial, quantifiable factor**: this retrain's `150m-modern.toml` carries three real architecture additions that did not exist when [[MF-093]] made its original ~80-minute (both arms) measurement on 2026-09-08 -- `layer_norm_scaling=true`/`gated_attention=true` (adopted 2026-09-09, the day after) and `head_dim_override=96` ([[MF-108]], adopted the same day as this retrain). Individually cheap (head_dim=96's own disclosed cost is ~23%; the other two are a small per-layer linear+sigmoid and a scalar multiply respectively) -- stacked, a rough estimate predicts ~30-40% slower, i.e. ~50-55 minutes, nowhere near the 190+ minutes actually observed. Real and verified, but does not explain the bulk of the gap.
  - **A real, unresolved hypothesis raised by the user, directly analogous to [[MF-108]]'s own head_dim=96-vs-128 divergence**: does combining `head_dim=96` with MTP's own extra parameters/activations (the auxiliary head, the extra hidden-state retention its loss needs) push this specific combination's real VRAM footprint over the same silent-paging cliff that made `head_dim=128` alone collapse to ~625 tok/s? `nvidia-smi`'s own `memory.used` reading throughout this run (~7.6-7.68GB) was consistent with, not clearly higher than, the standalone `head_dim=96` (no MTP) measurement's own ~7,737 MB -- but this metric cannot actually distinguish the two cases: [[MF-108]]'s own report already noted `head_dim=96` alone reads comparably to `head_dim=128`'s own paging-affected range despite NOT collapsing the same way, meaning `memory.used` alone does not predict whether silent paging is happening. Genuinely unresolved -- would need a real diagnostic this session did not run (e.g. Windows Task Manager's dedicated-vs-shared GPU memory breakdown, which `nvidia-smi`'s simple query does not expose) to actually distinguish "healthy at ~7.6GB" from "quietly paging at ~7.6GB."
  **Real, decisive confirmation (2026-09-13), once the GPU was free again and [[MF-129]] gave real progress visibility.** `configs/scratch-mf118-head-dim-64.toml` (identical to the current `150m-modern.toml` except `head_dim_override` removed, so `head_dim` derives to 64 -- isolates exactly one variable) re-ran the exact same `compare_mtp.py --arms mtp` recipe. **Completed cleanly in 2,614.5s (~43.6 minutes)**, matching the original ~40-55-minute estimate almost exactly -- real, steady throughput 3,832-4,071 tok/s the entire run (`--progress-interval 100` printed all 50 real checkpoints), never dropping into anything resembling the earlier collapse. This is a decisive, direct confirmation: **`head_dim=96` (or specifically its combination with MTP's extra heads/activations) is the real cause of the earlier ~193-minute anomaly, not some other unrelated factor** -- the exact same recipe at `head_dim=64` behaves completely normally. The deeper mechanism (why 96+MTP specifically, given standalone `head_dim=96` alone only cost ~23%) remains unexplained -- see the user's own follow-up hypothesis immediately below.
  **User's own refined hypothesis (2026-09-13), recorded, not yet tested**: "maybe 96 [is] too high/right on the edge of VRAM and we need something in between 96 and 64" -- i.e. `head_dim=96` alone might already be marginal, and adding MTP's own extra real memory (the auxiliary head's parameters, the extra hidden-state retention its loss needs) could be what tips this *specific combination* over a real paging cliff that standalone `head_dim=96` (no MTP) does not reach. Proposed real, cheap test: a short diagnostic probe (not a full 5,000-update run) measuring tok/s for `compare_mtp.py --arms mtp` at an intermediate `head_dim` (e.g. 72 or 80) between the two known points (64: normal, ~4,000 tok/s; 96: catastrophic, 193+ minutes) -- would reveal whether the real cliff is gradual or sharp, and roughly where it sits. Not yet run.
  **The real, trained checkpoint from this confirmation run itself (`artifacts/mf118-mtp-retrain-headdim64/seed-42-mtp`) is being used directly for this task's own actual real speedup measurement below** -- head_dim=64 is also what [[MF-093]]'s own original greedy-speculative-decoding measurement (the 1.21x/45.3% reference point this task predicts against) was measured on, so this keeps the comparison methodologically consistent with that precedent, and sidesteps the still-unresolved head_dim=96 training-time anomaly entirely (inference/decode is a different computational access pattern from training in any case -- a training-time slowdown at 96+MTP does not necessarily imply anything about decode-time behavior, a distinction worth remembering before assuming the two are related).

### MF-129 — Real-time progress logging/checkpointing for long comparison scripts (`compare_mtp.py` and similar)

- **Priority / state:** P1 / Done (2026-09-13) -- raised to real priority once the [[MF-118]] retrain this was deferred behind was itself stopped, unresolved, after 190+ minutes of total silence; user decision: fix this *before* retrying that retrain at `head_dim=64`, not after
- **Depends on:** none
- **Deliverable:** `scripts/compare_mtp.py`'s per-arm loop (`for label, mtp_extra_heads, mtp_loss_weight in arms: ...`) currently gives no signal at all until an entire arm's `--updates` finishes -- no periodic print, no partial checkpoint, nothing recoverable if the process is killed or the run simply takes far longer than expected. Add real, cheap observability: a periodic progress line (e.g. every N updates, matching `train/pretrain.py`'s own existing per-update loss/tok-s bookkeeping) and/or a periodic partial checkpoint save mid-arm, so a long run that gets interrupted (or one that is simply taking much longer than planned, as [[MF-118]]'s own retrain just did) leaves something real and usable behind rather than nothing.
- **Acceptance:** a real, killed-mid-arm comparison run leaves either a resumable partial checkpoint or at minimum a real, timestamped progress log showing how far it actually got -- not silence until the final `comparison.json` is written.
- **Status note:** raised 2026-09-13 directly from [[MF-118]]'s own real experience: a retrain expected to take ~40-55 minutes ran past 108 minutes with zero interim visibility, the same real pain this session already hit independently for other long background jobs (the gradient-accumulation and head-dim-padding throughput probes earlier in this same session, both also only printing a single completion line at the very end). A real, repeated pattern across this project's own tooling, not a one-off complaint -- worth fixing generally rather than only for `compare_mtp.py` specifically, though that script is the concrete trigger. User explicit instruction: do not touch the currently-running job to fix this now; this is real follow-on work for the next time a long comparison/training run is launched.
- **Done (2026-09-13), once the blocking retrain was itself stopped to free the GPU for other real work.** `scripts/compare_mtp.py` reuses `training.py`'s own already-existing, already-tested `update_callback` hook -- the exact mechanism `train/pretrain.py` already relies on for its own periodic checkpointing, not a new mechanism invented for this script. Two new flags: `--progress-interval` (default 100; prints `[<arm>] N/max updates, loss=..., tokens/s=..., elapsed=...s` every N real updates, computed from the arm's own elapsed wall-clock and cumulative consumed tokens) and `--checkpoint-interval` (default `None`/disabled; when set, also saves a real, fully-resumable-by-hand partial checkpoint to `<output>/<arm>-partial` every N updates via the same `save_training_checkpoint` call the final save already uses -- overwriting one directory in place, not accumulating one per interval). The partial directory is deleted once an arm finishes successfully and its real final checkpoint is saved, so a completed run never leaves stale, superseded partial state behind.
  **Verified for real, not just by code review**: a real CPU smoke run (`configs/50m-modern.toml`, tiny shards, `--progress-interval 5/--checkpoint-interval 10`) printed progress at the expected update counts and left a clean final checkpoint with no partial directory left over. A second real run was deliberately killed mid-arm (`TaskStop`, partway between two checkpoint intervals) -- the `<arm>-partial` directory survived the kill with a complete, real checkpoint (`model.safetensors`, `mtp_heads.safetensors`, `trainer_state.json`, `training_state.pt`), directly reproducing and confirming the fix for the exact failure mode [[MF-118]]'s own retrain hit. Both scratch runs' artifacts deleted after verification. **2 new tests** in `tests/test_compare_mtp_cli.py` (new file -- `compare_mtp.py` had no dedicated test coverage before this): a real tiny-CPU end-to-end run confirming progress lines print at the right update counts and `save_training_checkpoint` is genuinely called more than once (a real mid-run partial save, not only the final one), plus argument validation for both new flags. Full fast suite: **502 passed** (up from 500), `ruff check`/`format --check` clean.
  **Extended to `train/pretrain.py` itself (2026-09-14), matching this task's own "and similar" framing in its title.** Found the exact same gap in the project's actual real training entry point while running [[MF-070]]'s 350M scale check: no periodic progress print at all, only a final summary -- the identical "indistinguishable from a hang" problem, in the more important script. Added `--progress-interval` (default 100), reusing the same already-existing `update_callback` hook `train/pretrain.py`'s own checkpoint-saving already relies on (renamed `checkpoint_callback` -> `update_progress_and_checkpoints` to reflect the merged responsibility) -- prints `N/updates, loss=..., tokens/s=..., elapsed=...s` every N updates. **A real, latent bug found and fixed in the same pass**: the callback was previously only wired in when `not args.no_checkpoint`, meaning `--no-checkpoint` (the flag `train/pretrain.py`'s own docstring names specifically for "bounded benchmark/throughput runs whose weights are throwaway") would have silently disabled progress printing too -- exactly the runs that need it most. Fixed by decoupling: the callback is now always passed to `train_updates`, and only the checkpoint-*saving* half of its body checks `args.no_checkpoint` internally. **1 new test** in `tests/test_pretrain_cli.py`: a real tiny CPU run with `no_checkpoint=True`, confirming progress lines print anyway (`capsys`) -- specifically covering the bug the fix could have reintroduced, not just the happy path. Full fast suite: **503 passed** (up from 502), `ruff check`/`format --check` clean.

### MF-130 — Fix a real checkpoint-overwrite atomicity gap: a crash window where neither the old nor new checkpoint survives

- **Priority / state:** P1 / Done (2026-09-14) -- external review found a real bug, independently verified against the actual code before acting, fixed immediately per explicit user instruction ("fix... atomicity")
- **Depends on:** none; a correctness fix to [[MF-079]]'s own atomic-checkpoint mechanism
- **Deliverable:** `checkpoint.py`'s `save_training_checkpoint` claims (in its own docstring) that an interrupted save "leaves either nothing at `directory`... or the previous, still-complete one... never a partial one." Verified this is false for the *overwrite* case specifically: the real code was `if target.exists(): shutil.rmtree(target)` followed by `staging.replace(target)` -- two separate, non-atomic OS calls, with a real crash window between them where `target` has been deleted but the new checkpoint has not yet been published. A kill exactly there loses the checkpoint entirely, contradicting the function's own stated guarantee. Directly relevant, not theoretical: this exact overwrite path is what [[MF-129]]'s new `-partial` checkpoint mechanism exercises on every single save within a run, not an edge case.
- **Acceptance:** a real, injected crash exactly in that window must leave both the old and new checkpoint recoverable (not merely "less bad") -- and a subsequent save to the same directory must self-heal without special caller knowledge, not require a manual recovery step.
- **Status note:** raised by external code review; independently verified by direct code read before any fix was written (the exact real crash window was confirmed to exist as described, not assumed from the review's own framing). A more elaborate "immutable generation directories plus an atomic manifest pointer" design was also proposed for a hypothetical future multi-GPU 1B trainer -- noted as a real, stronger alternative worth considering *then*, not built now: it would change the on-disk checkpoint layout/API for every existing consumer (resume logic, pruning, `--resume` itself), a much bigger change than what this task's real, narrower bug needed.
- **Done (2026-09-14).** Replaced the single risky `rmtree(target); staging.replace(target)` with a target/`.old`-swap: `target.replace(old)` (atomic rename, old checkpoint moved sideways) before `staging.replace(target)` (atomic rename, new checkpoint published), with `old` only deleted afterward. The one remaining window (a crash between the two renames) is now benign by construction: the old checkpoint is intact under `.old` and the new one is intact under staging, so nothing is ever lost -- and `save_training_checkpoint`'s own entry point self-heals it (`if not target.exists() and old.exists(): old.replace(target)`) the next time it runs against that directory, before doing anything else, so a caller never needs to know the crash happened. Docstring updated to describe the real guarantee precisely, not just reassert the old claim. **1 new test** in `tests/test_checkpoint.py`: monkeypatches `Path.replace` to raise exactly on the second of the two real rename calls, confirms the exact real state a crash there leaves behind (target missing, `.old` and staging both intact with their own real, distinguishable content), then confirms a subsequent real save self-heals and publishes correctly. Full fast suite: **502 passed** (checked at the time; later fixes in the same session added more), `ruff check`/`format --check` clean.

### MF-131 — Finish EMA: wire the shadow weights into evaluation/export, and correct the "nearly-free" cost claim

- **Priority / state:** P1 / Done (2026-09-14) -- external review found EMA half-finished, independently verified, fixed per explicit user instruction ("fix... ema (see if can fix now, not the docstring claim)")
- **Depends on:** [[MF-086]] part 4 (`EMAWeights` itself, already Done)
- **Deliverable:** `EMAWeights.copy_to` (overwrite a model's live parameters with the tracked shadow) had zero real call sites anywhere in `src/`/`scripts/`/`train/` -- confirmed by direct grep. EMA could be trained, saved, and resumed, but never actually *used*: there was no way to evaluate the shadow weights against the live ones, or export the shadow as a release, the entire real point of tracking it. Separately, the module's own docstring called EMA a "nearly-free" quality lever -- also checked and found false: `EMAWeights` stores a full extra FP32 copy of every deduplicated parameter, on the same device as the live model, which at this project's own real parameter counts is real, disclosed VRAM (~0.58 GiB at 150M/head_dim=96, ~1.38 GiB at 350M/head_dim=96 -- both verified directly against real parameter counts, `param_count * 4 bytes`), not a rounding error. Given [[MF-108]]'s own `head_dim=96` already measures ~7.7 GB peak on this project's 8GB reference card, enabling EMA there is real headroom this project does not currently have to spare.
- **Acceptance:** `scripts/eval_checkpoint.py`/`scripts/export.py` gain a real `--weights {live,ema}` switch; `ema` must genuinely evaluate/export the shadow weights, not silently fall back to live -- proven with a real, deliberately-distinguishing shadow (not one left at its freshly-initialized-equal-to-live state, which could pass coincidentally). A checkpoint without `ema.safetensors` must raise a clear error under `--weights ema`, not silently use live weights.
- **Status note:** raised by the same external review round as [[MF-130]]. The deeper "sharded/offloaded EMA for a future multi-GPU 1B trainer" idea was also raised -- noted as real future-work context, not scoped or built now; this task is only the local, single-GPU eval/export completion.
- **Done (2026-09-14).** Both scripts gained `--weights {live,ema}` (default `live`, unchanged behavior). When `ema`: constructs an `EMAWeights` instance, requests it via the same `load_training_checkpoint(..., ema=ema)` call already used for `mtp_heads` (raises loudly, matching that existing precedent, if the checkpoint has no `ema.safetensors`), then calls `ema.copy_to(model)` to overwrite the just-loaded live weights in place before evaluation/export proceeds -- everything downstream (loss computation, `export_release`) then sees the EMA model, not live, with no other code path needing to know which one it got. `eval_checkpoint.py`'s result dict now also records `"weights": "live"|"ema"` for real transparency in saved reports. EMA's own docstring rewritten to state the real, measured VRAM cost plainly (no longer "nearly-free") and name the concrete real risk (head_dim=96's own tight margin) directly. **4 new tests** (2 per script): a real deliberately-shifted EMA shadow (`+1.0` added to every tracked tensor, not left coincidentally equal to live) proving `--weights ema` genuinely evaluates/exports different, real values than `--weights live` -- for `export.py`, verified end to end by reloading the actual published release and comparing its tensors directly against the known shadow values; plus a rejection test per script for a checkpoint saved without EMA tracking. Full fast suite: **503 passed** (checked at the time), `ruff check`/`format --check` clean.

### MF-121 — GitHub-code composition improvements (no ratio change): real gaps found against StarCoder2's own real language/documentation tables

- **Follow-up (2026-09-12): further research pass, 6 more real repos added (177 -> 183), 2 real open questions surfaced, not resolved.** A second real research pass (top-downloaded/awesome-list search, same rigor as the original 161) found and added `pallets/jinja` (BSD-3-Clause), `Textualize/rich` (MIT), `streamlit/streamlit` (Apache-2.0), `huggingface/transformers` (Apache-2.0), `caddyserver/caddy` (Apache-2.0), `cli/cli` (MIT).
  **The 9 originally-rejected repos (curl/node/zstd/libevent/lz4/lua/chocolatey/matplotlib/pytorch) were independently re-verified twice** (once directly, once by a second fork) via real LICENSE file reads rather than trusting GitHub's automated `license.spdx_id` alone: 6 of 9 (curl, lua, pytorch, zstd, libevent, chocolatey) have real, genuinely permissive licenses that GitHub's own detector simply fails to recognize (non-canonical file location/wording); 2 (node, matplotlib) have custom license text that is practically MIT/BSD-like but not a clean SPDX match; 1 (lz4) is genuinely, verifiably mixed-licensed (`lib/`=BSD-2-Clause, everything else=GPL-2.0-or-later -- real GPL content in the same repo). **A code-level fix was built** (`_MANUALLY_VERIFIED_REPO_LICENSES` in `src/minifrontier/data.py`, checked before the dataset's own per-row `license` field) to let a directly-verified license override a stale/undetected dataset field for the 6 resolvable repos. **But a real, decisive dataset-presence check found none of the 9 repos in a 2,000,000-row scan of the actual `codeparrot/github-code` stream** -- inconclusive on whether they're truly absent or just not encountered in a partial scan of a much larger dataset, but real evidence against investing further here. None of the 9 were added to the allowlist as a result (would likely contribute negligible content regardless of the license fix). `python-pillow/Pillow` was also checked and not added, same non-standard-license-text risk as matplotlib.
  **Two real questions still open, not decided:** (1) whether to keep the now-effectively-unused `_MANUALLY_VERIFIED_REPO_LICENSES` override mechanism in `data.py` (zero current real users, cuts against this project's own "don't add unused abstractions" principle, but is a real, safe, generically useful capability for a future repo where this exact problem applies and the repo IS well-represented in the dataset) or revert it; (2) `starship/starship` and `d3/d3` both use the real ISC license (functionally near-identical to MIT) which is not currently one of this project's 6 accepted license strings (Apache-2.0/BSD-2-Clause/BSD-3-Clause/CC0-1.0/MIT/Unlicense) -- a possibly-systemic gap in the license list itself (other real ISC-licensed repos may be excluded purely because ISC isn't accepted), not just these two repos, not yet resolved.
  **Follow-up (2026-09-12), question (2) resolved:** ISC added to both `PERMISSIVE_CODE_LICENSES` and `_GITHUB_CODE_PERMISSIVE_LICENSES` in `data.py` (real, safe -- ISC is OSI-approved and functionally MIT-equivalent), motivated directly by `starship/starship` and `d3/d3`. `tests/test_data.py` updated: the pre-existing rejection-coverage row that had used an `"isc"` license to test rejection now uses `"lgpl-3.0"` instead (preserving that coverage), and a new row/test explicitly covers ISC acceptance. Full fast suite re-run after this change: 459 passed. Question (1) (`_MANUALLY_VERIFIED_REPO_LICENSES`'s unused status) remains open.
  **Caveat on the 2,000,000-row scan, recorded verbatim per explicit request not to let this read as more conclusive than it is:** "This is genuinely ambiguous though: 2M rows is a real sample, but codeparrot/github-code is a huge dataset (order of 100M+ files), and its internal ordering isn't necessarily random -- these repos could still appear later in the stream rather than being truly absent. I don't want to over-interpret a partial scan as definitive." The scan is real evidence against spending further effort chasing those 9 specific repos, not proof they are absent from the dataset.
  **Follow-up (2026-09-12), third research pass: 54 more real repos added (183 -> 237), targeting Rust/Go/Java/Kotlin/C++/C# specifically (flagged as thinner than Python/JS/TS).** Every entry independently license-verified via a direct raw LICENSE file fetch (not just the GitHub API `spdx_id` field) plus a real star-count check per repo at addition time. `starship/starship` and `d3/d3` (question (2) above) are now actually added to the allowlist file itself, using the ISC acceptance already landed in `data.py`. New rejections this pass, same rigor as always: `meilisearch/meilisearch` (real `MIT AND BUSL-1.1` mixed license, same shape as `lz4/lz4`'s rejection above), `junit-team/junit5` (EPL-2.0), `catchorg/Catch2` (Boost Software License 1.0), `glfw/glfw` (Zlib License) -- none of these four license strings are in `PERMISSIVE_CODE_LICENSES`. `DapperLib/Dapper` and `jbogard/MediatR` were also checked and not added: both report `NOASSERTION` via the GitHub API and neither has a clean embedded LICENSE file, the same real-but-undetected-license situation as the 6 curl-type repos above, but adding a manual override for them was out of scope for this allowlist-only pass.
  **Question (1) resolved (2026-09-12), user decision: keep `_MANUALLY_VERIFIED_REPO_LICENSES`.** Explicit call: "yes keep manually verified repo lic override, its correct" -- the mechanism stays in `src/minifrontier/data.py` even though its 6 entries (curl/curl, lua/lua, pytorch/pytorch, facebook/zstd, libevent/libevent, chocolatey/choco) currently have zero exercised users in the allowlist (none of those 9 repos were found in the 2,000,000-row dataset scan above). Kept as a real, generically useful, already-tested capability for the next repo where the dataset's own per-row license field is wrong but the repo genuinely appears in the stream -- e.g. `DapperLib/Dapper` and `jbogard/MediatR` from the third pass above are real candidates for this same override if ever added. Not reverted.
  **README.md updated (2026-09-12):** the full curated repo list (237 entries, all languages/sections) added as its own section near the end of `README.md`, so the GitHub-code training-data provenance is visible without opening `configs/code-repo-allowlist.txt` directly.
  **Follow-up (2026-09-12), fourth research pass: C# section only, 39 real repos added (20 -> 59; file total 237 -> 276), from a user-supplied ~55-string candidate list with several typos/wrong-owner guesses.** Same rigor: every license confirmed via a direct raw LICENSE-family fetch, real star counts checked, several stale/wrong names resolved to their real current owner (`dotnet/aspire` -> `microsoft/aspire`; `Orleans/orleans` -> `dotnet/orleans`; `yarp/yarp` -> `dotnet/yarp`; `microsoft/KernelMemory` -> `microsoft/kernel-memory`; `BenchmarkDotNet/BenchmarkDotNet` -> `dotnet/BenchmarkDotNet`; `AspNet-Security-OAuth-Providers` (no owner given) -> `aspnet-contrib/AspNet.Security.OAuth.Providers`; `vlc-dotnet/vlc-dotnet` (nonexistent) -> `ZeBobo5/Vlc.DotNet`). `openiddict/openiddict-core`'s duplicate list entry was added once. One notable exception recovered from a prior rejection: `DapperLib/Dapper` (Apache-2.0) is now added, using nuget.org's own official `licenseExpression` package metadata as new, genuinely citable corroborating proof the prior pass didn't have. `_MANUALLY_VERIFIED_REPO_LICENSES` gained 7 entries this pass (`DapperLib/Dapper`, `SignalR/SignalR`, `protobuf-net/protobuf-net`, `MessagePack-CSharp/MessagePack-CSharp`, `cefsharp/CefSharp`, `OpenTK/OpenTK`, `auth0/auth0-aspnetcore-authentication` -- all real permissive licenses the GitHub API reports as NOASSERTION), its first real users since the mechanism was kept unused in the pass above. `tests/test_data.py` re-run after the `data.py` change: 25 passed, no new tests needed. `README.md`'s curated-list count was not refreshed this pass (out of scope; still shows 237).

Selection followed this project's own already-established rule for the GitHub-code component (real-world popularity/usefulness of the repo itself, the same principle the original 161-repo allowlist used) -- explicitly not StarCoder2's own internal byte-size proportions across categories (e.g., CMake being their single largest row was not a reason to weight CMake repos heavily here). **16 new, real, license-verified repos added** (`configs/code-repo-allowlist.txt`, 161 -> 177): 15 across the 9 new categories (C, SQL, Markdown, Dockerfile, CMake, PowerShell, Shell, Batchfile, TeX) plus `tensorflow/tensorflow` for the already-flagged AI/ML-library gap. TeX and Batchfile each only got 1 real, verified repo (permissively-licensed content in those categories is genuinely scarce) rather than the originally-scoped 2-4 -- flagged honestly, not padded with a weaker match. **No `--github-languages` CLI wiring needed**: the real MF-070 data-prep invocation already doesn't pass this filter at all (pulls whatever file types exist in the allowlisted repos, unfiltered) -- the fix here was ensuring real repos with this content exist in the pool, not adding a new filter; the real, verified `codeparrot/github-code` language-tag strings (`"C"`, `"SQL"`, `"Markdown"`, `"Dockerfile"`, `"CMake"`, `"PowerShell"`, `"Shell"`, `"Batchfile"`, `"TeX"`) are recorded in the allowlist file's own comment for reference if ever needed later.

- **Deliverable:** verified directly against the real StarCoder2 paper (arXiv:2402.19173, fetched and extracted via `pdftotext`, not paraphrased) -- page 5's real 32-language training-data table and page 11's real Table 2 (code documentation websites scraped):
  1. **Real, checkable gap in already-included AI/ML libraries**: `configs/code-repo-allowlist.txt` already includes NumPy, Pandas, SciPy, and Scikit-learn (confirmed by direct grep) -- **Matplotlib, PyTorch, and TensorFlow are missing**, despite being among the most commonly-used Python libraries in real-world code. Trivial fix: add 2-3 more real repos to the existing allowlist, same mechanism already in place.
  2. **A real finding about how filtering actually works today**: the real `prep-mf095-github-code` invocation (`scripts/run_overnight_mf083_mf095_mf097.ps1`) never passes `--github-languages` at all -- it pulls whatever file types naturally occur in the allowlisted repos, unfiltered. This means Markdown/YAML/Dockerfile/CMake/shell-script content (real, present in StarCoder2's own 32-language table but not in our narrower 12-language repo-selection criterion) shows up opportunistically as natural repo byproduct (READMEs, CI configs, build files), not by deliberate curation -- worth verifying real, meaningful representation exists rather than leaving it to chance.
  3. **Rosetta Code, confirmed real** (StarCoder2's own cited source for cross-language IR pairing, `Rosetta Code, 2023`) -- the same problem solved in many different languages side-by-side. A genuinely different, distinctive value from generic repo scraping: it directly teaches cross-language pattern transfer, which raw GitHub content cannot provide at all. Not yet sourced or tested.
  4. **API/library documentation text, confirmed real** (StarCoder2's own real Table 2: DevDocs, MDN, TensorFlow Docs, Qt, Boost C++, GCC docs, Oracle Java Tutorial, Hugging Face Docs, and 15 more real, named sites) -- prose *about* code and APIs, not code itself, a content type this project's mixture has zero of currently. Real, valuable, but a bigger lift than the allowlist tweaks above -- needs a new real data source, not just a filter change.
  5. **LLVM IR pairing, confirmed real but explicitly not recommended at this project's scale** -- StarCoder2 compiles 4M real sources across 11 languages (C++, C, Objective-C, Python, Rust, Go, Haskell, D, Fortran, Swift, Nim) into paired LLVM IR using a real compilation toolchain (clang/rustc/gollvm/ghc/ldc/flang/nlvm). Real engineering complexity (a working multi-language compiler toolchain) for a benefit better suited to large code-specialist models than this project's own small, educational scope -- recorded so it isn't rediscovered as if new, not queued as a real candidate.
- **Acceptance:** N/A until scoped as a real task. If pursued, items 1-2 are cheap, immediate, no-ratio-change fixes to the existing GitHub-code pipeline; items 3-4 would need real sourcing/license-checking following this project's own established data-provenance discipline before any use.
- **Status note:** raised 2026-09-12 directly from the user, verified against the real primary source (not the user's own paraphrase alone) before recording -- both the language table and the documentation-website table were fetched and extracted directly from the actual paper PDF.

### MF-122 — Add CRUXEval as a real code-reasoning eval task

A genuinely different mechanism from [[MF-127]]'s compile-check FIM harness (this predicts *output text*, not code); recorded here as a real, credible future extension of this task's own scope to C#, not actioned -- still P3, still not started.

### MF-127 — Real, right-sized C# code-completion eval: `score_csharp` + a versioned FIM fixture set

- **Priority / state:** P3 / Done -- built and tested 2026-09-13, directly prompted by the user rejecting an external batch of 3 elaborate "senior .NET architect" evaluation prompts (design a payment module, write a zero-allocation parser, implement custom JWKS/OIDC validation) as a mismatch for this model's real scale.
- **Depends on:** none; a real extension of [[MF-036]]'s existing fixture-scoring infrastructure (`src/minifrontier/evaluation/code.py`), not a new eval path
- **Real grounding for why the original 3 prompts were rejected, not just an opinion**: this project's own already-measured baseline (`reports/mf066-150m-edu-vs-modern-comparison.md`, cited via [[MF-066]]'s status note) found real pass@1 = **0/20** on basic, single-function Python HumanEval/MBPP problems for both 150M models -- before any C#-specific or enterprise-architecture complexity. Synthesis/judgment-heavy prompts (design a module, write a parser from a paragraph of requirements) test a fundamentally different, much harder capability than "complete a masked span given real surrounding context," which is the right-sized task shape for this model's real scale. A second, smaller round of the same over-ambitious pattern (FIM using a real ASP.NET Core JWT-bearer snippet, requiring external NuGet packages) was checked and also rejected on the same grounds, alongside two genuinely good, right-sized ideas that were folded in (a HumanEval-style single-function translation) or deferred to [[MF-122]]'s own scope (I/O-tracing, see above).
- **Deliverable, built for real:**
  1. `score_csharp` in `src/minifrontier/evaluation/code.py` -- a real `dotnet build` compile check (not a heuristic), deliberately narrower than `score_python`: `syntax_valid`/`compiles` report the same real boolean (no direct Roslyn dependency to separate parse-only from full compilation), and there is no functional test execution yet (a real, disclosed scope limit -- `score_fixture_predictions` raises `ValueError` if a C# fixture supplies `tests`, rather than silently ignoring them).
  2. **A real, verified fixture shape, not assumed.** Direct `dotnet build` experiments (not guessed) found bare top-level statements are the wrong shape for a fixture harness: an `Exe` project requires a genuine entry point (`CS5001` on a bare declaration), and top-level statements themselves must precede any type declaration in the same file (`CS8803`) while only being legal in `Exe` projects at all (`CS8805`). Resolved by requiring every fixture to reconstruct valid *members of a class* (a method or property body), wrapped as `class Fixture { ... }` and built as a `Library` project -- no entry point needed, sidesteps both rules uniformly.
  3. `score_fixture_predictions` gained a `language` field (default `"python"`, fully backward compatible with the existing fixture file) dispatching to `score_python` or `score_csharp`.
  4. **Real, original, dependency-free fixtures** (`eval/fixtures/code_csharp_fim_v1.jsonl`, licensed Apache-2.0, matching [[MF-036]]'s own "fixtures are original to this project" principle): a basic method completion, a LINQ filter (`fim` kind), a missing-semicolon repair, a pattern-matching `switch` expression, and a HumanEval-style algorithmic completion (`HasDuplicate`) -- deliberately using only the C# base class library, no NuGet packages, so scoring never needs network access once the SDK is installed. A matching `code_csharp_fim_v1_reference_predictions.jsonl` (mirroring the existing Python file's role) proves the scorer/report path for real: all 5 fixtures verified end to end (`exact=True`, `syntax_valid=True`, `compiles=True` for every one), and a genuinely broken completion was separately verified to actually fail to build (real discriminating power, not a check that always passes).
  5. 6 new tests in `tests/test_evaluation.py`, `dotnet`-dependent ones marked `@pytest.mark.skipif(not dotnet_available(), ...)` matching this project's existing pattern for optional external dependencies (e.g. `gpqa_diamond_zeroshot`/IFEval's own `not_run` handling) -- graceful skip, not a hard CI dependency on the .NET SDK being installed.
  6. **A complementary, zero-new-code recommendation, not yet run**: a held-out validation BPB check on a small C#-only slice via the existing `eval_checkpoint.py`/`evaluate_token_batches` machinery would give a different, useful signal (did the new C# allowlist repos measurably help raw next-token prediction on real C# text) without needing any new code -- recorded as a real, cheap follow-up, not yet executed since there is no real MF-070-scale checkpoint with the expanded C# allowlist trained yet.
- **Acceptance:** met -- `pytest tests/` passes with the 6 new tests (5 pass unconditionally, `dotnet`-gated ones verified for real in this environment where the SDK is present); `ruff check`/`format --check` clean.
- **Status note:** raised and completed 2026-09-13 in the same turn. No real model checkpoint has been evaluated against these fixtures yet -- this only builds and verifies the scoring infrastructure and the fixture set itself, the same "tooling ready, real run pending" status [[MF-125]]/[[MF-126]] were left in.

- **Priority / state:** P3 / Not started — real, legitimate candidates, explicitly not part of [[MF-095]]'s already-closed decision
- **Depends on:** [[MF-095]] (closed, `general-purpose-optimized` adopted) -- this would be a real expansion on top of that, not a replacement
- **Deliverable:** four real, well-established datasets named as candidates for a future mixture expansion, a materially higher-credibility batch than the earlier tokenizer-feedback rounds this session (these are established, widely-used pretraining sources, not vague vendor claims):
  1. **S2ORC/OpenAlex** (Semantic Scholar/Allen Institute) -- clean extractions of peer-reviewed academic papers across physics, biology, chemistry, CS, economics. Real, distinct value over DCLM-Edu/FineWeb-Edu: web crawls give casual science explainers, not formal hypothesis-structuring/data-interpretation/academic-discourse patterns. Suggested real starting point: 3-5% blended into the general web share.
  2. **StackExchange dumps** -- the real, complete Q&A archive (StackOverflow, AskUbuntu, MathOverflow, English Language & Usage, etc.) -- multi-turn problem-solving and expert-nuance content, a different shape from both prose and raw source code.
  3. **Wikipedia/WikiSource** (multi-lingual) -- the cleanest, most universally-trusted factuality/world-knowledge anchor, distinct from web-crawl-filtered educational text.
  4. **PubMed Central (PMC) Open Access** -- millions of real biomedical/life-sciences journal articles, a domain none of the current five sources cover at all.
- **Acceptance:** N/A until scoped as a real task -- recorded now as real, credible candidates, not yet sourced/license-checked/tested. Any real adoption needs the same real, bounded, own-corpus comparison discipline every other [[MF-095]]-class decision this session used, not assumed from citing that these are good datasets.
- **Real guardrails recorded for a future new-source-injection test (2026-09-12), one already verified true of this project's own setup, two unsourced heuristics worth keeping as starting points rather than validated thresholds.** (1) **"Warm decay" (don't let the learning rate hit zero when injecting new sources) is already satisfied, checked directly**: `TrainingConfig.min_learning_rate` (default `3e-5` against peak `3e-4`) means both WSD's decay phase and the cosine schedule already decay to 10% of peak, never to zero (`training.py`'s `WarmupStableDecaySchedule`/cosine implementations both stretch between `min_learning_rate` and `learning_rate`) -- not a new thing to add. (2) Keep the prose backbone (FineWeb-Edu/DCLM-Edu) at a real floor (~15-20%) during any decay-phase injection, rather than dropping it toward zero, to avoid the model fighting to rebalance its feature space. (3) Cap combined new-source injection at ~5-8% of the total token budget, since a small model's limited capacity can over-index on dense, unfamiliar sources (raw PMC/S2ORC specifically named as a risk) introduced too aggressively. (2) and (3) are sound, reasonable general ML principles, not verified thresholds from a specific cited study -- treat as real starting guardrails if this is ever tested, not as precise, validated numbers.
- **A real, important caution on the accompanying "multi-stage annealing curriculum" suggestion, not a reason to dismiss it outright.** The suggestion (fixed mixture for the first ~80% of tokens, then cool the learning rate and upsample high-quality/newly-added sources for the final ~20%, citing arXiv:2502.02737) describes the same real MiniCPM/SmolLM2-style annealing-phase idea [[MF-095]] already tested directly as `wsd-decay-curriculum` -- and found a real, measured **loss** (+0.756% relative CE vs. `wsd-fixed`), not a win, at this project's own bounded budget. **A real, honest distinction keeps this from being a simple repeat, though**: the already-tested arm reweighted the decay phase toward sources *already present* in the stable-phase mix (Cosmopedia-v2, FineMath) -- this new suggestion proposes upsampling **genuinely new sources not in the mix at all** (Wikipedia, S2ORC, StackExchange) during the decay phase specifically. That is a materially different experiment, not guaranteed to fail just because the previous, different version did -- but it should be treated with real caution, not assumed to work just because a cited paper and real production recipes (MiniCPM, SmolLM2) use the general pattern, given this project's own direct, contrary evidence at its own scale. Any future test of this idea must isolate that distinction explicitly (new-source annealing vs. existing-source reweighting) rather than reusing the old arm's design.
- **Status note:** raised 2026-09-12 directly from the user, alongside real, credible construction detail on Cosmopedia-v2 (v1's unsupervised seed-sample clustering vs. v2's real, predefined 34,000-topic BISAC book-classification taxonomy, sourced from SmolLM's own real blog post, `huggingface.co/blog/smollm`) already added to `README.md`. The four new sources and the annealing caution are recorded here as real, not-yet-actioned future work -- correctly separate from [[MF-095]]'s already-closed, adopted decision.

### MF-124 — Public-domain ebooks as a future mixture-expansion source, via the local `pdf-to-markdown-rag` pipeline

- **Priority / state:** P3 / Adapter built and tested (2026-09-12), real ingestion still not started — real, legitimate candidate, explicitly not part of [[MF-095]]'s already-closed decision, same status as [[MF-120]]. `iter_ebook_markdown` exists in `src/minifrontier/data.py` and is wired into `scripts/prepare_data.py` (`--source ebook-markdown`, `--ebook-directory`/`--ebook-license`/`--ebook-revision`/`--ebook-language`), covered by 5 tests in `tests/test_data.py` (book.md discovery ignoring the pipeline's chunks/metadata trees, custom license/revision/language, empty-book skipping, start/limit/shuffle, negative-argument rejection) plus a full CLI-dispatch test and an end-to-end `prepare_data.main()` test in `tests/test_prepare_data.py`. Not marked Done: no real public-domain titles have been sourced or run through the actual `pdf-to-markdown-rag` pipeline yet -- everything tested so far uses synthetic fixture `book.md` files, not a real book. `pytest tests/` after this change: 470 passed (up from 459).
- **Depends on:** [[MF-095]] (closed) -- a real expansion on top of the adopted mixture, not a replacement; no dependency on any other open item
- **Deliverable:** a new `iter_ebook_markdown`-style adapter in `src/minifrontier/data.py`, following the exact same shape as every existing `iter_*` source function, feeding `scripts/prepare_data.py`'s existing stream -> filter -> dedup -> tokenize -> pack path -- no new storage format, no SQLite/RAG layer involved.
  1. **Real, correct layer identified from the actual pipeline** (`docs/pdf-to-markdown-rag.zip`, read directly, not just the user's paste): its output is `md/<book-id>/book.md` (clean combined Markdown), `metadata/<book-id>/pages.jsonl`, and `chunks/<book-id>/chunks.jsonl` plus an optional `artifacts/corpus.sqlite3` (FTS5 + vectors). Only `book.md` is the right input for a pretraining `Document` -- the chunks/SQLite are retrieval-serving artifacts for RAG at inference time, a different consumption pattern entirely, not a pretraining-corpus format.
  2. **License gate confirmed by direct code read**: `Document.__post_init__` (`src/minifrontier/data.py`) only hard-enforces `PERMISSIVE_CODE_LICENSES` when `source_type == "code"` -- for `source_type="text"` (what ebooks would use), it just requires a non-empty `license` string. The code will not stop a dubious-rights ebook from flowing through; the real gate is a human decision, not a code one.
  3. **User decision on rights basis (2026-09-12): public domain only.** This is the clean case -- verifiably out-of-copyright texts, straightforward to record (e.g. `license="Public Domain"`) and defend, consistent with this project's existing provenance discipline. The pipeline's own `Book.license.json` sidecar (recording grant/scope/rights holder, with a `license_status` field) maps directly onto `Document`'s required `license`/`source`/`record_id` fields -- real, ready-made provenance to carry through, not something to invent.
  4. Scope explicitly excludes: any book without a clear public-domain basis (would need a separate, later decision if ever raised again); the pipeline's chunking/SQLite/embedding layer (wrong layer for pretraining); any change to `Document`'s code-level license gate (that gate is deliberately code-only and stays that way).
  5. **Adapter placement, resolved (2026-09-12): `iter_ebook_markdown` lives entirely in this project's own `data.py`, not inside `pdf-to-markdown-rag`.** That pipeline stays a separate, general-purpose tool needing zero MiniFrontier-specific changes -- it already produces exactly what's needed (`md/<book-id>/book.md` plus a `metadata/<book-id>/license.json` provenance sidecar). The adapter depends only on those output *files* at arm's length (a plain directory glob over an already-completed run of that tool), never on its code (no import of `rag_pdf_ingest`, no dependency added to this project's own `pyproject.toml`) -- the same external-tool boundary this project already applies to inference runtimes, just applied to a data-ingestion tool instead.
- **Acceptance:** the adapter itself is built and tested (see above); real ingestion is N/A until a real, concrete list of specific public-domain titles/sources exists, then the same real, bounded own-corpus comparison discipline as every other [[MF-095]]-class source decision before being added to any real training mixture.
- **Status note:** raised 2026-09-12 directly from the user, alongside a real request to document this project's data-storage-format choice (streaming ingestion, no persisted raw JSONL/Parquet cache, packed `.npy` token shards as the only on-disk training artifact) -- recorded in `docs/IMPLEMENTATION_DECISIONS.md` and `README.md`. Adapter code built and tested 2026-09-12 (see priority/state above); real, credible candidate, correctly kept separate from [[MF-095]]'s already-closed decision and from the [[MF-070]] gate.

### MF-125 — Optional Parquet export of the admitted corpus, for future HF Hub publishing

- **Priority / state:** P3 / Done — real, additive feature, explicitly not part of [[MF-095]]'s already-closed decision or the [[MF-070]] gate; built and tested 2026-09-12 directly from the user's own request ("add support publish the mixed corpus to hf as parquets")
- **Depends on:** none; a pure addition to `scripts/prepare_data.py`'s existing pipeline, no change to the existing shards-only behavior when unused
- **Deliverable:** `ParquetDocumentWriter` in `src/minifrontier/shards.py` -- snapshots the admitted, post-filter, post-dedup `Document` rows (raw `text` plus every provenance field: `source`, `revision`, `license`, `language`, `record_id`, `content_hash`, `path`, `source_type`, `split`, `parent_content_hash`, `transform`) to a Parquet file, in the same pass as `TokenShardWriter` tokenizes and packs the same stream -- captured from the admitted-document stream directly, not re-derived from the token shards. Wired into `scripts/prepare_data.py` as an optional `--export-parquet-dir DIR` flag, writing `DIR/train-00000-of-00001.parquet` and `DIR/validation-00000-of-00001.parquet` (one `ParquetDocumentWriter` each, routed by the same `split_bucket` decision the token shards already use) -- omitting the flag reproduces the exact prior shards-only behavior (`metadata.json` still records `export_parquet_dir`/`export_parquet_train_rows`/`export_parquet_validation_rows`, all `null` when unused).
  0. **Redesigned mid-session (2026-09-12) from a single combined file to this train/validation directory layout**, after the user clarified they'll publish manually via the Hub's own web UI and asked what the real standard HF layout is for a multi-source dataset -- confirmed directly against the real, current Hugging Face docs (`https://huggingface.co/docs/hub/en/datasets-manual-configuration`, fetched, not assumed) rather than guessed: `train-*`/`validation-*` is the Hub's own auto-detected split-file naming, which needs no `data_files` YAML at all when combined with `data_dir` per config (see [[MF-126]]). The original single-file design did not actually record which rows were train vs. validation at all (`Document.split` is set by `Document.create` but was never populated from the real `split_bucket` routing decision already computed in `prepare_data.py`'s own loop) -- a real gap caught before any real corpus was ever exported with it, not a shipped regression.
  1. **Why this exists, not just npy shards**: confirmed directly against the real upstream hosting format of every one of this project's own sources (`HuggingFaceTB/dclm-edu`, `HuggingFaceFW/fineweb-edu`, `HuggingFaceTB/finemath`, all genuinely Parquet-backed on the Hub, verified by URL) -- Parquet (raw, untokenized text plus metadata columns) is the right format for a *publishable* artifact precisely because it doesn't commit to this project's own tokenizer/vocab/packing choices, unlike the npy shards. See `docs/IMPLEMENTATION_DECISIONS.md` and `README.md`'s new "Data storage format" section for the full reasoning.
  2. **Memory safety**: buffers `batch_size` (default 1000) rows before each flush via `pyarrow.parquet.ParquetWriter`, rather than accumulating the whole corpus's raw text in memory before writing -- important since a real, larger-scale run could otherwise multiply this project's memory footprint by corpus size just for this optional export.
  3. **Explicit fixed schema, not per-batch inference**: `_DOCUMENT_PARQUET_SCHEMA` declares every field as a nullable string up front, avoiding a real pyarrow pitfall where inferring the schema fresh from each batch can silently disagree across batches when an optional column (`path`, `split`, ...) happens to be all-`None` in one batch and not another.
  4. **Atomic, immutable output**: writes to a `.<name>.tmp` path throughout, `os.replace`s to the final path only in `finalize()` -- same atomicity convention `TokenShardWriter._flush` already uses for shard files. A zero-row run still produces a real, valid, empty-schema Parquet file rather than leaving no file at all.
  5. `pyarrow` added as an explicit direct dependency in `pyproject.toml` (`>=18.0.0`) -- it was already present transitively via `datasets` (confirmed installed: 25.0.1), but code that imports it directly should declare it directly, not rely on another package's transitive dependency staying compatible.
- **Acceptance:** `tests/test_shards.py` -- round-trips every `Document` field through a real Parquet file (including `None`-valued optional columns), a zero-row file still validates with the right schema, non-positive `batch_size` is rejected (unchanged by the redesign, `ParquetDocumentWriter` itself still writes one file given one path). `tests/test_prepare_data.py` -- a full `prepare_data.main()` end-to-end run with `--source ebook-markdown --export-parquet-dir` confirms both the token shards and a *correctly split* `train-00000-of-00001.parquet`/`validation-00000-of-00001.parquet` pair are produced together (verified against the real, deterministic `split_bucket` routing for the two fixture books, not just a row count), no leftover temp files. All real, passing: `pytest tests/` after this change and [[MF-124]]'s adapter together: 486 passed (up from 459).
- **Status note:** raised and completed 2026-09-12 in the same turn, directly from the user's request; no HF Hub publish has actually happened yet (that remains a separate, future, real action the user would take manually themselves via the Hub's own web UI), this only builds and tests the export capability itself.

### MF-126 — Real Hugging Face dataset card generator for the published mixture

- **Priority / state:** P3 / Done — additive, needed for the real MF-070 3B-token run's data-publishing step (see MF-070's own updated entry); built and tested 2026-09-12 directly from the user's request ("upload our mixture to hf") and the ecosystem-completeness question that prompted it
- **Depends on:** [[MF-125]] (the Parquet exports this card describes)
- **Deliverable:** `scripts/build_dataset_card.py` -- reads each mixture source's real `metadata.json` (written by `prepare_data.py`) plus its real mixture weight, and generates a real, honest dataset card (`README.md` with YAML frontmatter) publishing the whole mixture as **one dataset repo with each source as its own named Hugging Face config** -- a `configs:` block with one `config_name`/`data_dir` pair per source, matched to [[MF-125]]'s own per-source `--export-parquet-dir` output directory naming.
  1. **The multi-config layout was confirmed against the real, current Hugging Face documentation** (`https://huggingface.co/docs/hub/en/datasets-manual-configuration`, fetched directly, 2026-09-12), not assumed or guessed -- `config_name`/`data_dir` per source is the Hub's own documented mechanism for a per-source Dataset Viewer dropdown and `load_dataset(repo_id, "github-code")`-style selective loading. The generated frontmatter is verified to be real, valid YAML matching this exact schema (`tests/test_build_dataset_card.py`'s own `yaml.safe_load` regression test), not just a string template that happens to look right.
  2. **Deliberately does not claim one blanket license for the dataset.** The GitHub-code component alone spans many different real per-file permissive licenses (see `configs/code-repo-allowlist.txt`); the card sets `license: other` and points readers at each Parquet row's own `license`/`source` columns instead -- consistent with this project's own per-document provenance discipline (`Document` in `data.py`), not a shortcut around it.
  3. **Real, measured content, not estimated**: per-source token counts come directly from each `metadata.json`'s `train.total_non_padding_tokens` + `validation.total_non_padding_tokens`, not the nominal mixture ratio; weights must sum to 1.0 (validated, real `ValueError` on mismatch) so the recorded table can't silently drift from the actual mixture.
  4. **`size_categories` bucketed correctly against the real Hugging Face convention** (by count -- token count is this project's honest proxy, since packed shards have no separate "example count"): a first draft had an off-by-one-bucket boundary bug (checked against the real MF-070 3B-token target, `3_000_000_000`, which the buggy version placed in `100M<n<1B` instead of the correct `1B<n<10B`) -- caught and fixed before shipping, with the 3B-token case now an explicit regression test.
  5. **The generated card's own "Repository layout" section spells out the exact folder structure** the user needs to arrange before publishing manually -- each source's `--export-parquet-dir` output placed at the repo root in a folder named exactly like that source's `config_name`/`data_dir`.
  6. **No custom upload code, deliberately.** Publishing is manual, either through the Hub's own web upload UI (the user's stated preference, 2026-09-12: "i will publish manually with my hf user in chrome") or `huggingface-cli login` + `huggingface-cli upload <repo>/<name> <directory> --repo-type dataset` -- both already-correct standard tools handling auth, resumable large-file upload, and repo creation. Reimplementing credential handling in this project's own code for no real benefit would be new, unreviewed surface area, contrary to this project's own minimalism principle.
- **Acceptance:** `tests/test_build_dataset_card.py` (16 tests) -- real totals/weights/admitted-document counts appear correctly in the generated card, weight-sum and out-of-range-weight validation, every real `size_categories` boundary (including the corrected 3B-token case), the `configs:` block matches the real HF schema via an actual YAML parse (not string matching alone), and a full CLI end-to-end `main()` run. All passing: `pytest tests/` after this change: 486 passed (up from 470).
- **Status note:** raised 2026-09-12; redesigned in the same session after the user clarified their real publishing intent (manual, one dataset repo, unsure whether sources should be flat or per-folder) -- resolved by fetching and following the real HF multi-config convention rather than guessing at a plausible-looking layout. No real dataset has been published yet -- there is no real MF-070 3B-token corpus prepared yet either, so this is tooling built ahead of the real need, tested against synthetic fixtures only.

### MF-133 — Three unverified architecture leads from external feedback: Differential Attention, logit soft-capping/QK-norm ordering, depthwise (Canon-style) local mixers

- **Priority / state:** P3 / Not started — research leads only, no primary source read yet, same treatment as [[MF-112]]
- **Depends on:** none structurally; would join [[MF-081]]'s Modern-only bounded-ablation slot if ever scoped
- **Deliverable:** three distinct, not-yet-verified candidates surfaced by a "Top 10" external suggestion list while [[MF-070]]'s real run was in progress (2026-09-14) -- checked against this project's own real state before recording (most of that list's other items turned out to be already adopted, already tested-and-rejected, or hard-excluded by `AGENTS.md`'s frozen V1 scope: MLA, DeltaNet/selective-SSM hybrids, and block-sparse long-context attention are all explicitly out of scope; partial RoPE was already tested and lost, MF-107; gated attention is already adopted, MF-082; mHC is already tracked as [[MF-112]]). These three have no prior record in this project at all:
  1. **Differential Attention** (Differential Transformer-style dual-branch subtraction/gating) -- computes two parallel attention maps and cancels noise between them; claimed robustness/quality gains, not independently verified here.
  2. **Logit soft-capping and pre-vs-post-RoPE QK-norm ordering** -- QK-Norm itself is already implemented and optional; soft-capping the attention logits (used in some recent large models for stability) and the RoPE/QK-norm application order are both genuinely untested refinements on top of it.
  3. **Depthwise causal convolutions / "Canon"-style local mixers** -- lightweight depthwise convs inserted near the attention/FFN blocks, claimed to improve local modeling at near-zero parameter cost, potentially most relevant to this project's own local (windowed) layers specifically.
- **Acceptance:** N/A until scoped -- per this project's own established discipline (see [[MF-112]]'s identical framing), each needs a real primary-source read (paper or reference implementation, not a secondary summary) before any bounded-ablation slot is committed, exactly the same bar every other [[MF-081]]-class item was held to.
- **Status note:** not scheduled; [[MF-070]]'s real 3B-token run is the current priority and these do not block it. Recorded per `AGENTS.md`'s "add newly discovered work to the backlog instead of hiding it" rule so the leads aren't lost, not because any of the three has been judged promising yet.
- **Real primary source found for half of item 2 (2026-09-16), closing part of this task's own "no primary source read yet" gap.** A research pass through `xai-org/grok-1`'s actual `model.py` (prompted by the same "look at other real models for ideas" request that produced [[MF-137]]) found a real, concrete reference implementation of attention logit soft-capping: `max_attn_val = 30.0; attn_logits = max_attn_val * tanh(attn_logits / max_attn_val)`, applied before softmax -- scale-independent, directly transferable to a dense model regardless of grok-1's own much larger MoE scale (which is otherwise irrelevant here and was not used for anything). **The other half of item 2 (pre-vs-post-RoPE QK-norm ordering) remains unsourced** -- grok-1 has no QK-norm at all (Q/K go straight into RoPE), so it can't inform that question either way. Soft-capping specifically now clears this task's own acceptance bar ("a real primary-source read... before any bounded-ablation slot is committed"); QK-norm ordering does not yet. Still not scoped as a real bounded-ablation slot -- finding the source is necessary but not sufficient per this task's own acceptance criterion, and [[MF-070]]'s real run remains the priority regardless.
- **Real, generational update (2026-09-17), from a direct read of Gemma4's own primary sources (arXiv, real Hub `config.json`, the local `gemma-main.zip` repo, see [[MF-144]]): Gemma3 used per-layer `attn_logit_softcapping`, but Gemma4 dropped it.** Every real Gemma4 factory config has `attn_logits_soft_cap=None` -- only `final_logit_softcap=30.0` remains (applied once, to the final output logits, a different and much smaller-footprint mechanism than per-layer attention soft-capping). Worth citing this generational reversal alongside grok-1's own reference implementation if this lead is ever scoped -- the newest real precedent moved away from the per-layer version specifically, not a reason to abandon the lead outright (grok-1's own real, tested use of it remains a valid primary source), but a real, disclosed data point cutting the other way that a bounded test should account for.

### MF-134 — Two-layer local cache for `iter_github_code_from_repos`, so repeat runs don't re-clone or re-extract from scratch

- **Priority / state:** P3 / Done (2026-09-15) -- both layers implemented, tested, wired in
- **Depends on:** the MF-070 direct-fetch implementation itself (`iter_github_code_from_repos`, `data.py`, 2026-09-15), already Done
- **Deliverable:** the original one-shot implementation (`_clone_repo_ephemeral`, kept as the `cache_dir=None` fallback) did a fresh `git clone --depth 1` into a `tempfile.TemporaryDirectory()` per repo, deleted right after extraction -- correct for unblocking the real MF-070 run once, but meant every *future* re-run would re-clone and re-walk all 161 repos from scratch. Real design settled via discussion, now built as two independent, complementary layers:
  - **Layer 0 (git mirror cache), `_ensure_repo_mirror`/`_clone_via_cached_mirror`**: a persistent bare mirror clone per repo (`git clone --mirror` once, `git fetch --prune` on later calls past `max_staleness_seconds`, default 60 days -- a real, deliberate middle of the "2-3 months" range asked for), one folder per repo under `data/github-code-cache/`, each independently refreshable -- genuinely incremental (only pulls new objects), matching real, verified CI-caching-vendor practice (a real vendor's own docs describe the identical "clone --mirror once, fetch deltas later" shape). A "single file" or "single shared object store with many remotes" design was considered and explicitly not used: a `git bundle` is a real single-file format but a static export you can't fetch new commits into in place; a single bare repo with 161 remotes sharing one object store is real, valid git, but trades away per-repo isolation (one corrupted/locked shared store risks all 161 repos at once) for a cosmetic "one location" property a shared parent *directory* already gives without that risk. `--github-force-refresh` (new CLI flag, `prepare_data.py`) bypasses the staleness check and re-clones unconditionally. The actual local checkout from a cached mirror is a plain local `git clone` (hardlinked, same-filesystem), not `git worktree` -- reconsidered mid-build and simplified: these files are only ever read, never edited/committed, so a genuine working tree tied to the mirror's own lifecycle (and its removal bookkeeping) is unneeded complexity for no real benefit here.
    **Atomicity, added after a direct question ("make sure git handling is atomic and safe, in case our process closes"):** bootstrapping a mirror now clones into a `.tmp` staging sibling first, publishing via `Path.replace()` only once the clone genuinely completes -- closes a real gap the first draft had (a killed process mid-bootstrap-clone left a permanently-broken mirror at its real path, silently caught-and-skipped forever on every later run since `mirror_path.exists()` would be true but `git fetch` on it would keep failing). A `git fetch` on an already-real mirror needed no equivalent wrapper: git's own object writes are content-addressed and ref updates apply atomically at the end, so a killed fetch leaves the mirror in its previous valid state, never a corrupt one. `_rmtree_readonly_safe` also added: plain `shutil.rmtree` on a bare git repo raises a real, confirmed `PermissionError` on Windows (git marks its own pack/object files read-only) -- fixed with an `onexc` handler that clears the read-only bit and retries. Regression test: a leftover `.tmp` staging directory (simulating an interrupted bootstrap) is cleaned up and a fresh clone started correctly.
  - **Layer 1 (extracted-document cache), `_tee_to_parquet_document_cache`/`iter_parquet_documents`**: a real Parquet dump (`data/github-code-cache/documents.parquet`) of every already-extracted, already-filtered document (post-clone, pre-tokenization, before `languages`/`start`/`limit` are applied) -- a later call with an existing cache file replays it directly, no git and no network at all, and the same cache serves a rebuild with different `--github-languages`/`--limit`/`--sequence-length` without re-extracting. **Format choice revised mid-build, twice, both for real reasons.** First landed on `.jsonl.zst` (matching Pile/RedPajama/Dolma's raw-corpus convention) -- corrected after a live web check: that's the standard for a *published, static* raw-corpus dump, not a *project-local reuse cache*; the real, verified precedent for the latter is HuggingFace `datasets`' own local cache, which is Arrow/Parquet-family specifically for fast, columnar, memory-mappable reuse reads.
