Switched to Parquet, reusing `shards.ParquetDocumentWriter`/a new `iter_parquet_documents` reader (local import, `data.py`<->`shards.py` would otherwise be circular) rather than building a second writer. This also let the `zstandard` PyPI dependency added for the first attempt be removed again (`uv remove zstandard`) -- `pyarrow`'s own Parquet writer supports zstd compression natively, verified directly, no separate package needed.
    **A real, unrelated environment incident happened mid-pivot**: `uv remove zstandard` triggered a full dependency re-resolution that uninstalled `torch`, `lm-eval`, and ~35 other real packages from the venv (outside the base `dependencies` group the bare command apparently resolved against) -- caught immediately via a broken `import torch`, fixed with `uv sync --extra cu130 --group dev --group eval --group labs` to restore the exact original environment shape. A real, worth-remembering lesson: `uv remove`/`uv add` on this project's venv should be paired with an explicit `--extra`/`--group` sync check afterward, not assumed to preserve every previously-installed optional group.
    **Compression default reconsidered again, a third real correction**: `ParquetDocumentWriter` (shared with [[MF-125]]'s existing HF-publish export) defaulted to Parquet's own traditional `snappy` codec. Verified directly (real, current HF docs): snappy is HF's own dataset-viewer converter's deliberate choice specifically for fast decompression under many *interactive* reads (a live web UI) -- a real reason that does not apply to either of this project's own two Parquet use cases (a one-time publish download, or an occasional local cache replay), where zstd's better compression ratio is the more relevant real benefit. `ParquetDocumentWriter` gained a `compression` parameter (default changed to `"zstd"` for both the export and the new cache, a single shared default now rather than an override) -- confirmed no existing test hardcoded the old codec before changing it. **The 4 already-built MF-070 mixture parquet exports (cosmopedia-v2/finemath/fineweb-edu/dclm-edu, built before this change under the old snappy default) were re-compressed in place** via a real, row-count-verified conversion (read, write to zstd, verify new row count matches original before atomically replacing) -- real, measured savings: 27-33% smaller across all 8 files (e.g. dclm-edu's train file: 4,063.6MB -> 2,862.7MB, 1,121,762 rows verified unchanged).
  **Two more real gaps found and fixed in the same pass, from a direct "see if more places need this atomic pattern" audit** (a fresh general-purpose subagent searched `src/`/`train/` for non-atomic writes of trusted state): `checkpoint.py`'s `export_release` wrote every release file (`model.safetensors`, config, tokenizer, README, generation config) directly into its target directory, no staging -- fixed to use the identical staging+`.old`-swap pattern `save_training_checkpoint` (MF-130) already uses, with the same self-healing regression test (crash between the two renames, verified both the old and new release survive intact under their own names). `tokenizer.py`'s `MiniFrontierTokenizer.save()` wrote `tokenizer.json` then `tokenizer_config.json` directly and sequentially -- a real, standalone caller (`scripts/train_tokenizer.py`, the actual production-tokenizer entry point that built `data/tokenizer`) had no outer staging protecting it at all, unlike the `export_release`/checkpoint callers. Fixed with per-file (not whole-directory) atomic rename, since `save()`'s own target directory may already hold other real files a caller is writing separately (as `export_release` now does, in its own already-atomic staging pass) -- a whole-directory stage-then-rename would have destroyed those. Real gaps NOT found (re-confirmed, not just re-asserted): `TokenShardWriter`/`ParquetDocumentWriter` (already atomic), `save_training_checkpoint` (MF-130's fix confirmed complete), `scale.py`'s report writer (already atomic), `DiskDeduplicator` (correctly append-only by design, not a gap), and the terminal per-run summary files (`prepare_data.py`'s `metadata.json`, `train/pretrain.py`'s `run.json`) -- real, but not a risk, since nothing automated re-reads them to decide resume/trust.
  **Real precedent check, honestly caveated**: no verified insight into any specific frontier lab's own internal git-mirror-caching architecture. What is real and documented: for internet-scale code ingestion, BigCode/The Stack pull from Software Heritage's own pre-built archive rather than each lab re-cloning independently -- a path this project already explicitly declined ([[MF-110]]: requires a credentialed AWS account, against the "free to reproduce" principle). At this project's real, much smaller scale (161 curated repos, refreshed occasionally), a persistent per-repo git-mirror cache plus a Parquet document-reuse cache is the right-sized answer; Software-Heritage-grade infrastructure solves a problem this project doesn't have.
- **Acceptance:** met -- real tests (all using a real local git repo fixture and/or injectable `clone_repo`/`resolve_license`, no real network in the fast suite) cover: first-run bootstrap clone, second-run fetch-not-reclone within the staleness window, stale-cache-triggers-a-real-fetch, `force_refresh` bypassing staleness, self-healing from a leftover `.tmp` staging directory, the document cache being created then replayed (with the injected `clone_repo`/`resolve_license` asserting they are *not* called on replay), the same cache reused across a different `languages` filter, and `force_refresh` bypassing the document cache too. Full fast suite: 538+ passed, `ruff check`/`format --check` clean.
- **Status note:** Done. Documented in `README.md`'s "Data storage format" section (both layers, and the Parquet-vs-JSONL.zst reasoning) per a direct request to record both formats and their real uses.
  **Real cleanup refactor (2026-09-15), same day, user-requested**: all git-exe subprocess handling (`clone_repo_ephemeral`, `ensure_repo_mirror`, `clone_via_cached_mirror`, `rmtree_readonly_safe`, the staging/atomicity logic) moved out of `data.py` into a new `src/minifrontier/git_utils.py` -- `data.py`'s own scope stays document provenance/streaming, not git mechanics. License-text detection (`_detect_license_from_text`/`_resolve_repo_license`) deliberately stayed in `data.py`: it reads a file that happens to live inside a git clone, but never invokes git itself, and is real provenance logic matching that file's own stated scope. One-directional dependency (`data.py` imports from `git_utils.py`, never the reverse) -- no circular-import risk, verified. Moved functions dropped their leading underscore (`_ensure_repo_mirror` -> `ensure_repo_mirror`, etc.), since they're now `git_utils.py`'s own real public surface, not `data.py`-internal helpers. Tests split the same way, matching this project's established one-test-file-per-source-file convention: the 7 real git-mirror-cache tests (using a real local git repo fixture, no network) moved to a new `tests/test_git_utils.py`; the Parquet-document-cache tests (which test `data.py`'s own `iter_parquet_documents`/`iter_github_code_from_repos`, not git mechanics) stayed in `test_data.py`. Full fast suite re-confirmed passing after the split.
  **A real, near-miss caught right before declaring the pipeline safe to launch (2026-09-15)**: a stale `data/github-code-cache/documents.parquet` file was found still sitting at the real project path -- leftover fake test data (`"print('hi')\n"`, `x/a:main.py`) written by a real unit test *before* the `document_cache_path=None` test-isolation fix above landed. Had this gone unnoticed, the actual pipeline launch would have found this file, treated it as a complete real cache, and replayed **one fake document instead of doing the real 161-repo extraction** -- a silent, wrong-data bug, not a crash. Deleted; re-ran the full suite and confirmed the real path is never recreated by any test now. A real, concrete argument for why "did the fix work" needs checking against the actual filesystem state, not just "did the test pass."
  **Real research pass (2026-09-15), user-requested: how do real GUI git clients (SourceTree/VS Code/Visual Studio/GitKraken/Tower) and git's own documentation inform `git_utils.py`, while staying industry-standard.** Real, primary-source-verified findings (git-scm.com's own docs fetched directly, not a secondary summary), each independently tested before landing, not trusted on the docs alone:
  - **Adopted: `git fetch --prune-tags` alongside the existing `--prune`.** Plain `--prune` alone does not remove deleted tags by default (verified against real git-mirroring guidance, then tested directly: a tag deleted on a real local origin repo was confirmed to persist in the mirror without this flag, and to be correctly removed with it).
  - **Adopted: `_sync_mirror_head` (a real, previously-unclosed gap, now fixed and tested).** `--mirror`'s own fetch refspec (`+refs/*:refs/*`) does not cover the top-level `HEAD` file -- confirmed directly from git's own docs -- so a plain `git fetch --prune` never re-points an already-cached mirror at a renamed remote default branch, the exact gap flagged (and left as a disclosed, unfixed limitation) earlier the same day. `git remote set-head` was the first candidate fix considered and confirmed *not* applicable: it manages `refs/remotes/<name>/HEAD`, which `--mirror` clones never create (verified directly from git's own docs, not assumed). The real fix: `git ls-remote --symref <url> HEAD` (queries the remote's actual current default branch, no local clone needed) then `git symbolic-ref HEAD <branch>` on the mirror -- both real, documented, purpose-built git commands, not a workaround. Real regression test: a local origin's default branch is renamed mid-test (matching a real `master`->`main`-style rename), and a subsequent stale-triggered refresh is confirmed to follow it, both the symref and the resolved commit SHA.
  - **Tried and reverted, a real negative result worth keeping recorded so it isn't re-attempted blindly: `--filter=blob:none` (blobless partial clone) on the mirror bootstrap.** Git's own docs describe this as compatible with `--mirror` in the abstract ("useful for bandwidth-constrained scenarios while maintaining mirror semantics for future updates") -- real, but insufficient without testing this project's own actual pipeline shape. A direct, real end-to-end test (a real bare local origin with `uploadpack.allowfilter` enabled server-side, a genuinely blobless mirror confirmed via `git rev-list --missing=print`, then the exact real second step this project's own `clone_via_cached_mirror` takes -- a plain `git clone` of that mirror into a working tree) failed outright: `error: unable to read sha1 file... fatal: unable to checkout working tree`. Root cause: a local clone of a local bare repo does not inherit the mirror's own promisor/upstream relationship needed to lazily fetch the missing blobs -- real for this project's specific two-hop shape (a persistent mirror, then a *separate* local clone for extraction), not a general flaw in `--mirror`+`--filter` itself. Caught by testing before it reached the real pipeline, not discovered in production. **A real, separate testing gotcha found along the way, worth remembering for any future git-related test**: `--filter` is silently ignored against a plain local filesystem path (`warning: --filter is ignored in local clones; use file:// instead`) -- a `file://`-prefixed URL is required to genuinely exercise partial-clone behavior in a local test, and the local "server" additionally needs `uploadpack.allowfilter=true` set explicitly (GitHub.com already supports this for real remote clones, unrelated to this local-testing requirement).
  - **Considered, no change made**: whether real large-scale AI/LLM code-corpus pipelines (BigCode/StarCoder/The Stack) reveal a more specific "industry standard" for cloning many individual GitHub repos -- they don't, because that precedent bypasses individual `git clone` entirely (consuming Software Heritage's own pre-built archive instead), a path this project already declined for real, unrelated reasons ([[MF-110]]: credential-free access). General git-mirroring/CI-caching practice (already the basis for this task's own design) remains the correct comparison at this project's real, much smaller scale.
  Full fast suite re-confirmed passing after these changes; `ruff check`/`format --check` clean.
  **Real completeness audit (2026-09-15), directly requested ("is git utils complete or we forgot important feature we need"), found and fixed two real robustness gaps, both confirmed against real, concrete failure scenarios, not assumed:**
  1. **`--quiet` was correctly not the problem -- git's own real behavior only suppresses progress/status noise, never errors -- but this project's own calling code was silently discarding captured stderr on every clone/fetch failure.** `_extract_repo_documents`'s `except Exception: continue` swallowed the exception with zero diagnostic; a real, multi-hour run could fail on many repos with no way to know why (transient network error? repo renamed? git not installed?). Fixed: a real `WARNING: skipping <repo>: <detail>` print to stderr, preferring a `CalledProcessError`'s own real captured stderr (what git itself actually said) over the generic exception text when available, falling back gracefully for any other exception type (e.g. `FileNotFoundError` if git itself isn't on PATH).
  2. **A more serious, previously-unprotected gap: only the clone step was wrapped in a try/except -- `resolve_license()` and the entire file-walking loop were not.** An unexpected exception anywhere past the clone (a `resolve_license` edge case, a file-walking surprise never anticipated) would have propagated all the way up and killed the whole generator, losing every remaining repo in a real 161-repo run over one bad one -- directly contradicting this task's own already-stated design intent ("one bad repo can't crash the whole run"), which the implementation only actually enforced around the clone call. Fixed by extracting the per-repo body into its own generator (`_extract_one_repo_documents`) and wrapping the entire `yield from` call in one outer `except Exception` -- verified for real (not just reasoned about) that `yield from` correctly propagates an exception raised mid-iteration by the sub-generator back to the outer handler, via a real regression test (`resolve_license` raising `RuntimeError` for one repo, confirming both the diagnostic prints and the *next* repo still gets processed, not just that the crashing one is skipped).
  **The "orphaned cache entry" item above was re-framed (2026-09-15, user-confirmed) from an unresolved gap into a deliberate, now-tested design decision, not something to build cleanup logic for.** User's own words: a repo that disappears from GitHub should stay in `configs/code-repo-allowlist.txt` (never auto-removed), a failed fetch should just skip it for the current run, and the *existing* cached mirror should be kept exactly as-is -- "its the latest available." Verified this is already exactly what the real code does, not assumed: grepped the whole codebase and confirmed nothing anywhere ever writes to `configs/code-repo-allowlist.txt` (every reference is a read, a CLI arg, or a comment); and confirmed directly from `ensure_repo_mirror`'s own real control flow that a failed `git fetch` raises *before* reaching any line that would touch `mirror_path` -- the directory is left completely untouched, still holding the last real, successfully-fetched content. This was previously only an emergent property nobody had actually locked in with a test -- added `test_ensure_repo_mirror_preserves_the_existing_cache_when_a_refresh_fails` (real: deletes the origin entirely, confirms the refresh fails loudly with a real `CalledProcessError`, then confirms the existing mirror still exists and still resolves to the original real commit, unchanged). **Also considered and deliberately not added**: automatic retry on a transient clone/fetch failure. A single flaky network moment currently costs one repo for the *current* run only (a failed bootstrap never publishes a real mirror, so `mirror_path.exists()` stays false and the *next* full pipeline run retries it cleanly) -- combined with the new diagnostic visibility (gap 1 above), this is judged an acceptable, disclosed limitation rather than a gap worth the added complexity of a retry/backoff mechanism.
  Full fast suite: 547 passed (up from 546), `ruff check`/`format --check` clean.
  **Real run interrupted (2026-09-15, ~19 minutes in, 14/161 repos cloned) by a genuine crash -- three distinct real bugs found and fixed from this one incident, not one:**
  1. **A real, extension-dictionary gap** (found by a direct question -- "do we miss any extension" -- prompting an actual re-check of `configs/code-repo-allowlist.txt` rather than trusting the original 12-language framing): MF-121 added nine real language categories to the allowlist on 2026-09-12 (C, SQL, Markdown, Dockerfile, CMake, PowerShell, Shell, Batchfile, TeX, with real repos like `libuv/libuv`, `openssl/openssl`, `supabase/supabase`, `Kitware/CMake`, `ohmyzsh/ohmyzsh`) but `_GITHUB_EXTENSION_LANGUAGES` was never updated to match -- those repos would clone successfully and yield **zero documents each**, every real file silently unmatched. Fixed: added `.c`/`.h` (C -- a real, disclosed, unresolved ambiguity with C++'s own header convention, since plain `.h` is used by both in practice), `.sql`, `.md`/`.markdown`, `.cmake`, `.ps1`/`.psm1`/`.psd1`, `.sh`/`.bash`/`.zsh`, `.bat`/`.cmd`, `.tex`, plus real filename-based (not extension-based) matching for `Dockerfile`/`Dockerfile.*` and the exact `CMakeLists.txt`, since neither has a distinctive extension of its own. Real regression test covers all nine categories in one pass.
  2. **A real robustness gap one level deeper than the repo-level fix from earlier the same day, found from a direct follow-up ("even if 1 file/1 repo/1 folder fails it shouldn't fail all loop"):** the per-repo `except Exception` (added earlier today) covers a whole repo's clone/license/extraction as one unit, but within a repo, only the file *read* step had its own narrow `(UnicodeDecodeError, OSError)` catch -- an exception anywhere after that (line-length scan, license-header stripping, `Document.create`'s own validation) would abandon the *rest of that repo's real, good files*, not just the one bad file. Fixed by extracting the per-file body into its own `_document_for_file` helper, called under a per-file `try/except Exception: continue` -- silent on failure, matching this same loop's own already-established quiet-skip convention for file-level issues (unlike the louder, repo-level diagnostic; a single bad file among possibly thousands isn't worth a warning print).
  3. **The real, immediate crash itself: a genuine Windows `MAX_PATH` (260-character) limit hit on a real repo (`dotnet/reactive`, a deeply-nested UWP test-app-packaging path), compounded by a real, separate, more severe bug in the orchestration script.** The new per-repo diagnostic print (added earlier the same day) correctly caught and reported the failure (`WARNING: skipping dotnet/reactive: error: unable to create file ... Filename too long`) -- proof the *Python-level* handling worked exactly as designed. But `run_mf070_150m_release.ps1`'s own `Invoke-Step` combines `$ErrorActionPreference = "Stop"` with `*> $logFile` (redirecting a native command's stdout+stderr together) -- a real, confirmed Windows PowerShell 5.1 behavior: with `Stop` active, *any* stderr line from a native command gets promoted from a non-terminating `NativeCommandError` into a terminating one, aborting the whole script -- even though `python.exe` itself was working correctly and would have continued to the next repo on its own. Fixed by scoping `$ErrorActionPreference` down to `"Continue"` for just the one native call (restored to `"Stop"` immediately after), leaving the already-correct `$LASTEXITCODE` check as the real, sole pass/fail signal. **Root cause (the MAX_PATH hit itself) also fixed, not just the crash around it**: added `-c core.longpaths=true` (a per-invocation git config override, verified to never touch the user's persistent global git config, matching the same principle already applied to the earlier `uploadpack.allowfilter` question) to both real checkout-producing clone calls (`clone_via_cached_mirror`'s local checkout, `clone_repo_ephemeral`'s fallback clone) -- not the bare mirror bootstrap, which never needs it, since git's own internal object storage uses fixed-length, content-addressed paths regardless of the repo's real directory structure, only an actual working-tree checkout recreates arbitrarily-long real paths on disk. **Real, empirically verified, not trusted from git's own docs alone**: reproduced the exact real failure locally (a genuinely committed file at a matching deep path, checkout failing with the identical `Filename too long` error without the fix) and confirmed the fix resolves it cleanly. A real alternative (moving the cache to the drive root to shorten the path prefix) was raised and correctly rejected: the real failing path's own *internal* portion was already ~200 characters on its own, so a shorter prefix only raises the threshold for this one repo, it does not remove the underlying limit the way `core.longpaths=true` does (up to ~32,767 characters).
  **Real cleanup after the interrupted run**: partial `data/shards/mf070-150m-3b-github-code`/`data/parquet/mf070-150m-3b-github-code` output deleted (the interrupted run's own real progress there is invalid, pre-fix); the git-mirror cache itself (`data/github-code-cache/`, the 14 real repos already cloned) was deliberately preserved, so a restart skips re-cloning them and only needs the fast, local, no-network re-extraction pass before continuing with the remaining ~147 repos.
  **Real question asked and directly verified before restarting a multi-hour run, not just assumed (2026-09-15): is the file-walk/extraction step idempotent -- can it safely re-run against the same cached repo content?** Reasoned through first (the pipeline is a pure, deterministic transform -- same cached mirror commit -> same checked-out files -> same deterministic license detection -> same `sorted()` file walk -> same `Document`s, including a real SHA-256 `content_hash`; nothing in the extraction path mutates the git mirror or its staleness marker, only an actual `git fetch` does that), then confirmed empirically with a new real test (`test_github_code_from_repos_extraction_is_idempotent_across_repeated_runs`): the same simulated repo content extracted twice, independently, produces byte-identical `Document`s including matching `content_hash` values. **A real, necessary follow-up clarification, also recorded**: "idempotent" here means *same cached commit -> same documents*, not frozen forever -- once the mirror's own real staleness window passes (or `--force-refresh` is used) and a real `git fetch` updates it, the *next* extraction run correctly sees the repo's real, current state and produces different (fresher) documents. Both properties are intentional and coexist: stable/reproducible within a staleness window, genuinely fresh across one -- the whole point of today's real switch away from the frozen 2022 snapshot.
  Full fast suite re-confirmed passing after the idempotency test; `ruff check`/`format --check` clean.
  **Real second interruption (2026-09-15, ~4h20m into the restarted run, 165+ repos done): Git LFS confirmed as a real, active problem, not a hypothetical -- found from actual log content, not guessed.** `saadeghi/daisyui`'s entire mirror bootstrap ballooned to a real **12GB** and timed out at the real 600-second limit; `microsoft/vscode`, `qdrant/qdrant`, and `JetBrains/kotlin` each hit real "smudge filter lfs failed" / "remote missing object" errors during their local checkout (caught and reported correctly by the per-repo diagnostic from earlier the same day -- proof that fix already worked; this is a new, separate root cause). Real total cache size had reached **76GB across 166 folders** by the time this was caught, with `Azure/azure-sdk-for-net` mid-clone at a real 8GB and still growing (no timeout hit yet, but the currently-running process still lacked the fix).
  **Real fix: skip Git LFS content entirely, not work around individual failures.** None of the LFS-tracked binary content (images, tarballs, sqlite caches) is code this project wants anyway, and this project's own extension-based filter would discard it even if it downloaded successfully -- so removing the fetch attempt entirely removes the failure mode, rather than papering over it. `GIT_LFS_SKIP_SMUDGE=1` (git-lfs's own real, documented environment variable for exactly this) added via a new `_no_lfs_env()` helper (a real `os.environ.copy()`, not a replacement -- replacing the whole environment would risk breaking git's own PATH/credential-helper/SSH resolution) and wired into all four real git subprocess calls that clone/checkout/fetch (`clone_repo_ephemeral`, `ensure_repo_mirror`'s bootstrap and its `fetch`, `clone_via_cached_mirror`'s local checkout) -- verified for real, not just assumed wired: a new test spies on every real `subprocess.run` call during a genuine local clone-and-checkout cycle and confirms every one received the env var.
  **A second, independent real gap found and fixed from the same incident**: `subprocess.run(..., timeout=600)` terminates the child process on timeout but never deletes whatever it already wrote -- `saadeghi/daisyui`'s real 12GB staging directory was left orphaned on disk indefinitely (no future cleanup would happen until a *later* bootstrap attempt for that exact same repo happened to trigger the existing pre-clone cleanup check). Fixed: the mirror bootstrap's own `subprocess.run` call is now wrapped in `try/except Exception` that removes the real staging directory before re-raising, regardless of whether the failure was a clean `CalledProcessError` or a `TimeoutExpired`. Real regression test: a fake `subprocess.run` writes real partial content to the staging path before raising `TimeoutExpired`, confirming the staging directory is gone afterward.
  **A real, separate operational lesson, not a code bug**: killing the parent `python.exe` process (via `Stop-Process`) did not terminate its own child `git.exe` subprocess -- a real, known Windows behavior (no automatic process-group cascade the way POSIX signals would provide). The orphaned git process (a local checkout already reading from an already-complete mirror) had to be found and stopped separately. Worth remembering for any future manual stop of this pipeline: check for and clean up orphaned child processes explicitly, not just the top-level python.exe.
  **Real allowlist size correction, surfaced while investigating the 165-vs-"161" discrepancy**: `configs/code-repo-allowlist.txt` actually holds **276 real entries**, not the "161" figure repeated earlier this session (that number was accurate only for the original 12-language pass, before this project's own later, extensive addition rounds -- MF-121's 9 new categories plus several later popularity-driven passes -- grew it substantially). 165+ cached mirrors partway through a run is genuine real progress against 276, not an overshoot or a test-data leak (briefly suspected and ruled out directly: the two names matching a crude "test" substring check, `google/googletest` and `stretchr/testify`, are real, legitimate repo names, not artifacts).
  Both processes (the real `prepare_data.py` and its orphaned git child) stopped cleanly; partial `data/shards/mf070-150m-3b-github-code`/`data/parquet/mf070-150m-3b-github-code` output removed; all 166 real cached mirrors (including `Azure/azure-sdk-for-net`, whose bootstrap had already completed successfully) preserved for the next restart. Full fast suite: 552 passed (up from 549), `ruff check`/`format --check` clean.
  **Real per-repo mirror-size anomaly check (2026-09-15), user-provided real `du`-style sizes across the 166 cached mirrors.** A handful of monorepos dominate total disk use: `Azure/azure-sdk-for-net` alone at **8.36GB**, then `JetBrains/kotlin` 4.53GB, `kubernetes/kubernetes` 3.87GB, `dotnet/roslyn` 3.84GB, `vercel/next.js` 3.53GB, `supabase/supabase` 3.07GB, `tensorflow/tensorflow` 3.03GB, `microsoft/vscode` 2.58GB, `dotnet/maui` 2.54GB, `aws/aws-sdk-net` 2.43GB, plus ~20 more in the 0.5-2.3GB range (`godotengine/godot`, `apache/pulsar`, `envoyproxy/envoy`, `pandas-dev/pandas`, `bazelbuild/bazel`, `rust-lang/rust`, `gradle/gradle`, `scikit-learn/scikit-learn`, `grpc/grpc`, `swc-project/swc`, `apache/hadoop`, `django/django`, `apache/kafka`, `ggml-org/whisper.cpp`, `QuantConnect/Lean`, `quarkusio/quarkus`, `apache/lucene`, `golang/go`, `openssl/openssl`, `dotnet/sdk`, `nestjs/nest`, `argoproj/argo-cd`) -- these ~31 repos alone sum to roughly 62-63GB out of the real ~76GB/166-repo total. **Root cause confirmed, not assumed**: a real `git ls-tree -r -l HEAD` scan (current-tip file sizes straight from each mirror's object database, no checkout needed) across all 166 repos found only **13.50GB of real current-tip file content total** -- meaning the great majority of on-disk mirror size is full commit *history* (every blob ever committed, across years of commits for these specific monorepos), not the current source tree this pipeline actually extracts from. This is the real, now-quantified cost of the `--mirror` (full-history) design already chosen deliberately for reliability over a blobless partial clone (see the `--filter=blob:none` revert above, same task) -- disclosed and accepted, not a bug: the large repos still clone and extract correctly, they simply cost more disk than their extracted contribution to the training mixture would suggest.
  **Real submodule-handling check (2026-09-15), user-requested ("check that our git util know to handle those cases correctly").** 11 of the 166 cached repos carry a real `.gitmodules` (`AvaloniaUI/Avalonia`, `apache/rocketmq`, `grpc/grpc`, `icsharpcode/ILSpy`, `numpy/numpy`, `nvm-sh/nvm`, `openssl/openssl`, `rust-lang/rust`, `swc-project/swc`, `webpack/webpack`, `xunit/xunit`) -- confirmed via `git show HEAD:.gitmodules` against each real mirror, not guessed. Verified directly (real local checkout of `openssl/openssl`, which alone declares 10 real submodules including `krb5`, `pyca-cryptography`, `wycheproof`, `fuzz/corpora`): since `clone_via_cached_mirror`'s local checkout is a plain `git clone` with no `--recurse-submodules` flag, git's own real, correct default behavior already applies -- each submodule path is created as a genuinely **empty** directory (confirmed by listing `krb5/` and `fuzz/corpora/` post-checkout: zero entries besides `.`/`..`), no network call attempted, no error raised. This is the desired behavior already, with no code change needed: submodule content is a separate repo's own code (would need its own independent license/provenance resolution this pipeline doesn't do), and an empty directory contributes zero documents to extraction, which is correct. `microsoft/vscode` itself has **no** `.gitmodules` entry -- its Copilot extension (the one earlier referred to informally as "the submodule") is a plain subdirectory of the vscode repo proper, not a real git submodule; its LFS declaration lives at `extensions/copilot/.gitattributes`, already correctly caught by the tree-wide (not root-only) `git grep -l "filter=lfs" HEAD -- "*.gitattributes"` check used to confirm LFS repos below.
  **Action taken**: the 3 confirmed-LFS cached mirrors (`JetBrains__kotlin.git` 4.5GB, `qdrant__qdrant.git` 147MB, `microsoft__vscode.git` 2.6GB) were deleted from `data/github-code-cache/` so the next run re-clones them fresh under the now-active `GIT_LFS_SKIP_SMUDGE=1` fix, rather than keeping mirrors bootstrapped before that fix existed (which may hold incomplete LFS-smudge state from the original failed attempts). 163 mirrors remain cached; `saadeghi/daisyui` (also LFS, confirmed via its earlier 12GB timeout) was never cached successfully and needs no deletion -- it will be attempted fresh on the next run regardless.
  **Real directory-structure/size scan across all 166 cached mirrors (2026-09-15), user-requested, to decide what belongs in `_GITHUB_CLONE_SKIP_DIRS` before extraction.** Same `git ls-tree -r -l HEAD` approach as the anomaly check above (real per-blob sizes straight from each mirror's object database, no checkout needed), this time aggregating bytes by directory-name pattern (any path component, case-insensitive) against a candidate list of common non-source directory names. Real results, largest first: `tests` 1.80GB/197k files/83 repos, `test` 1.47GB/168k files/102 repos, `docs` 1.12GB/26k files/95 repos, `www` 400MB (1 repo only -- not a general pattern), `third_party` 263MB/8 repos, `testdata` 234MB/96k files/25 repos, `examples` 177MB/59 repos, `doc` 172MB/25 repos, `assets` 139MB/44 repos, `thirdparty` 131MB/4 repos, `samples` 119MB/20 repos, `documentation` 75MB/13 repos, plus smaller amounts (`locale`, `images`, `__tests__`, `snapshots`, `website`, `translations`, `testfixtures`, `benchmarks`, `fixtures`, `spec`, `e2e`, `i18n`, etc). Total: **6.44GB** (of the real 13.50GB current-tip total across all 166 repos) sits under one of these candidate names -- already-excluded build-artifact dirs (`.git`/`node_modules`/`vendor`/etc, the existing `_GITHUB_CLONE_SKIP_DIRS`) account for a comparatively small further 0.33GB, leaving 6.73GB of real, non-matched, plausibly-genuine source content. The two dominant top-level dirs that remain unmatched and clearly *are* real source (`sdk` 2.28GB, `src` 879MB -- both expected, dominated by `Azure/azure-sdk-for-net`'s own generated-SDK breadth) confirm the candidate list isn't accidentally swallowing legitimate source trees. **Implemented (2026-09-15)**: `_GITHUB_CLONE_SKIP_DIRS` extended with `testdata`, `test-data`, `fixtures`, `testfixtures`, `__snapshots__`, `snapshots`, `docs`, `doc`, `documentation`, `assets`, `images`, `img`, `media`. **`test`/`tests`/`__tests__` themselves deliberately NOT added**, after real back-and-forth: first added per an initial broad choice, then reverted the same session -- real source files inside test directories (assertions, real API usage) are genuine training signal, same treatment `examples`/`samples` (177MB/119MB, 59/20 repos) already get and were also deliberately not touched. Considered and rejected: a heuristic to keep only "real usage" test files while dropping "dummy"/mock-heavy ones -- no reliable general way to make that distinction from directory/file structure alone (would need per-language semantic analysis of mocking-library usage, fragile and unexplainable across this project's 12+ supported languages), so the fallback is "keep all real source under test/tests, only strip the non-source content in and around them." **A related, independently-found real gap fixed in the same pass**: the skip-dir match (`part in _GITHUB_CLONE_SKIP_DIRS`) was case-sensitive against an all-lowercase set -- a real scan across the 166 cached repos found genuine case variants for these exact names (`Tests` in 14 repos, `Assets` in 13, `TestData` in 9, `Documentation` in 8, `Images` in 8, `testFixtures`/`Test`/`Fixtures`/`testData`/`Media`/`Snapshots`/`Docs` in smaller counts) that a case-sensitive check would have silently let through; fixed by lowercasing each path component before the membership check. Two new tests (`test_github_code_from_repos_keeps_real_source_files_under_test_directories`, `test_github_code_from_repos_skips_noise_directories_regardless_of_case`), plus the existing noise-directory test extended to cover the new names. Full suite: 540 passed (excluding the separate, slower `test_git_utils.py` real-git-repo suite), `ruff` clean.
  **Considered and rejected, real primary-source research done (2026-09-15), not just a quick local test: replacing the persistent `--mirror` cache with a persistent, periodically-refreshed shallow (`--depth 1 --single-branch`) bare clone, to close the gap this same finding quantified (13.5GB of real extractable current-tip content vs. ~76GB of actual on-disk mirror size, dominated by full commit history on a handful of monorepos -- `Azure/azure-sdk-for-net` alone 8.36GB).** A real local test (fresh shallow bare clone, a normal fast-forward update, a real history-rewriting force-push, then the same second-hop local checkout `clone_via_cached_mirror` already does) passed end to end, including correctly inheriting shallow state on the second hop with no repeat of the earlier `--filter=blob:none` two-hop breakage -- **but this was correctly challenged rather than trusted on a small synthetic test alone.** Direct primary-source verification (git-scm.com's own docs, fetched live, not recalled from memory) settled it two ways: `git-clone`'s own docs describe `--mirror` as purpose-built for exactly this project's real use case ("persistent mirror repositories that need to be kept up-to-date with periodic refreshes"); `git-scm.com/docs/shallow` itself discloses real, still-unresolved edges directly relevant to a *repeatedly refreshed* shallow cache specifically (not one-shot shallow clones, which are fine and extremely common, e.g. CI checkouts) -- "unclear how push into/from a shallow repo should behave," a real locking/race-condition caveat on the shallow-boundary file across fetches, and tag handling on deepening that "does not work right now." A real, easy-to-miss mechanical finding from the local test, also worth keeping on record: `git fetch --depth <n>` into an already-shallow repo rejects even an ordinary, genuinely fast-forward-compatible update as "non-fast-forward" unless the refspec is force-prefixed (`+branch:branch`) -- the shallow boundary itself defeats git's normal ancestry check, so a shallow persistent cache would need to force *every* refresh unconditionally, not just real rewrites. Given the user's own already-established principle this session ("stay the `--mirror` way, unless reliability is similar") and this real, primary-source-confirmed asymmetry -- `--mirror` is the documented right tool for this exact job, shallow-as-persistent-cache has git's own acknowledged rough edges for it -- kept as `--mirror`, unchanged. No re-clone of any kind needed; this was evaluated and rejected before any implementation, not after.
  **A real, unrelated root-cause correction found while investigating a second real interruption (2026-09-15): `saadeghi/daisyui`'s "mirror bootstrap ballooned to 12GB" finding (recorded above under the LFS incident) was mis-attributed -- it is NOT an LFS problem, and `GIT_LFS_SKIP_SMUDGE` does not fix it.** Verified directly, not assumed: a real local reproduction (a genuinely LFS-tracked file pushed to a real local bare origin, then `git clone --mirror` against it, with and without `GIT_LFS_SKIP_SMUDGE=1`) showed a bare/mirror clone transfers only the tiny real LFS pointer text either way (72KB total) -- smudge filters never run at all during a bare clone (no working tree is ever materialized), so the env var was already a no-op for this specific failure mode from the start; it only ever helped the *other* three repos (`kotlin`/`qdrant`/`vscode`), whose failures happened during the real second-hop *checkout* (which does materialize a working tree and does invoke smudge). **The real root cause, confirmed via GitHub's own live REST API (`api.github.com/repos/saadeghi/daisyui`), is that the repository's own real, disclosed size is 12,338,027 KB (~12.3GB)** -- almost exactly matching the observed ballooning -- independent of LFS entirely, most plausibly from binary assets (screenshots/demos, common for a UI component library) committed directly into ordinary git history rather than through LFS. Given this real cost for what a CSS/component library realistically contributes in actual extractable source (a small fraction of that 12.3GB), and that it would keep re-attempting and timing out on every single future run indefinitely (a failed bootstrap never publishes a mirror, so nothing ever marks it "done") -- **removed from `configs/code-repo-allowlist.txt`** rather than accepting the recurring real cost. Real allowlist count: 275 (down from 276).
  **A real, separate, well-evidenced disk-cost finding from the same investigation, found by cross-checking GitHub's own REST API `size` field against this project's own real local mirror sizes for the largest cached repos**: `Azure/azure-sdk-for-net` (GitHub: 4.65GB, local mirror: 8.36GB), `kubernetes/kubernetes` (GitHub: 1.5GB, local: 3.87GB), `dotnet/roslyn` (GitHub: 2.6GB, local: 3.84GB) -- all substantially *larger* locally than GitHub's own reported repository size, a real, measurable gap (1.5-2.6x) worth explaining, not just observing. Root cause confirmed directly on the real cached `kubernetes/kubernetes` mirror: 62 real branches, 1,245 real tags, 123,523 total real refs (`git for-each-ref`) -- almost certainly including GitHub's own `refs/pull/*` history for a project with this many merged PRs over its lifetime, none of which this project's own extraction ever reads (only the current default-branch tip is ever checked out). Real object-reachability comparison confirms the mechanism directly: 1,655,323 objects reachable from `HEAD` alone vs. 3,451,427 from `--all` -- a ~2.08x ratio, closely matching the observed ~2.6x disk-size gap. **Ruled out as the explanation, checked directly rather than assumed**: git submodules do not explain this -- `kubernetes/kubernetes`/`dotnet/roslyn`/`Azure/azure-sdk-for-net` are not among the real 11 repos with a `.gitmodules` found earlier this session, and even if they were, this project's plain (non-`--recurse-submodules`) checkout already leaves submodule paths completely empty regardless (already verified on `openssl/openssl`), so submodule content can never inflate a mirror's size under this project's own cloning code either way. **A real, well-evidenced, and -- unlike the shallow-clone idea above -- *validated* design improvement follows directly from this**: since `--mirror`'s only real advantage over a plain, full-history, single-branch clone is fetching refs this project never reads, a persistent, single-branch (still full-history, *not* shallow) bare clone should give most of `--mirror`'s disk cost back without inheriting any of shallow's real reliability gaps. **Verified two ways before treating this as sound, matching this session's own established discipline: a real local test, and a real primary-source check of git's actual C source (not docs, per direct request), not just one or the other.**
  - **Real local test** (`git clone --bare --single-branch`, no `--depth`): correctly fetched only the tracked branch (plus, expectedly and harmlessly, any tag reachable from that branch's own history -- confirmed this is `--single-branch`'s real, documented behavior, not a leak) and never touched an unrelated second branch created in the same test repo. A plain (non-forced) fetch correctly succeeded for a genuine fast-forward update -- unlike the shallow case, no forced refspec is needed for an ordinary update, only for a genuine history rewrite (standard git behavior for any branch tracking, confirmed by first getting a real "non-fast-forward" rejection without `+`, then success with it). A real default-branch rename was correctly handled by reusing the exact same `git ls-remote --symref` mechanism `_sync_mirror_head` already uses, fetching the new branch name into a new local ref (reusing already-downloaded objects, confirmed fast) and re-pointing `HEAD`. The second-hop local checkout (matching `clone_via_cached_mirror`'s real existing second step) succeeded cleanly and was confirmed *not* shallow -- no repeat of the earlier `--filter=blob:none` two-hop failure.
  - **A real, easy-to-miss mistake caught mid-verification, worth recording precisely because it demonstrates why "the fetch command exited 0" is not sufficient evidence of correctness**: a follow-up check assumed a `--single-branch --bare` clone would auto-configure a `remote.origin.fetch` refspec (the way `--mirror` does), so that a later plain `git fetch --prune` (no explicit refspec) could rely on it. This was empirically wrong -- the fetch exited 0, downloaded the new commit into the object database and `FETCH_HEAD`, but **the real branch ref never moved** (confirmed directly via `git for-each-ref`, not just trusting the exit code). **Confirmed against git's own actual C source** (`builtin/clone.c`, `write_refspec_config()`, fetched live from `github.com/git/git`, per direct request to check real source over docs): the refspec-writing block is gated on `option_mirror || !option_bare` -- so a plain `--bare` clone gets *no* fetch refspec written at all, regardless of `--single-branch`, and only `--mirror` gets the automatic `+refs/*:refs/*` convenience. **This does not invalidate the single-branch design** -- the actual working test steps (3-5 above) always passed an explicit `+branch:branch` refspec on the command line, which is unaffected by this gap and is now understood to be a *requirement* of this design, not an optional convenience: every refresh must explicitly track and pass the branch name itself (readable from the local mirror's own `git symbolic-ref --short HEAD`), rather than relying on stored config the way the current `--mirror`-based `ensure_repo_mirror` does.
  **Superseded before landing by a simpler, better design (2026-09-15), found by directly asking whether `--mirror` itself could be kept via a flag/config fix instead of replaced by per-refresh bookkeeping.** The single-branch-with-explicit-refspec-tracking design above was validated but never actually implemented -- a real follow-up test found something strictly better: a plain `git clone --bare --no-tags` (not `--single-branch` -- a plain bare clone already fetches every branch by default, real-confirmed directly) followed by **one, one-time** `git config remote.origin.fetch "+refs/heads/*:refs/heads/*"` gives back `--mirror`'s *exact* operational convenience -- bootstrap once, refresh forever after with a plain, argument-free `git fetch --prune`, zero Python-side bookkeeping -- while the wildcard (not a named branch) makes it automatically robust to a remote default-branch rename, real-tested directly (a real rename on a real local origin, then a plain `git fetch --prune` with no explicit arguments: the renamed branch's fresh content was fetched under its new name, and `--prune` itself deleted the old, now-gone branch name's local ref with no manual cleanup needed). The one real remaining gap -- a wildcard branches refspec still does not cover the top-level `HEAD` file, so `HEAD` was confirmed to dangle at the deleted old name after the rename+prune above -- is *identical* to `--mirror`'s own already-known gap, and is fixed by reusing the existing `_sync_mirror_head` function completely unchanged (the mechanism doesn't care which refspec fetched the content). **Implemented and shipped** (`src/minifrontier/git_utils.py`'s `ensure_repo_mirror`): all branches still get fetched (a real, deliberately tolerated, small cost -- kubernetes' real 62 branches are a rounding error next to the 1,245 tags and ~122k likely `refs/pull/*` refs this design actually excludes), only tags and non-standard ref namespaces are dropped. `_sync_mirror_head`'s own docstring updated to confirm its HEAD-rename fix re-verified directly against the new design, not assumed to still hold. Real regression tests: `test_ensure_repo_mirror_never_fetches_tags` (bootstrap and a subsequent refresh both confirmed tag-free, replacing the now-obsolete `..._prunes_a_real_deleted_tag_on_refresh`, whose premise -- that tags get fetched at all -- no longer holds); an added-then-deleted test asserting other branches are *excluded* was itself wrong (contradicted the design's own real, intended behavior of fetching all branches) and was removed rather than "fixed" to match a false premise. Full suite: 554 passed (up from 552 before this session's git-cache work), `ruff check`/`format --check` clean on both changed files. **Real, disclosed limitation, not a bug**: the 163 already-cached mirrors from the old `--mirror` design are unaffected by this change on the next pipeline restart -- they are within the 60-day staleness window, so `ensure_repo_mirror` reuses them as-is (still their old, larger, full-`--mirror` size and stored refspec) rather than retroactively narrowing them; the new design only applies to freshly bootstrapped or force-refreshed cache entries (the 3 just-removed LFS repos on this restart, and any repo naturally recloned or refreshed later). A full 166-repo reclone was considered and deliberately not done -- not necessary for correctness, and not worth the real extra bandwidth/time this session's own priority (getting the actual MF-070 training run moving) doesn't need blocked on a non-urgent disk optimization.
  **Real, precise Azure SDK for .NET measurement under the new design (2026-09-15), computed directly against the already-cached mirror -- no network needed.** `git rev-list --disk-usage --objects --branches` (new design) vs `--all` (old `--mirror`) on the real, already-downloaded `Azure__azure-sdk-for-net.git`: **4.69GB new (branches only) vs 8.57GB old (all refs)**, a real ~45% reduction -- `du -sh` confirms 8.2GB on disk today, consistent with the `--all` figure. Real ref breakdown: **1,715 branches**, 7,478 tags, 54,878 total refs -- branches are a much bigger share here than for `kubernetes/kubernetes` (62), so this repo's real branches-only cost is less negligible, but still far smaller than the tag/PR-ref volume being dropped. **User decision: keep it in the allowlist.** Unlike `saadeghi/daisyui`, this size is judged proportional to real training value -- a large, professionally-maintained, legitimate C# SDK codebase across many Azure services, not binary/asset bloat disconnected from source content; it will remain the single largest cache entry by a wide margin even after the fix, and that is an accepted, deliberate cost.
  **Real, basic, atomic resumability added to the document cache (2026-09-15), user-requested after the real "617MB `.tmp`, confirmed unreadable" finding above (`pyarrow.parquet.ParquetFile` raised `ArrowInvalid: Parquet magic bytes not found in footer` on the genuine `exit -1` kill's leftover file) made the cost of the old, whole-run-spanning single-write design concrete, not hypothetical.** Old design: one `ParquetDocumentWriter` spans the *entire* multi-hour run, only gets a valid footer at the very end (`finalize()`) -- a hard kill at *any* point loses *all* progress, unreadable and unresumable, regardless of how much real work it represents. **New design** (`_extract_repo_documents_resumable`/`_finalize_document_cache_from_parts`, replacing `_extract_repo_documents` + the now-dead, removed `_tee_to_parquet_document_cache`): one real, independently-finalized Parquet part file per repo, under `document_cache_path`'s own sibling `<stem>.parts/` directory, named deterministically from the repo name (`repo_name.replace("/", "__") + ".parquet"`). **Design choice, made deliberately, not the only option considered**: per-repo checkpointing (not batched ranges like "repos 1-20, 21-40") -- a repo is already this codebase's own natural atomic unit of work (`_extract_one_repo_documents` already processes one fully before the next), so per-repo sidesteps a whole category of edge cases a batch scheme would need to handle (a batch interrupted partway through, tracking which repos are in which batch) for no real benefit; worst-case lost work on a crash is exactly one repo's re-extraction, not up to a whole batch's. **No separate manifest file** -- a part file's own existence *under its real, final (non-`.tmp`) name* is itself the complete "already done" signal, so there is nothing that could drift out of sync with the real files on disk (a manifest-plus-files design would need to keep both in agreement; this design has only one source of truth). Each part reuses `ParquetDocumentWriter`'s own already-atomic `finalize()` (staging `.tmp` + `os.replace`) -- the same mechanism, not a second one -- so a crash mid-write of any *one* repo's part leaves only that repo's own orphaned `.tmp` (self-healed on the next attempt at that same repo: proactively deleted before re-extracting, the identical principle already used for the git-mirror cache's own staging path) and never touches any other, already-completed repo's part. A repo that fails entirely (clone error, unresolved license) still gets a real, valid, empty-schema part written (`ParquetDocumentWriter.finalize()`'s own existing empty-case handling) -- preserving, not changing, this project's already-established "a failed repo is skipped for good, not retried forever" contract (a fully-replayed cache already never re-attempted a zero-document repo either). The single, final `document_cache_path` file -- the one thing `iter_github_code_from_repos`'s fast-path replay actually checks for -- is only ever (re)built once every repo in `names` has a real part on that call, by `_finalize_document_cache_from_parts`: reads each part's documents back via the existing `iter_parquet_documents` and re-writes them into one fresh `ParquetDocumentWriter`, one part's worth of rows at a time (never every part loaded into memory simultaneously), then best-effort (non-load-bearing) deletes the now-superseded `parts_dir` -- `document_cache_path`'s own existence stays the sole authority for "complete," matching the existing, unchanged contract exactly (an early stop via `limit`, or a hard kill mid-run, both still correctly leave no complete `document_cache_path` behind). `force_refresh=True` discards any existing `parts_dir` outright, matching the already-existing "bypasses both caches unconditionally" contract. Real regression tests (`tests/test_data.py`): a resumed run replays an already-completed repo's part without re-calling `clone_repo`/`resolve_license` for it, while still extracting a not-yet-done repo fresh, producing one correct combined final cache; an early-stopped (simulated-interrupted) run leaves no final cache but leaves the one already-completed repo's own real part file behind, and does not create a part for the not-yet-reached repo; a permanently-failed repo's empty part is never retried on a later replay; `force_refresh` discards existing parts and re-extracts everything; a leftover `.tmp` from an interrupted single-repo part-write is self-healed rather than blocking that repo. Full suite: 558 passed (up from 554), `ruff check`/`format --check` clean.
  **Real clarification recorded (2026-09-15), by design, not a surprise or a bug**: every one of the 163+ real cached mirror folders under `data/github-code-cache/` is a *bare* clone (`--bare`, no working tree) -- browsing one directly shows only git's own internal object database (`objects/`, `refs/`, `HEAD`, `config`, `objects/pack/*.pack`), never plain, readable `.py`/`.cs`/`.js` source files sitting in a directory tree. The real source content is there, just stored as compressed, content-addressed blob objects inside the pack files, not laid out as a browsable tree -- confirmed directly inspectable without a checkout via `git -C <mirror> ls-tree -r HEAD` / `git -C <mirror> show HEAD:<path>`, the exact technique already used this session to inspect LFS pointer files and compute real sizes. This is deliberate, not incidental: a bare clone is the correct, standard format for something meant to be `git fetch`-refreshed repeatedly (this project's own Layer 0 cache design, already established earlier in this task) -- a persisted working-tree checkout alongside it would roughly double each repo's real disk cost (the compressed objects *and* a full plaintext copy of every file) for no benefit, since nothing ever reads these directories directly. The actual human-readable checkout step *does* happen, just transiently and never persisted: `clone_via_cached_mirror`'s real second step clones the bare mirror into a `tempfile.TemporaryDirectory()`, which is where extraction genuinely walks real files -- deleted automatically the moment that one repo's extraction finishes, never landing in `data/github-code-cache/` at all.

### MF-132 — A parquet re-import loader, so a published HF dataset repo (or its local `--export-parquet-dir` output) could feed back into `prepare_data.py`

- **Priority / state:** P3 / Not started
- **Depends on:** [[MF-125]] (the parquet export format this would read back)
- **Deliverable:** verified directly (2026-09-14), while building [[MF-070]]'s real launch script, that no such path exists today: `prepare_data.py`'s `document_stream` only supports two source kinds -- a streaming `--source {fineweb-edu,dclm-edu,finemath,cosmopedia-v2,github-code,ebook-markdown}` or `--manifest <file.jsonl>` (`iter_jsonl_documents`), and `--manifest` explicitly rejects `--limit`/`--start`/`--shuffle-seed` (cursor/shuffle options require a streaming source). [[MF-125]]'s `--export-parquet-dir` output (and any future HF dataset repo built from it, per [[MF-126]]) is one-directional -- written for publishing, never wired as something `prepare_data.py` itself can read back in as a document source.
- **Acceptance:** a new loader (e.g. `iter_parquet_documents`, mirroring `iter_jsonl_documents`'s shape) that reads a local `--export-parquet-dir`-style directory (or, via `datasets.load_dataset("parquet", ...)`/`datasets.load_dataset(<hf-repo-id>, <config-name>)`, a published HF dataset repo) back into `document_stream`, reconstructing real `Document` rows (including provenance fields) rather than raw tokenized shards -- letting a mixture already published to the Hub be re-tokenized (e.g. at a different `--sequence-length`, or with a different tokenizer/packing mode) without re-running the original streaming/filtering/dedup pipeline against each upstream source again.
- **Status note:** user question, 2026-09-14, while reviewing [[MF-070]]'s real data-prep script ("what if we in the future want to import to prepare_data the 5 folders in our parquet export... to use in pretrain?"). Not blocking [[MF-070]]'s own real run (which reads straight from the streaming sources as always); recorded per `AGENTS.md`'s "add newly discovered work to the backlog instead of hiding it" rule, not implemented now.

### Small real fix (2026-09-13), prompted by a disk-I/O-pattern question about this same pipeline: `DiskDeduplicator` now batches its SQLite commits

Not its own MF-number -- a small, isolated hygiene fix to existing infrastructure, found while answering the user's question about whether the new Parquet export (above) was HDD-friendly. The Parquet writer itself was already well-batched (1,000-row flushes); the *same real pipeline*'s pre-existing `DiskDeduplicator` (`src/minifrontier/shards.py`, used by every `prepare_data.py` run since early in this project, not something this session added) turned out to call `.commit()` after every single admitted document -- a much higher-frequency write/fsync pattern than the Parquet export, for potentially millions of documents on a real corpus.

**Fixed**: `DiskDeduplicator` gained a `commit_batch_size: int = 1000` constructor parameter (matching `ParquetDocumentWriter`'s own batching convention) -- `add()` now only commits every `commit_batch_size` calls, and `close()`/`__exit__` always flushes any remaining partial batch first, so a normally-completed run never loses its own tail end. **Real correctness argument, not just a performance tweak taken on faith**: `classify()` and `add()` share one live SQLite connection for the whole run, and a connection always sees its own uncommitted writes -- so batching `commit()` only defers *durability* (what survives an actual crash), not *within-run* duplicate detection, which stays exactly as correct as committing every row. `prepare_data.py`'s own pipeline has no per-document resume anyway (`--output` must be absent or empty to start; a killed run's partial output is discarded and restarted from scratch, never resumed row by row), so batching introduces no new correctness risk given that existing design. 3 new tests in `tests/test_shards.py`: rejects non-positive `commit_batch_size`; a fresh reader connection sees only committed batches while the writer stays open, and sees everything after a normal `close()`; an exact duplicate arriving after a batch boundary has already committed is still correctly caught (validating the durability-only claim directly, not just asserting it). Full suite: 489 passed (up from 486), `ruff check`/`format --check` clean. Verified the concurrently-running [[MF-108]] training was completely undisturbed by this change (separate process, separate files) -- GPU still at 100% utilization/checkpoint progressing throughout.

### MF-123 — Source real step-by-step reasoning demonstrations for the required SFT stage

- **Priority / state:** P1 / Not started — real, scoped implementation work created by [[MF-116]]'s resolved decision. **Not part of the pre-[[MF-070]] gate** (user correction, 2026-09-12): [[MF-070]] is the base-pretraining run; SFT is a separate, later stage that only needs a trained base checkpoint to fine-tune, so this can proceed after [[MF-070]] launches/completes rather than blocking it.
- **Depends on:** [[MF-116]] (the decision this implements); reuses [[MF-059]]/[[MF-060]]/[[MF-061]]'s already-built SFT mechanism unchanged (loss masking, training loop, eval harness) -- this is a data-content task, not a mechanism change
- **Deliverable:** a real, license-clear, provenance-tracked set of SFT examples where the assistant's response demonstrates explicit multi-step reasoning (showing intermediate steps, not just a final answer), replacing or supplementing the current trivial 32-example placeholder set (`artifacts/mf066-tmp/sft_dataset.jsonl`, plain instruction-following only, e.g. `"Reply with exactly the word RED"` -> `"RED"`). Real, concrete candidate source worth checking first: **GSM8K's own real training split** (distinct from its test split, which the eval harness already uses) already contains human-written, step-by-step worked solutions for real grade-school math word problems -- directly on-target for this project's own stated math/code priority, and likely requires no new dataset search if its license/format check out.
- **Acceptance:** every SFT example carries the same real provenance fields this project already requires elsewhere (source, revision, license, record ID, content hash -- matching `AGENTS.md`'s existing code-data manifest discipline, applied here to SFT data). **A real, explicit contamination check is required, not assumed**: if GSM8K's train split is used, verify directly that the lm-eval harness's own `gsm8k` task evaluates against the test split only (the standard, well-established convention for this benchmark, but this project's own rule is to verify, not assume). A real base-vs-SFT comparison (reusing [[MF-061]]'s existing evaluation pattern) shows the reasoning-demonstration-trained model actually produces step-by-step responses, not just longer generic text.
- **Status note:** raised 2026-09-12 as the direct, real follow-on from [[MF-116]]'s resolved open scope question. Not started; joins the pre-[[MF-070]] gate per the user's own explicit inclusion.

### MF-116 — Reasoning-capability priorities for the Modern preset: data/SFT/scale outrank architecture, and today's eval suite can't see small-scale progress on this axis

- **Priority / state:** P1 / Done — analysis complete, real decision made (see below); the resulting real, scoped implementation work is handed off to [[MF-123]]
- **Depends on:** none structurally; directly informs how [[MF-095]]'s mixture work and [[MF-098]]'s SFT/distillation scope get prioritized
- **Deliverable:** two real findings from a direct comparison exercise (2026-09-12): the user posed 4 genuinely hard questions (a parasitic-gap syntax judgment requiring real explanation, not just a grammaticality guess; a multi-step arithmetic word problem with an independent state-tracking trap; a "Monty Fall" variant designed so memorized-puzzle pattern-matching gives the wrong answer; Boolos's "Hardest Logic Puzzle Ever," requiring embedded counterfactual questions) to four frontier models (real transcripts in `4q.md`, hand-graded against the actual correct answers). All four models handled the syntax/arithmetic/trap questions correctly; one model had a real, traceable logical gap on the hardest (formal multi-step construction) question -- see `4q.md`'s own grading section for the full trace.
  1. **Ranked priorities for closing this capability gap on MiniFrontier's own Modern preset, at the current 150M-500M scale**: (a) data quality/mixture toward reasoning-dense text (math, code, synthetic textbook-style reasoning) -- the highest-leverage, already-in-motion lever ([[MF-095]]); (b) SFT with demonstrations that explicitly work through multi-step reasoning, not just instruction-following Q&A pairs -- already a required V1 stage (`AGENTS.md`), the open question is whether its *content* targets this specifically (see the open question below); (c) scale itself (the already-planned 350M/500M post-V1 checks) -- the honest ceiling neither of the first two levers can substitute for. **Architecture tweaks (GQA ratio, `head_dim`, partial RoPE -- [[MF-107]]/[[MF-108]]) are deliberately *not* in this list**: they are real and worth testing, but they affect efficiency/quality-per-FLOP at a given scale, not whether the model can do this *class* of multi-step/formal reasoning at all -- consistent with, not a new departure from, this project's own already-recorded conclusion elsewhere that data matters more than architecture at this scale.
  2. **A real eval-sensitivity gap, found while grounding this analysis in MiniFrontier's own actual numbers.** `reports/mf-overnight-eval/mf095-fineweb-only-harness-limit100.json` shows `gsm8k` exact-match at **0.0** for a real ~10M-token 150M checkpoint -- not a bug, a real floor. This means GSM8K (and likely several of the other exact-match end-task metrics at this budget) has **zero discriminating power** for comparing candidate small-scale recipes: two differently-mixed or differently-SFT'd 150M checkpoints at this token budget would very plausibly both score exactly 0.0, making the comparison uninformative regardless of which recipe is actually better. The project's own established practice elsewhere (held-out cross-entropy/BPB, not exact-match, for every [[MF-095]]/[[MF-097]]/[[MF-100]]-class comparison) already avoids this exact trap for its other comparisons -- this is the same principle, not yet applied to the reasoning/math axis specifically.
- **Acceptance:** N/A for the analysis itself (recorded, not implemented). If pursued: a real held-out CE/BPB metric computed specifically on reasoning/math-flavored text (e.g. FineMath's own held-out split, or GSM8K's questions-plus-reference-solutions scored as plain text rather than via exact-match generation) would give a continuous, sensitive small-scale proxy signal for comparing mixture/SFT candidates *before* they're anywhere near good enough to move an exact-match end-task metric off the floor -- exact-match tasks (GSM8K, etc.) would then serve as an eventual milestone/floor-check at [[MF-070]]'s larger real scale, not as the small-scale comparison signal itself.
- **Open scope question, resolved (2026-09-12): yes, the required SFT stage's data should explicitly include step-by-step reasoning demonstrations, not stay generic instruction-following.** Checked directly what currently exists: the only real SFT dataset this project has (`artifacts/mf066-tmp/sft_dataset.jsonl`, 32 examples, CC0-1.0) is genuinely trivial, plain instruction-following (`"Reply with exactly the word RED"` -> `"RED"`), confirming there is real, current work needed here, not just a hypothetical gap. This decision does not require or wait for [[MF-098]]'s bigger post-070 distillation effort -- see [[MF-123]] for the real, scoped sourcing task this decision creates.
- **Status note:** raised 2026-09-12 directly from the user's own question ("for a model to be good it must do those 4 well... how should we improve our modern model on those, top 3 ways") after the real 4-model comparison exercise above. The eval-sensitivity gap finding was resolved separately as already-exercised-in-practice (the real math-CE checks run during [[MF-095]]'s own mixture comparison already served this exact purpose) -- no separate formalized utility was judged necessary. The SFT-content question above was the one concrete, actionable piece; it's now decided and handed off to [[MF-123]].

### MF-117 — Pre-rental hardware re-validation checklist: today's tuned defaults are RTX 2070 Super-specific, not universal

- **Priority / state:** P1 / Not started -- directly gates efficient use of any rented compute for [[MF-070]]'s real run
- **Depends on:** [[MF-101]] (batch-size/gradient-accumulation sweep, already Done -- but tuned to this specific card)
- **Deliverable:** every hardware-tuned default this project has established so far was measured on one specific card (RTX 2070 Super, Turing, 8GB, no native BF16 Tensor Cores) and is real, trustworthy evidence *for that card* -- not necessarily for whatever GPU eventually gets rented for [[MF-070]]'s real run. Four settings need re-measurement on the actual rented hardware before trusting them there, not carried over by assumption:
  1. **`batch_size=2`** ([[MF-101]]): chosen specifically because `batch_size=4` catastrophically thrashes this card's 8GB VRAM ceiling. A rented GPU with meaningfully more VRAM (any modern datacenter card) would very likely support a much larger batch size efficiently -- reusing `batch_size=2` there would leave real throughput on the table, not play it safe.
  2. **FP16+GradScaler over BF16** (`AGENTS.md`'s own hardware-aware precision rule, `reports/mf049-rtx2070s-checkpointing-benchmark.md`): chosen because Turing has no native BF16 Tensor Cores. Any Ampere+ rented card *does* have native BF16 -- `AGENTS.md`'s own rule (`torch.cuda.is_bf16_supported(including_emulation=False)`) already says to check per-device and use native BF16 where it's real, so this isn't even a new test, just applying the existing rule's other branch correctly on the new hardware.
  3. **`torch.compile` measured unhelpful** (`reports/mf050-rtx2070s-profile-matrix.md`): specifically because Inductor declines native BF16 compilation on this card, and even where it does compile (FlexAttention), it was measured *slower*. An Ampere+/Hopper rented card has full Inductor BF16 support and much better-tuned Triton kernels for that hardware generation -- this finding very plausibly does not transfer, and could go the other way entirely.
  4. **Learning-rate/warmup-updates retuning for a larger effective batch size** -- [[MF-101]]'s own status note already named this as an explicitly deferred follow-up once the batch-size question was settled ("`learning_rate`/`warmup_updates` retuning is explicitly deferred until after this sweep settles the batch-size question"). If a rented GPU makes a genuinely larger batch size viable (per item 1 above), this deferred retuning becomes necessary, not optional -- LR needs to scale with effective batch size by standard practice, not stay fixed at a value tuned for `batch_size=2`.
- **Acceptance:** before committing the bulk of any rented compute budget to [[MF-070]]'s real training run, a real, short, cheap re-measurement of all four items above runs *on the actual rented hardware* (same bounded-comparison discipline as every other measurement this project has made -- real numbers, not assumptions carried over from the RTX 2070 Super). Each of the four either confirms the existing default transfers, or produces a new, hardware-specific default for that rented card -- either outcome is a real, recorded result, not a guess either way.
- **Status note:** raised 2026-09-12 directly from the user's own question, "what will move the needle at the same scale before we scale larger, so that when I rent I know it's efficient." This is the direct, practical answer: not a new architecture or data lever, but making sure this project's own existing, real, hardware-specific findings don't get silently misapplied to different hardware where the underlying reason for each finding (VRAM ceiling, missing Tensor Core generation, Inductor/Triton support) may not hold at all. Cheap to check, real risk of wasted rented budget if skipped.

- **Priority / state:** Dropped (2026-09-10) — see status note
- **Depends on:** none structurally; would have fed [[MF-095]]'s real mixture comparison and [[MF-070]]'s actual code component
- **Deliverable (as originally scoped, not pursued):** a real pipeline that resolves `HuggingFaceTB/stack-edu`'s blob metadata (`repo_name`, `path`, `blob_id`, `license_type`, etc. -- confirmed via its real HF schema, no code text in the dataset itself) into actual source-file content by fetching each blob from Software Heritage's real S3 object store (`boto3`, real auth/credentials, gzip decompression per blob), then feeding the result through this project's existing `Document.create`/license-gate machinery (`PERMISSIVE_CODE_LICENSES`, already enforced for `source_type="code"`) using each blob's own real per-file `license_type`, not one blanket dataset license.
- **Status note:** split out 2026-09-10 from [[MF-095]]'s own data-sourcing work, at the point [[MF-095]] discovered Stack-Edu's real schema holds no code text -- verified directly against the dataset's actual HuggingFace page before recording, not assumed from FineWeb-Edu/DCLM-Edu/FineMath's simpler direct-`streaming=True` shape (all three of those are real, schema-verified, and already implemented; Stack-Edu was the one real outlier). Briefly raised to P1/blocking, then **dropped entirely (2026-09-10) once the real access requirement was verified**: fetching real Stack-Edu content requires a credentialed AWS account (`AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID`/`AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY`), confirmed directly against BigCode's own official `the-stack-v2` usage example -- every official code path signs requests with a real account, despite the bucket being nominally "public." **The user explicitly does not want this project's data pipeline to ever require paid/credentialed cloud access for anyone to reproduce it** ("I want users to be able to use my repo for free"), so this is a real, permanent design-values decision, not a scheduling deferral. See [[MF-095]]'s 2026-09-10 status note for the replacement plan (expanding this project's own curated code corpus, plus researching what comparable free small-model projects use for code data instead).

### MF-098 — Post-training depth: preference optimization (DPO-style) and offline distillation from a stronger teacher

- **Priority / state:** P3 / Not started — needs an explicit scope decision before any implementation
- **Depends on:** a trained base model worth post-training seriously (practically, this follows MF-070's real 350M run, not the current reduced-budget 150M checkpoints)
- **Deliverable:** **not yet scoped as an implementation task, only as a decision to make.** The candidate additions are (1) a DPO-style (or IPO-style) preference-optimization stage on top of the existing assistant-only SFT loss — single-GPU-friendly, no environment/rollout loop, a modified pairwise loss over (chosen, rejected) response pairs; (2) offline distillation, using a larger third-party teacher model's outputs (explanations, corrected solutions, FIM completions) as additional SFT-style training signal, without training the teacher.
- **Acceptance:** N/A until scoped. If approved, a real acceptance criterion needs its own task, following this project's existing SFT-evaluation pattern ([[MF-061]]).
- **Scope framing (2026-09-08), explicitly deferred until after [[MF-070]] by the user's own request — recorded now so the decision doesn't have to be re-derived later.** The realistic target for distillation here is narrow specialization, not general competition: a 350M-1B from-scratch model will not match strong ≤7B models on open-ended knowledge, multi-step reasoning, long-context agentic work, or general chat quality, broadly speaking. On narrow, high-value tasks (the domains this project already cares about — explanations, corrected solutions, FIM, code) distillation from a stronger teacher's outputs is a real, commonly-used way for small models to punch above their weight, and is one of the higher-ROI levers available at this project's actual hardware scale (no need to train the teacher, only to use its outputs as training signal). This does not change the task's own scope-decision requirement above (RL-adjacency, provenance/licensing of teacher outputs) — it only records why the user wants this revisited seriously once a real 350M checkpoint exists, rather than treating it as a nice-to-have footnote.
- **Status note:** raised by a fourth round of external feedback, framed as "the biggest missing piece for a useful chat/coding model." **This needs an explicit scope decision, not silent adoption, because it sits right next to a frozen V1 exclusion.** `AGENTS.md`'s frozen V1 scope explicitly excludes "RL/GRPO" — DPO itself is not classically RL (no environment, no policy rollout, no reward model interaction; it is a supervised contrastive loss over static preference pairs, algorithmically closer to a modified SFT loss than to GRPO), so a case exists that it does not violate the exclusion as written — but this is exactly the kind of interpretation `AGENTS.md`'s own governance says must be "recorded as a decision before implementation," not assumed. Distillation from a third-party teacher's outputs also raises this project's own data-provenance requirements ([[MF-006]]'s licensing policy, the code-corpus manifest discipline already enforced elsewhere) — whatever teacher and prompts are used would need the same license/provenance accounting as any other training data source, which has not been thought through here. Not started; not to be started without the user's explicit go-ahead on both the RL-adjacency question and the practical scope (which teacher, what data, what license).
- **Further evidence recorded (2026-09-08), explicitly deferred to after [[MF-070]] by the user's own request.** Concrete real-world precedents cited for why distillation + synthetic data + focused post-training (not pure from-scratch pretraining) is what actually makes small models competitive in the 0.5B-7B range people run as GGUF: DeepSeek-R1's distilled 1.5B/7B students retaining a large share of the teacher's reasoning ability on math/code benchmarks; the Phi family's reliance on synthetic "textbook-quality" data as one of the clearest public examples of curation letting small models beat larger ones trained on noisier data; the SmolLM family's Cosmopedia-style synthetic data plus specialized math/code mixtures (already a real, existing candidate source for [[MF-094]]'s own mixture proposal); and a cited (not independently verified here) claim that a carefully filtered 20k-example synthetic set beat a much larger noisy set by 20+ points on a target task — consistent with this project's own "every token needs to be better" framing for [[MF-094]].
