    - Matrix-preconditioned optimizers (Muon and variants): [[MF-057]]/[[MF-070]]'s own pre-work found AdamW beating Muon's best real wall-clock-matched result (val PPL 90.4 vs 95.9), even after a real ~21% Muon speedup from an FP32 Newton-Schulz fix -- the literature's claimed ~1.2-1.4x edge did not transfer on this project's own reference Turing GPU (no native BF16 tensor cores, which Muon's internals lean on).
    - Shorter local-attention windows: [[MF-070]]'s own corrected re-test (128/256/512, genuinely `< sequence_length`) found validation PPL flat (170.5-171.8) -- no measurable quality effect at this scale, contradicting the general "shorter windows help" framing.
    - MTP for both training quality and inference speedup: already implemented, tested, and adopted for exactly this reasoning ([[MF-093]]/[[MF-105]] -- training kept the heads specifically because the same trained heads also give a real self-speculative-decoding win, not just the small training-time quality edge alone).
    - Batch-size/accumulation tuning: [[MF-101]]'s real sweep already found a free throughput win from raising `--accumulation-steps` -- though only in that direction, not toward smaller batch sizes (see the genuinely-new item below for the gap this leaves).
  - **Explicitly out of frozen V1 scope regardless of external merit:** production serving / continuous-batching frameworks -- `AGENTS.md` already bans vLLM/SGLang/etc. from the core.
  - **Inapplicable to the actual reference hardware, not pursued further:** FP8 attention and FlashAttention-3 both require Hopper/Ada-class tensor cores; the project's real dev GPU (RTX 2070 Super) is Turing, with neither. Only relevant if/when a future 350M+ run moves to rented hardware, which [[MF-117]]'s own re-validation gate already covers.
  - **Four items have no prior record in this project at all**, recorded here so they aren't lost, not because any is judged promising yet:
    1. **Iterative / dynamic data-mixture reweighting** (CLIMB-style clustering-based bootstrapping, or simpler quality-ordered curricula) -- a real change in kind from the current fixed-weight static mixture ([[MF-094]]/[[MF-095]]), not just a weight-tuning pass. [[MF-095]]'s own decay-phase curriculum (different data in WSD's stable vs. decay phase) is the closest existing precedent but is still a one-time, pre-decided split, not an iterative/model-driven reweighting loop. A closely related lead from the same feedback -- model-based data selection / coreset methods, and quality filtering beyond this project's existing dedup+license admission gate -- belongs in the same future scoping pass, since both are about *which* tokens get trained on rather than architecture or optimizer choice.
    2. **Small-batch training as a first-class methodology, not just an accumulation-steps sweep**: a literature claim that small (even `batch_size=1`) training can match or beat large-batch-plus-accumulation per real FLOP, given correct Adam hyperparameter rescaling (a "hold the second-moment half-life constant in tokens, not steps" rule as batch size changes). [[MF-101]]'s own real sweep only tested raising `--accumulation-steps` at the existing `batch_size=2` floor -- lowering batch size, and the specific hyperparameter-rescaling rule this claim depends on, are both untested here. SOAP (a second matrix-preconditioned optimizer, alongside the already-extensively-tested Muon) is a related, also-untested lead from the same list item.
    3. **Memory-efficient full-fine-tune optimizers** (GaLore/APOLLO/SCALE-style low-rank or column-norm gradient/optimizer-state compression) -- relevant once a real continued-pretrain or SFT stage ([[MF-098]]/[[MF-123]]) actually starts on this project's own 8GB reference card, not for base pretraining itself, where AdamW's memory footprint is already known and budgeted.
    4. **Quantized / paged KV cache** -- the project's own ring KV cache already exists and is measured (~3.4x savings, [[MF-050]]), but at full precision; quantizing cached K/V and/or paging it is a distinct, untested further optimization, relevant to inference throughput/context-length headroom specifically, not training.
- **Acceptance:** N/A until scoped -- matching [[MF-133]]'s own bar, each needs a real primary-source read (paper or reference implementation, not a secondary summary) before any bounded-ablation slot is committed.
- **Status note:** not scheduled; [[MF-070]]'s real 3B-token run remains the current priority and none of these block it. Recorded per `AGENTS.md`'s "add newly discovered work to the backlog instead of hiding it" rule, at the user's own direct request to record what's needed from this feedback and check whether new tasks are warranted.
- **Primary sources identified and independently verified (2026-09-15), closing this task's own "no primary source read yet" gap for all four leads.** A follow-up from the same external reviewer supplied concrete arXiv citations and per-lead ablation protocols; each was fetched and checked directly against its own real abstract (not accepted on the reviewer's paraphrase alone) -- all four confirmed accurate, no misrepresentation found:
  1. **Dynamic data-mixture reweighting: CLIMB / Nemotron-CLIMB, Diao et al., arXiv:2504.13161 (NeurIPS 2025).** Confirmed: embed corpus -> hierarchical cluster -> sample candidate mixture-weight vectors -> train small proxy models on each -> fit a lightweight regressor predicting downstream performance from weights -> iterate. Real reported result: a 1B model trained on their resulting `ClimbMix` (400B tokens, 20 clusters) beat Llama-3.2-1B by 2.0% on a 12-task suite; domain-targeted optimization gave ~5% over random sampling. **Suggested bounded protocol**: baseline = current static 5-source mix; ablation = 1-2 rounds of cluster-based reweighting (embed existing shards, cluster, score by validation loss or a cheap proxy, re-weight sampling once or twice), same token budget, compare final val PPL -- a simplified one-shot version of the full iterative search, not the full CLIMB pipeline.
  2. **Small-batch training + β2 token-half-life scaling: Marek et al., arXiv:2507.07101 (NeurIPS 2025), code at github.com/martin-marek/batch-size.** Confirmed: conventional "small batches are unstable" is largely an artifact of not rescaling Adam's β2 as batch size changes; the fix holds the second-moment's *token* half-life fixed rather than the raw β2 value, letting `batch_size=1` train stably, more hyperparameter-robust, and competitive-or-better per FLOP -- directly contradicting [[MF-101]]'s implicit assumption (never tested lower than `batch_size=2`) that larger effective batch via accumulation is the only lever worth sweeping. Paper explicitly argues gradient accumulation is usually wasteful outside multi-replica bandwidth-limited settings. **Suggested bounded protocol**: AdamW with correctly β2-rescaled micro-batches (1-4) vs the current `batch_size=2` baseline, same token budget, val PPL vs wall-clock -- same MF-101-style methodology, one new axis (batch size down, not accumulation up).
  3. **SOAP optimizer: Vyas et al., arXiv:2409.11321.** Confirmed: SOAP runs Adam inside the eigenbasis of a Shampoo-style Kronecker-factored preconditioner, updating that basis only infrequently (one added hyperparameter: preconditioning frequency) -- mathematically, "Shampoo is equivalent to running Adafactor in the eigenbasis of Shampoo's preconditioner." Real reported result (360M/660M models, large-batch regime): 40% fewer iterations and 35% less wall-clock time than AdamW, 20% better than plain Shampoo on both. **Suggested bounded protocol**: SOAP vs this project's own already-extensively-tested Muon/AdamW baselines ([[MF-057]]/[[MF-070]]'s pre-work), same token budget -- explicitly lower priority than lead 2, and only after the β2 rule above is settled (per the reviewer's own suggested order).
  4. **Memory-efficient full-FT optimizer for a future SFT stage: SCALE, Glentis et al., arXiv:2506.16659 (ICML 2026).** Confirmed: two cheap ingredients -- column-wise gradient normalization (along the output dimension), plus first-order momentum applied *only* to the last/output layer (where gradient variance is highest), everywhere else just the normalized gradient. Real reported result: matches or exceeds Adam's quality at 35-45% of Adam's memory; beats GaLore/Fira/APOLLO at 60M-1B scale, and beats APOLLO/Muon on LLaMA-7B (both perplexity and memory). **Suggested bounded protocol**: SFT-stage-only comparison (AdamW full-FT vs SCALE full-FT vs LoRA), same token budget/sequence length, quality + peak VRAM -- explicitly parked until a real SFT/continued-pretrain stage actually begins ([[MF-098]]/[[MF-123]]), not for base pretraining.
  5. **Quantized/paged KV cache: not one paper but a real, named landscape** (PagedAttention/vLLM's block allocator; KIVI's asymmetric INT4 -- per-channel keys, per-token values -- as the practical 2024 baseline; 2025-2026 refinements: Kitty, HyQuant, various FP8/INT4/mixed schemes) -- not independently fetched/verified per-paper (unlike 1-4 above, this is a landscape summary, not one falsifiable primary-source claim), but internally consistent with what's already known to be true here: the project's own ring KV cache exists and is measured (~3.4x savings, [[MF-050]]) at full precision only, with no quantization layer yet. **Suggested bounded protocol**: inference-only, same checkpoint, FP16 KV baseline vs FP8 vs INT4 (KIVI-style if easy on this project's actual PyTorch/llama.cpp backends), reporting peak memory, tokens/s, and a small quality check (PPL + a couple of generation samples) -- explicitly a pure inference change, no retraining needed.
  **Reviewer's own suggested priority order, recorded as their opinion, not yet adopted as this project's own decision**: (1) small-batch β2 scaling -- lowest effort, most directly addresses this project's own real 8GB constraint; (2) quantized/paged KV -- pure inference win, no retraining; (3) simplified cluster-based mixture reweighting -- highest quality upside, highest effort; (4) SCALE -- parked until the SFT stage exists; (5) SOAP -- lowest priority, nice-to-have matrix-optimizer comparison after the β2 rule is settled.
  **Independent second-opinion corroboration (2026-09-17)**: a separate external review, given only the repo/README (no access to this backlog), reached the identical three-lever prioritization independently -- data mixture/selection, optimizer+batch details (specifically naming the same token-half-life β2 rule), and KV-cache quantization, in the same order, plus explicit confirmation that PyTorch SDPA+cuBLAS is "already good enough" at this scale/hardware and custom kernels would be premature. No new information -- recorded because independent convergence on the same three levers and the same ordering, from a cold read with none of this project's own reasoning available, is itself real, if modest, evidence the prioritization is sound, not an artifact of this project's own framing.
  **Still not scoped or started** -- this pass only closes the "primary source" gap the original acceptance criterion required; actually committing GPU time to any of these five (rather than [[MF-070]]'s own real, currently-running 3B-token release run) remains an open decision for whenever that's the priority, not implied by recording this briefing.

### The published mixture dataset is directly usable to train *other* base models too (2026-09-15 discovery), documented, not a MiniFrontier scope change

User found, and directly verified as accurate, that `igalk474/MiniFrontier-150M-Modern-3B-token-mixture` ([[MF-126]]'s own dataset publish) is not tied to MiniFrontier's own training loop at all -- `datasets.load_dataset` hands any HF-ecosystem trainer the same raw `text` column, so the mixture works as ordinary continued-pretraining/LoRA data for a *different* small open base model (e.g. `HuggingFaceTB/SmolLM2-135M`, a close size match; `Qwen/Qwen2.5-0.5B` for more VRAM), via `unsloth`/`transformers`+`peft`+`trl`, then llama.cpp's `convert_hf_to_gguf.py` + `quantize` + `llama-server` for local serving. **This is exactly the payoff the Parquet-export design already claimed for itself** (`README.md`'s own "why the upstream sources are Parquet" note: "raw, untokenized text plus metadata columns doesn't commit to any one consumer's tokenizer" -- written as a design rationale before this session, now demonstrated as a real, working use case rather than just an intended one).

**Documented in `README.md`'s "Published dataset" section** (a new bullet under "Use it to train") rather than opened as a new MF-task: PEFT/TRL/Unsloth are explicitly barred from MiniFrontier's own core (`AGENTS.md`), and nothing about this changes that -- it is downstream use of the *published dataset* by external tooling, not a change to this project's own raw-PyTorch training loop or scope. No code added to this repo; the workflow (install, load base + dataset, LoRA/continued-pretrain, merge, convert to GGUF, serve) lives entirely outside it.

### External feedback on the live MF-070 run's effective batch size (2026-09-16): checked against the actual code, not applied

Feedback on the currently-running real 3B-token release run argued `--batch-size 2`/`--accumulation-steps 1` (effective batch 2) is too small for stable/high-quality pretraining and recommended raising `--accumulation-steps` to 4-8, since accumulation is "almost free" on VRAM. Checked directly against the real code before acting on it, matching this project's own established discipline -- **not applied to the live run**, for reasons that are new findings worth keeping, not just a restatement of the existing decision:

1. **It cannot be applied to the live run via `--resume` at all -- confirmed, it would hard-crash.** `train/pretrain.py`'s resume path compares the *entire* freshly-built `TrainingConfig` against the checkpoint's stored one via `asdict()` equality (`if trainer_values.get("training_config") != asdict(train_config): raise ValueError(...)`, `pretrain.py:356-357`). `TrainingConfig.gradient_accumulation_steps` and `max_updates` are both fields in that struct -- changing `--accumulation-steps` between the original launch and a later `--resume` trips this check immediately. No supported path exists to change it mid-run; only a fresh run from scratch could use a different value. (`batch_size` itself is *not* a `TrainingConfig` field and is not covered by this check -- CLI/provider-only -- but that wasn't the feedback's own suggested lever.)
2. **Applying it to a *fresh* run exactly as suggested would silently redefine the token budget by ~8x, not just the batch shape -- a real trap the feedback itself never flagged.** One "update" (what `--updates 732422` counts) consumes `batch_size x accumulation_steps x sequence_length` tokens (`training.py`'s `train_updates()`: one loop body pulls `gradient_accumulation_steps` micro-batches per update; `state.consumed_target_tokens` increments by the whole accumulated batch's tokens each update). Raising `--accumulation-steps` 1->8 while leaving `--updates 732422` unchanged -- exactly what the feedback's own example showed, no mention of adjusting `--updates` -- would take the run from the frozen 3B-token budget to ~24B tokens, and from an already-long ~17.75-day estimate to roughly **142 days**. WSD's own decay window is a pure fraction of `max_updates` (`wsd_decay_start_update`, `training.py:458-473`), not of tokens, so this also silently changes the stable-vs-decay-phase balance, not just "more data."
3. **The quality argument itself is contradicted, not just untested, by a source this project already vetted the same day**: [[MF-135]]'s own verified primary source (Marek et al., arXiv:2507.07101) found "small batches are unstable" is largely an artifact of *not rescaling Adam's β2* for batch size -- correctly-scaled small batches (even `batch_size=1`) are stable and competitive-or-better per FLOP, with gradient accumulation "usually wasteful unless bandwidth-limited across multiple replicas." The external feedback's generic "bigger effective batch = more stable" framing is the opposite of this project's own already-sourced evidence, not merely unverified.
4. **User-confirmed: this exact recipe's batch/accumulation choice was already a deliberate, tested decision, not an oversight** -- [[MF-070]]'s own entry already states `batch_size=2`/`accumulation_steps=1` is "the exact real-measured-safe combination, not [[MF-101]]'s untested-at-this-recipe 8/32," specifically because MF-101's own real throughput win from raising accumulation was measured *without* `head_dim=96`+`--activation-checkpointing`+MTP all active together -- and the live run is already at 94% VRAM utilization (7.7/8.2GB) on this combined recipe, leaving real risk in assuming a bigger effective batch transfers cleanly here untested.

**No change made to the live run.** Folded into [[MF-135]]'s existing small-batch/β2-scaling/SOAP lead as context for that future bounded ablation (test the β2-token-half-life rule properly, on a fresh small-budget run with `--updates` correctly rescaled to hold the token budget constant -- not "just raise accumulation-steps" on a live release run), not opened as a new task.

### MF-136 — Wire periodic in-loop validation and print z-loss/MTP-loss alongside the training loss, for the *next* release run

- **Priority / state:** P2 / Done (2026-09-16)
- **Depends on:** none structurally; motivated directly by the live [[MF-070]] run's own missing signal, discussed while it was running
- **Deliverable:** two real, previously-missing gaps found while discussing the live run's own loss signal, both fixed. Neither could be applied to the already-running [[MF-070]] release run (editing source doesn't touch an already-running process, but `--resume` requires exact `TrainingConfig` equality -- see the entry above -- so neither takes effect there without a restart, deliberately not done); both are real, tested, and available starting with the next real launch.
  1. **z-loss and the MTP auxiliary loss were already computed every single update (`training.py`) but silently discarded -- never retained on `TrainingState`, never printed.** Added `TrainingState.last_z_loss`/`last_mtp_loss` (both `float | None`, `None` when not configured rather than a misleading 0.0), tracked via new `detached_z_loss_sum`/`detached_mtp_loss_sum` accumulators mirroring the existing `detached_loss_sum` pattern, finalized the same way `last_loss` already is. `last_z_loss` is already weight-baked (matches what `chunked_next_token_loss_stats` returns); `last_mtp_loss` is deliberately the RAW summed MTP loss, *before* `config.mtp_loss_weight` is applied, so it stays comparable across runs using different weight settings -- documented directly in `TrainingState`'s own field comments so the asymmetry isn't a surprise later. `train/pretrain.py`'s progress-print line now appends `z_loss=`/`mtp_loss=` only when each is actually configured -- a plain run's log line is byte-identical to before (confirmed by the fast suite's existing `"1/2 updates"` substring check still passing unmodified).
  2. **Real gap found reading the code, not assumed: `TrainingConfig.validation_interval` and `train_updates`'s own `validation_fn` callback were already fully implemented, but `train/pretrain.py` -- the only entry point any real run has ever used -- never constructed a `validation_fn` or exposed any CLI flag for it, and never even passed `validation_interval` into its own `TrainingConfig(...)` call.** This is why every prior real validation (e.g. [[MF-065]]'s) was always a separate, after-the-fact `scripts/eval.py`/`scripts/eval_checkpoint.py` pass, not a deliberate design choice to keep it that way. Fixed: `--validation-interval` (requires `--validation-shards`, fails closed with a clear `ValueError` if only one is given, matching this file's existing `--decay-mixture requires --mixture` convention) and repeatable `--validation-shards NAME;SHARDS_PATH` (mirrors `--mixture`'s own syntax). Each source is evaluated separately (reusing `evaluation/validation.py`'s existing `batches_from_packed_shards`/`evaluate_token_batches` -- the same real, weighted CE/PPL/BPB recipe [[MF-086]] already established and `scripts/eval_checkpoint.py` already reuses) and printed individually plus one token-weighted combined figure, matching this project's own real mixture-training shape rather than assuming one pool. `--tokenizer` (default `data/tokenizer`) is only loaded when `--validation-shards` is actually given -- BPB needs it to decode packed tokens back to real UTF-8 bytes.
  - **Real, deliberate cost-control design, corrected once during review (2026-09-16, user caught it): `--validation-batch-size` and `--validation-max-batches` are not the same lever, and the first draft left the actual cost lever unbounded.** `--validation-batch-size` (default 4, not `eval_checkpoint.py`'s own 8 -- deliberately more conservative since this runs in-process, sequentially, on whatever VRAM training's own weights/optimizer state have left over, not independently measured on real tight-VRAM hardware) only controls per-forward-pass parallelism. **`--validation-max-batches` is the real total-cost lever** (total validation tokens per check ≈ `validation_batch_size x validation_max_batches x sequence_length`) -- the first draft left this `None` (unbounded, full held-out pool every check), which on this project's own real 5-source mixture (tens of millions of validation tokens per source) could cost a real, undisclosed chunk of an hour *per check*. Fixed: default changed to a small 20-batch "canary" sample, not the full pool -- the real, full-precision final validation still belongs to a separate `scripts/eval_checkpoint.py` pass (unaffected by this default, no cap there), matching the same before/after-training split this project has always used, just adding a cheap in-loop signal on top rather than replacing it. `--validation-interval` documented as entirely independent of `--progress-interval` (the plain-loss log line) -- no reason to set them equal, and a real validation pass costs meaningfully more per call than one log line.
  - **Real, honestly-disclosed caveat, not yet independently measured**: whether `--validation-batch-size 4` (or any value) is actually VRAM-safe when run in-process on a recipe already near its VRAM ceiling (the live [[MF-070]] run sits at ~89-94% utilization) has not been profiled -- this needs a real throwaway diagnostic probe before ever combining `--validation-interval` with a real production launch on tight-VRAM hardware, matching this project's own established "measure before trusting" discipline (e.g. [[MF-108]]'s own head_dim VRAM diagnostics), not assumed safe from the conservative default alone.
- **Acceptance:** 7 new tests, all CPU-only (no GPU touched, verified safe to run alongside the live [[MF-070]] GPU process): `test_train_updates_reports_last_z_loss_and_last_mtp_loss_only_when_configured` (`tests/test_training.py`, narrow unit coverage: both fields None on a plain run, real finite floats when configured, independently of each other); `tests/test_pretrain_cli.py` gains `test_z_loss_and_mtp_loss_are_printed_in_progress_lines_only_when_configured` (plain run's log line has neither substring; a configured run has both), `test_validation_interval_and_validation_shards_require_each_other` (both pairing-violation directions raise), `test_validation_interval_runs_real_periodic_validation_and_prints_metrics` (a real functional run through `pretrain.run()` end to end, confirms `[validation @ update N]`/`combined: ce=` actually appear at both configured checkpoints), `test_validation_multi_source_reports_each_source_and_a_combined_figure` (two distinct named validation sources, both appear individually plus a combined figure), `test_validation_shards_duplicate_names_are_rejected`. Full fast suite: 562 passed, `ruff check`/`format --check` clean on all four changed files.
- **Status note:** Done. Not applied to the live run (deliberately, per the user's own explicit "don't rerun it" preference) -- available starting with the next real `train/pretrain.py` launch.

### MF-137 — Real architecture/recipe comparison against Qwen3-0.6B-Base, Qwen2.5-0.5B, SmolLM2-360M/135M, and SmolLM2's real SFT recipes (2026-09-16)

- **Priority / state:** P3 / Not started -- real, sourced comparison findings, none yet scoped as a bounded ablation
- **Depends on:** none structurally for the architecture items (would join [[MF-081]]'s Modern-only bounded-ablation slot if ever scoped, same as [[MF-133]]); the SFT items depend on [[MF-116]]/[[MF-123]] (SFT getting real attention at all)
- **Deliverable:** user-requested direct research pass ("look at those models and internals... maybe we will have insights") across four real Hub model configs, `xai-org/grok-1`/`grok-build` (grok-1 findings recorded on [[MF-133]] instead, since they map directly onto that task's existing item 2), and SmolLM2's real alignment-handbook SFT/DPO recipes plus the smollm pretraining-mixture writeup -- three parallel research passes, each cross-checked against this project's own already-decided/already-tested state before recording anything, not accepted at face value:
  1. **RoPE theta = 10,000: real correction (2026-09-16), found by reading Qwen2.5/Qwen3's actual technical reports rather than just their released `config.json`.** The initial framing here (config.json shows 1,000,000 for both Qwen models -- 100x MiniFrontier's frozen default) mischaracterized what that number means: both reports state, near-verbatim identically, that RoPE base frequency is raised from 10,000 to 1,000,000 via the ABF technique **during a separate, later long-context-extension fine-tuning stage** -- their actual base-pretraining theta is 10,000, the *same* value MiniFrontier already uses. **The real, still-open idea is narrower and different in kind than "raise the base theta"**: a short, dedicated RoPE-ABF context-extension stage after base pretraining, a distinct technique MiniFrontier doesn't do at all (fixed, single-stage `max_seq_len` training) -- but even this has limited motivation here, since these real models' own extension stages target 32K+ context, far past MiniFrontier's own 1024-2048. **SmolLM2 is the one real, unexplained exception that survives this correction**: its actual deployed `config.json` uses `rope_theta=100,000` with no stated single-stage-vs-extension rationale anywhere in its own paper (whose own Table 6 claims theta=10,000, inconsistent with its own released config -- an error in SmolLM2's own documentation, not independent evidence of anything). This project's own already-tested `global_rope_theta` item ([[MF-081]]-adjacent, a local-vs-global split at a fixed base theta) remains a separate, different axis, unaffected by this correction.
  2. **GQA ratio has only been tested going *more* aggressive than the frozen 3:1 baseline.** [[MF-081]]'s real sweep covered 6:1 (neutral) and 12:1 (small real regression) -- never a *less* aggressive ratio. Qwen3-0.6B, a real, heavily-optimized 2025 model, uses 2:1 (16 Q / 8 KV heads) -- the opposite, untested direction on the quality-vs-KV-cache-savings tradeoff.
  3. **Embedding/output multiplier scaling (muP-style), found in `grok-1`'s own code, not yet cross-checked against this project's existing init-time scaling work.** `input_embeddings *= embedding_multiplier_scale` and `out *= output_multiplier_scale` -- fixed scalar multipliers applied at the embedding/final-logit layers, at forward-pass time, distinct in kind from `residual_std_damping`'s init-time weight-scaling (already adopted here). Flagged as a genuinely new, unverified lead pending a real check against whether it's already functionally equivalent to something [[MF-081]]/[[MF-092]] already covered -- not yet confirmed either way.
  4. **Real, concrete gaps in `train/sft.py`'s own current default hyperparameters, found by reading the code directly and comparing against SmolLM2's own real, working SFT recipe** (`alignment-handbook/recipes/smollm2/sft/config_smol.yaml`, directly comparable scale): SmolLM2 uses LR `1e-3`, effective batch 16 (batch 4 x grad-accum 4), warmup ratio 0.1, cosine schedule. MiniFrontier's `train/sft.py` defaults to LR `1e-5` (100x lower, never bounded-tested either way), **has no gradient-accumulation wiring at all** -- `TrainingConfig.gradient_accumulation_steps` exists and is already wired into `train/pretrain.py`, but `sft.py` never passes it, not even a hardcoded value, so SFT's effective batch is stuck at whatever `--batch-size` alone gives -- and defaults `--warmup-updates` to `0`, no ramp at all onto a freshly-loaded pretrained checkpoint. The missing-grad-accumulation gap is structurally the same class of previously-invisible CLI-wiring gap [[MF-136]] just found and fixed for `pretrain.py`'s validation path -- same fix shape (add `--accumulation-steps`, thread through `TrainingConfig`) would directly apply here too, whenever SFT gets real attention.
  - **Confirmed, not new, recorded for completeness rather than as findings**: MiniFrontier's own SwiGLU FFN ratio (2.67-2.75) already matches SmolLM2's exact 2.67 (classic 8/3 scaling) -- Qwen2.5-0.5B's own 5.43 is the real outlier among the four, not a convention worth chasing. `head_dim` override well above the derived value (MiniFrontier 64->96, [[MF-108]]) has real independent corroboration -- Qwen3 made the same *kind* of move for itself (64->128) -- reinforces an already-closed decision, doesn't reopen it. QK-Norm: MiniFrontier already has it on Modern; Qwen3 only just added it as new for itself -- already ahead, not behind. SmolLM2's own real pretraining writeup (arXiv:2502.02737) describes "a manual refinement process that updates the dataset mixing rates at each stage based on performance at the previous stage" -- real external precedent for the same idea already tracked as [[MF-135]] item 1 (dynamic/iterative mixture reweighting), corroborates rather than adds. SmolLM2's real, working DPO recipe (LR `1e-6`, beta 0.5) is solid external validation that [[MF-098]] (DPO/distillation, still not-started) is a standard, well-precedented next stage -- doesn't change its scope. Token budgets far past Chinchilla-optimal for both SmolLM2 (~14,800 tok/param) and Qwen3 (~60,000 tok/param) were already discussed and recorded elsewhere in this project's own backlog with these same two models named as precedent -- not new here.
  - **Qualitative follow-up (2026-09-16), reading past `config.json` into the models' own technical reports for attention-pattern and other named design choices, not just numeric comparison.** **MiniFrontier's own hybrid attention is already ahead of all four real comparables, confirmed, not a gap**: none of Qwen2.5-0.5B/1.5B, Qwen3-0.6B, or SmolLM2 use sliding-window or hybrid local/global attention at all -- all are full causal GQA throughout at these sizes, per their own technical reports. MiniFrontier's real 3-local/1-global FlexAttention hybrid ([[MF-041]]) is architecturally more sophisticated than any of these real small dense models actually ship. QK-Norm/bias-removal rationale in Qwen3's own report ("to ensure stable training") is the same generic stability framing already behind MiniFrontier's own [[MF-082]] adoption -- already matches, not new. SmolLM2's own paper states it inherits Llama2's architecture wholesale with no further architectural ablations beyond GQA and a 2k->8k context extension -- nothing further to extract there. **Net: this pass found one real correction (RoPE theta, above) and otherwise confirms MiniFrontier's existing architecture already matches or exceeds these real comparables -- no further new adoptable technique found.**
- **Acceptance:** N/A until scoped -- matching [[MF-133]]'s own established bar, each item needs a real primary-source read (already done for all four here, unlike some of [[MF-133]]'s own still-unsourced items) plus an explicit decision to commit a bounded-ablation slot before any of this touches real code, and the pretraining items (1-3) cannot touch the [[MF-070]] release run in progress either way.
- **Status note:** not scheduled; recorded per `AGENTS.md`'s "add newly discovered work to the backlog instead of hiding it" rule, at the user's own direct request to look for insights. [[MF-070]]'s real run remains the current priority for anything architecture-related; items 4 (SFT hyperparameters) are the more immediately actionable ones, since they don't require a new pretraining run to test -- worth prioritizing whenever [[MF-116]]/[[MF-123]] give SFT real attention.

### MF-138 — General checkpoint schema-evolution (exclude/rename) support, found from a full `grok-1` pipeline read

- **Priority / state:** P3 / Not started -- a real, actionable engineering idea, not yet scoped as a bounded task
- **Depends on:** none structurally; touches `checkpoint.py`'s existing `load_training_checkpoint`/`load_release`/`load_release_mtp_heads` (all currently `strict=True`)
- **Deliverable:** the user asked for a genuinely thorough pass across `xai-org/grok-1`'s *full* repository (not just `model.py`, already covered on [[MF-133]]/[[MF-137]]) plus a real, non-dismissive look at `grok-build`, since grok-1 is the one model in this whole research round with real, complete open-source pipeline code rather than just Hub-hosted weights.
  - **`grok-build` confirmed unrelated, with real evidence this time** (the first, narrow pass had dismissed it after only 3 tool calls total across both repos): it is xAI's terminal-based AI coding agent (Rust workspace, `crates/`/`Cargo.toml`, a TUI that edits files/runs shell commands/searches the web) -- nothing about model training, inference, checkpoints, or export. Confirmed, not assumed; contributes nothing.
  - **`grok-1` read properly this time**: `model.py`, `run.py`, `runners.py`, `checkpoint.py`, `tokenizer.model`, `requirements.txt` (JAX/dm-haiku/sentencepiece). Its own README states plainly: **inference-only, no training code** -- expected for a frontier lab's public release, confirmed rather than assumed.
  - **The real, new, actionable idea**: `checkpoint.py`'s `replace_with_load_state()` uses regex-based **exclude** and **rename** rules to let a checkpoint load against a *changed* parameter schema -- skip params no longer present in the code, remap renamed parameter paths -- rather than requiring an exact structural match. Checked directly against this project's own `checkpoint.py`: every real load path here (`load_training_checkpoint`, `load_release`, `load_release_mtp_heads`) uses `strict=True` with **no general, reusable partial-load mechanism** -- backward compatibility across this project's own real architecture changes (which has already happened repeatedly: MTP heads, EMA shadow weights, the head_dim override, gated attention) has always been handled ad hoc, one carve-out per feature (e.g. MTP heads simply live in their own optional `mtp_heads.safetensors` that may or may not exist), not through one general mechanism. As this project's own architecture keeps evolving, a general regex-based exclude/rename capability layered on top of the existing strict safetensors loading (never replacing the fail-closed default -- opt-in, explicit rules only) would be more robust than continuing to invent a new ad-hoc carve-out per future change.
  - **A smaller, related finding, worth folding into the same future task rather than a separate one**: grok-1 validates a checkpoint's real parameter set against the code's expected set *before* copying any tensors, raising one clear, named diff on mismatch. This project's own `strict=True` gets a similar fail-closed *outcome* today (safetensors itself errors on a key-set mismatch) but without as targeted a report of exactly what differs.
  - **Confirmed already-matched or already-exceeded, not gaps**: atomic/restart-safe checkpoint writes ([[MF-079]]/[[MF-130]]'s staged-then-atomic-rename work is more thorough than grok-1's own checkpoint code, which only addresses *read*-side distributed sharding, not write-side atomicity); sampling (grok-1's `runners.py` has plain top-p+temperature; this project's own [[MF-030]]/[[MF-091]] already cover a more complete set -- min-p, repetition control, a real degeneration metric); the KV-cache/prefill-then-decode generation loop shape (same fundamental design this project's own ring cache, [[MF-028]]/[[MF-029]]/[[MF-050]], already implements).
  - **Explicitly out of scope, noted only for completeness**: MoE (grok-1's actual core architecture, 8 experts/top-2) is already banned from MiniFrontier's core per `AGENTS.md`; multi-host/multi-GPU JAX mesh sharding and network-storage-contention checkpoint-read optimizations are distributed-cluster concerns with no single-consumer-GPU relevance here; `InferenceRunner`'s dynamic batching with free-slot recycling across concurrent requests is a continuous-batching serving pattern, already excluded elsewhere in this project's own scope (production serving frameworks stay out of the core).
- **Acceptance:** N/A until scoped -- would need a real design pass (what rule syntax, where rules are declared/versioned, a real regression test simulating a genuine past schema change like the MTP-heads addition) before implementation, not just porting grok-1's own regex-rule shape verbatim.
- **Status note:** not scheduled; recorded per `AGENTS.md`'s "add newly discovered work to the backlog instead of hiding it" rule. Purely a software-engineering robustness idea -- no GPU time, no bounded ablation, no interaction with the live [[MF-070]] run either way; could be picked up independently of any pretraining-side decision whenever checkpoint-compatibility work becomes a real priority.

### MF-139 — Insights from a real "fully-open" model sweep: OLMo 3, Nemotron 3 Super, Gemma, Pythia, LLM360 Amber/CrystalCoder, BigScience BLOOM

- **Priority / state:** P3 / Not started -- real, sourced findings, none yet scoped as a bounded task
- **Depends on:** none structurally
- **Deliverable:** user-requested deep dive (2026-09-16) into six projects specifically chosen because they claim to publish more than just weights -- real training code, data pipelines, and recipes, unlike the Hub-only model cards researched in [[MF-137]]. Four parallel research passes, each cross-checked against this project's own existing state before recording anything:
  1. **Gemma: confirmed no real training code at the time checked, same pattern as grok-1.** `google-deepmind/gemma`'s own README states plainly it is a fine-tuning/inference library operating on already-trained weights -- no pretraining code. Gemma is reportedly trained internally via Google's own `maxtext` (real, open, but TPU-cluster-scale infrastructure, not a Gemma-specific recipe writeup). Nothing actionable found at that time.
     - **Correction (2026-09-17), from the newer, Gemma4-era `reference/gemma-main.zip` archive -- this finding no longer holds as stated, see [[MF-144]] for full detail.** The current repo's `examples/dpo.py`/`examples/npo.py` are real, working DPO/NPO training configs (`gm.nn.AnchoredPolicy` wrapping a trainable policy + frozen reference model, the standard DPO mechanism). Real training code now exists in this repo -- just post-training (DPO/NPO from an already-pretrained checkpoint), not from-scratch pretraining, so "no pretraining code" stays technically accurate but "no real training code" does not.
  2. **Nemotron 3 Super: core architecture (MoE + Mamba/SSM hybrid) is entirely out of scope** (`AGENTS.md` bans both), and the publicly documented recipe detail was too shallow to mine further at the depth investigated. The one real data point -- a two-phase, 25T-token curriculum (20T diverse, then 5T quality-weighted late) -- is the same *class* of idea as this project's own already-implemented [[MF-095]] decay-curriculum and [[MF-135]] item 1 (dynamic mixture reweighting); corroborates, adds nothing new. Its real post-training stages (RLVR, SWE-RL, RLHF via a GenRM) are named but undocumented at the loss-function level, and need Slurm/cluster infrastructure regardless -- mild, non-actionable corroboration that [[MF-098]]'s eventual scope sits in well-trodden territory, nothing more.
  3. **OLMo 3 (`allenai/OLMo-core`, `allenai/dolma3`, `allenai/olmes`, `allenai/open-instruct` -- real repo names, verified): the richest source, one genuinely new idea plus real validation of this project's own foundational choices.**
     - **Real validation, not new but worth recording**: OLMo-core trains with **pure PyTorch, no Megatron/Trainer/Composer**, even at competitive 32B scale (fused kernels like FlashAttention/TransformerEngine are optional dependencies, not required infrastructure) -- direct, independent confirmation that MiniFrontier's own raw-PyTorch, no-heavy-framework approach (`AGENTS.md`'s own explicit ban on Transformers Trainer/Lightning/TRL/PEFT/Accelerate/DeepSpeed/FSDP in the core) is a legitimate real-world pattern, not just an educational simplification. Post-training (SFT/DPO/RL) also lives in a fully separate codebase (`open-instruct`) from pretraining -- a second independent confirmation (after SmolLM2/alignment-handbook) that MiniFrontier's own separate `sft.py` is the right shape, not something to eventually merge into the pretraining loop.
     - **Genuinely new**: a dedicated **long-context-extension training phase** using purpose-collected long-document data (`dolma3-longmino`), structurally separate from the main pretrain/midtrain phases -- distinct in kind from [[MF-095]]'s own decay-phase reweight (a single-run curriculum change, not a separate phase with its own dedicated data pool). Same caveat as the earlier RoPE-ABF finding on [[MF-137]]: targets long-context capability specifically, and this project's own `max_seq_len` (1024-2048) is far short of where the technique's value would show up -- a real, distinct idea, not a near-term priority.
     - **Minor context, not actionable**: Dolma 3's web-filtering removes 84.6% of raw documents, plus a further 67% via exact-hash dedup on what remains -- a real external data point on how aggressive a mature, SOTA pipeline's filtering is, useful only as future context if this project's own filtering thresholds are ever revisited.
  4. **Pythia (`EleutherAI/pythia`) -- genuinely new, cheap, and directly relevant to this project's own educational mission**: a **log-scale early-checkpoint density schedule** (steps 0,1,2,4,8,16,32,64,128,256,512,1000, then every 1000 thereafter -- 154 checkpoints total) instead of a fixed interval throughout. Structurally different from MiniFrontier's own constant-modulo `--checkpoint-interval`. Fine-grained density right where early-training dynamics (loss cliffs, capability emergence) actually happen is a real, cheap addition that directly serves an educational project's own stated goal of making training visible and explainable -- no new infrastructure needed, since atomic checkpoint writes already exist ([[MF-079]]/[[MF-130]]). All 8 Pythia sizes also train on identical data/order specifically to isolate model-size effects -- MiniFrontier already applies this principle informally where it matters ([[MF-066]]'s Edu-vs-Modern comparison reuses the same pool/seed), just not as a formalized cross-preset guarantee; minor, not worth its own task.
  5. **LLM360 Amber/CrystalCoder (`LLM360/amber-train`, `LLM360/crystalcoder-train`) -- genuinely new**: 360 checkpoints per model, each with a full data-to-checkpoint mapping and **complete published per-checkpoint metrics** (loss, gradient norm, eval results at every single checkpoint via W&B, not just a final summary). MiniFrontier's own `RunMetadata`/`run.json` (MF-005/037) is written once, at the end -- the periodic progress-log lines (loss/tokens-per-sec, now z_loss/mtp_loss per [[MF-136]]) exist only as unstructured print text, not a queryable per-checkpoint artifact. A cheap, real, structural gap: write a small structured record (loss, gradient norm, LR, tokens consumed) alongside each periodic checkpoint directory, not just the final one -- turns "grep the log" (exactly what was done by hand earlier this session to read [[MF-070]]'s own loss trend) into an actual queryable dataset.
  6. **BigScience BLOOM (`bigscience-workshop/Megatron-DeepSpeed`, real ROOTS corpus) -- one genuinely new, verified lead.** Their own published retrospective (huggingface.co/blog/bloom-megatron-deepspeed) states an earlier 104B attempt repeatedly diverged early in training; the two root causes identified were FP16 combined with insufficiently-cleaned data, and the fix that made the successful 176B run work was switching to BF16, cleaner data, **and adding a dedicated embedding layer-norm**. **Checked directly against `model.py` before recording this as new, not left unverified**: MiniFrontier's already-adopted `layer_norm_scaling` ([[MF-081]]) is a depth-dependent scalar `(layer_index + 1) ** -0.5` applied inside each block's own existing norm (`model.py:104`) -- a genuinely different mechanism from a dedicated normalization applied immediately after the token-embedding lookup, which MiniFrontier does not have at all (`token_embedding`'s output at `model.py:313` feeds straight into the first block, no norm in between). Real, cheap, scale-independent stability lever, confirmed novel, worth a bounded ablation slot. ROOTS's own governance/provenance discipline (four working groups, explicit per-source consent/licensing) is the same *kind* of rigor MiniFrontier's own `Document` schema ([[MF-023]]) and manifest-driven code ingestion ([[MF-051]]) already implement, just at a vastly different organizational scale -- validates, doesn't add a new mechanism.
  - **Explicitly out of scope across all six, noted only for completeness**: every cluster/multi-GPU-specific mechanism (Megatron-DeepSpeed's 3D parallelism, MaxText's TPU focus, Nemotron's Slurm/B200 RL infra, GCS-backed remote checkpoint storage) -- `AGENTS.md`'s single-consumer-GPU scope excludes all of it regardless of how well-documented.
- **Acceptance:** N/A until scoped -- three of these items (Pythia's checkpoint density, LLM360's per-checkpoint structured metrics, BLOOM's embedding layer-norm) are cheap and genuinely actionable without a large new bounded-ablation commitment; the embedding layer-norm specifically would want a real bounded quality comparison (matching this project's own established discipline) before being adopted as a default, not just ported because BLOOM used it. OLMo 3's long-context-extension-as-a-phase idea needs `max_seq_len` to matter first, which it doesn't yet at this project's own frozen scale.
- **Status note:** not scheduled; recorded per `AGENTS.md`'s "add newly discovered work to the backlog instead of hiding it" rule, at the user's own direct request for a deep, implementation-level dive across these six specific projects. [[MF-070]]'s real run remains the current priority.

### MF-140 — Real test: does hybrid local/global attention + GQA's efficiency actually pay off in quality at MiniFrontier's own short context?

- **Priority / state:** P1 / Not started -- elevated from a sub-item of [[MF-066]]/[[MF-137]] to its own tracked task, user-directed (2026-09-17), since this is the single open question that actually determines whether Modern's added sophistication is a real win, not just a different tradeoff
- **Depends on:** [[MF-070]] (needs the real, current-recipe 3B-token 150M-Modern checkpoint to exist first)
- **Deliverable:** resolve the real, still-open question first raised while discussing MF-066's own (stale) Edu-vs-Modern result: hybrid attention's real value (smaller KV cache, cheaper compute) is a long-context efficiency trade, and GQA's reduced KV-head count is a real, unconditional representational-capacity cost -- worth paying only when the efficiency is actually needed and the savings get reinvested into something that buys back at least as much quality (bigger batch, more tokens, longer context). At MiniFrontier's own `max_seq_len` (1024-2048), full attention is already cheap, so there is no direct evidence yet that this reinvestment is happening. Two concrete, already-scoped real tests close this:
  1. **The planned full-budget Edu-vs-Modern re-comparison** (already recorded on [[MF-066]]'s own entry): same real 3B-token budget, same current recipe (unlike the stale MF-065-era Modern that lost at 150M tokens), same tokenizer/mixture where comparable. This is the direct, current-generation answer to "does the *current* Modern beat Edu at matched budget," not the outdated 2026-08-26 snapshot.
  2. **[[MF-137]] item 2's GQA-ratio-direction test** (2:1, less aggressive than the frozen 3:1) run at the same real full budget -- tests whether restoring some of GQA's given-up capacity closes any gap found in (1), which would directly support the "capacity cost, not yet compensated" hypothesis.
  - **Three converging, independent signals already support this as the right thing to check, recorded on [[MF-066]]'s own follow-up note**: none of Qwen2.5-0.5B/Qwen3-0.6B/SmolLM2 use hybrid/windowed attention at all at small dense scale; Muse Glimmer's own real 64:1 window:context ratio is specifically justified by its 131K context; this project's own real `local_window` ablation (512 vs 1024) found no measurable quality difference at its own short training context.
- **Acceptance:** a real, reported comparison (not an estimate) of current-recipe Edu vs current-recipe Modern at the full 3B-token budget, plus the GQA-ratio arm, with an explicit verdict: does Modern's efficiency-oriented architecture win, lose, or tie on quality-per-token once the recipe is current and the budget is real -- and if it loses, whether a less-aggressive GQA ratio closes some or all of the gap.
- **Status note:** blocked on [[MF-070]]'s real run completing; cannot run in parallel with it (same GPU, same precious multi-day compute budget). This is the real, current-priority answer to "is Modern actually a win" -- not settled by anything measured so far.

### MF-141 — Real test: does raising `--accumulation-steps` actually help at the *current* combined recipe (head_dim=96 + activation-checkpointing + MTP), or was staying conservative the right call?

- **Priority / state:** P2 / Not started -- elevated from a disclosed-but-unverified caveat (recorded on the "External feedback on the live MF-070 run's effective batch size" note above) to its own tracked task, user-directed (2026-09-17)
- **Depends on:** [[MF-070]] (the live run itself cannot be used for this -- would need either a real throwaway diagnostic probe on the side, or to wait for the next real launch)
- **Deliverable:** [[MF-101]]'s own real sweep found raising `--accumulation-steps` off its default of 1 gives free throughput (8: +3.8%, 32: +9.4%, identical peak VRAM) -- but that sweep never ran with `head_dim=96`+`--activation-checkpointing`+MTP all active together, which is why the real [[MF-070]] launch deliberately stayed at `accumulation_steps=1` rather than assuming the earlier result transfers. This is real, disclosed, un-spent headroom, not evidence the current choice is already optimal -- it needs an actual measurement, not continued caution by default. A real bounded test (batch_size=2, accumulation_steps in {1, 8, 32}, identical `head_dim=96`/activation-checkpointing/MTP configuration, short throwaway run with `--no-checkpoint`) would confirm whether the free throughput win still holds at this exact combined recipe, and whether VRAM stays safe (this project's own real diagnostic-probe discipline, e.g. [[MF-108]]'s head_dim VRAM checks, is the template to follow -- not a live-run experiment).
- **Acceptance:** real measured tok/s and peak VRAM (reserved and allocated) at accumulation_steps in {1, 8, 32} under the exact current combined recipe, run as a real throwaway diagnostic (not an estimate), with an explicit go/no-go verdict for whether the next real release launch should raise `--accumulation-steps` off 1.
- **Status note:** not scheduled; safe to run any time on a spare GPU slot *other than* the live [[MF-070]] run (would compete for the same 8GB VRAM budget) -- a real candidate for the next dedicated GPU session, or immediately after [[MF-070]] completes if no side-GPU-time is available sooner.

### Real synthesis (2026-09-17): four hardware-reinvestment decisions that already worked, and the general OOM/paging lesson behind all of them

Not a new MF-task -- a consolidation of already-established real findings, recorded together because they answer a direct question this session ("are we actually reinvesting efficiency savings into quality, given this project's own real 8GB reference card, or just paying capacity costs for nothing?"). Four real, already-measured decisions confirm real reinvestment happened, distinct from [[MF-140]]'s still-open question about hybrid attention/GQA specifically:

1. **Activation checkpointing -> `head_dim=96`.** The cleanest example in the project. Activation checkpointing trades compute for memory (real ~33-38% throughput cost, [[MF-070]]/[[MF-108]]'s own diagnostic), and that freed memory is exactly what lets `head_dim=96` -- a real, measured quality win (-0.44% relative BPB, [[MF-108]]) -- fit inside 8GB at the real declared `seq_len=2048`. Without it, `head_dim=96` pages catastrophically (measured 12.37GB reserved vs 8.59GB physical). Directly measured, not inferred.
2. **FP16 over emulated BF16.** Not really a capacity trade -- correctly matching precision to hardware lacking native BF16 tensor cores (Turing). Real ~2.7x speedup ([[MF-075]]), no quality downside. Free money, correctly taken.
3. **Ring KV cache (~3.4x savings, [[MF-050]]).** Lets the model actually run generation on the 8GB reference card without OOMing on longer sequences -- the reinvestment is literally "can run on the target hardware at all," which is this project's own stated mission.
4. **MTP heads ([[MF-093]]/[[MF-105]]).** A real, small training-time cost (+0.06% train loss, ~5.3% slower) bought two things at once: a training-quality win (-0.74% PPL) *and* a separate, later inference-time win (self-speculative decoding) -- a genuine double reinvestment. The 150M-as-canonical-target / 350M-rejected sizing decision itself ([[MF-070]]'s real NO-GO) is downstream of this same "what does the real VRAM budget actually support" reasoning, not an arbitrary size pick.

**The general lesson under all four, worth stating plainly since it recurs constantly in this project's own real evidence**: an efficiency technique is a trade, not a free improvement -- it converts model capacity into a resource saving, and that's only worth it if (a) the resource being saved was a real, binding constraint, and (b) the saving gets reinvested into something that buys back at least as much quality. A same-tokens, same-everything comparison strips the reinvestment step out and can reveal a raw capacity cost with nothing offsetting it -- exactly what [[MF-066]]'s stale Edu-vs-Modern result may have shown, and exactly what [[MF-140]] exists to re-check properly.

**The recurring, real "OOMing" gotcha behind why activation checkpointing/head_dim/batch-size choices matter so much on this hardware, worth its own explicit note (already scattered across [[MF-050]]/[[MF-070]]/[[MF-108]], consolidated here)**: this project's reference card (RTX 2070 Super, 8GB, Turing, no native BF16) does **not** raise a clean CUDA out-of-memory error when a real config exceeds physical VRAM. Windows silently falls back to system-memory paging instead -- `nvidia-smi` still reports ~100% GPU utilization, the process keeps running, and there is no exception or crash -- but real throughput collapses 10-13x (measured: 150M/`head_dim=96` at the real declared `seq_len=2048` dropped from ~2,600-3,100 tok/s to ~239 tok/s once paging began, [[MF-070]]/[[MF-108]]'s own real diagnostic). **A run that is simply paging looks identical to a run that is merely slow, from the process's own output alone** -- the only way to tell the difference is checking `nvidia-smi`'s reported memory against the card's real physical total (8.59GB on this reference card) while a job is running, not waiting to see if it eventually errors, because it never will. This is the concrete, real reason every VRAM-sensitive decision in this project ([[MF-108]]'s head_dim testing, [[MF-070]]'s 350M NO-GO, activation-checkpointing's adoption) was verified with a real measured `nvidia-smi`/`peak_reserved_vram_bytes` check rather than trusted from an absence-of-errors alone.
- **Recorded in `README.md`'s "Frozen V1 targets" section** (a new subsection directly below the existing hardware-target table) so a reader trying this on their own similar-class card gets the same warning up front, not buried in `tasks/backlog.md`.

### MF-142 — Insights from real local `reference/` archives: `transformers`, `peft`/`LoRA-main`, `trl`/`unsloth` (llama.cpp/ggml findings recorded directly on [[MF-073]])

- **Priority / state:** P3 / Not started -- real, sourced findings; none scoped as a bounded task yet, except item 4 (CPU-offloaded gradient checkpointing) which is a real, cheap, directly-relevant-to-this-project's-own-recurring-VRAM-problem candidate worth prioritizing over the others here
- **Depends on:** none structurally; item 1 would join [[MF-133]]'s own bounded-ablation slot if ever scoped; item 4 could be tested independently of any pretraining decision, similar in kind to [[MF-141]]
- **Deliverable:** user downloaded seven real, full-source library archives locally (`reference/`, gitignored) specifically to mine for whole-stack insights, continuing this session's research arc. All four planned passes (`transformers`; `peft`/`LoRA-main`; `trl`/`unsloth`; `llama.cpp`/`ggml`, recorded separately on [[MF-073]]) are complete.
  1. **`transformers`: MiniFrontier's own HF adapter (`adapters/huggingface/`) already matches current real conventions precisely -- genuinely well done, not padding this.** `AutoConfig`/`AutoModel` registration via `config.auto_map`+`trust_remote_code=True`, `DynamicCache`+`ALL_ATTENTION_FUNCTIONS.get_interface(...)`, `_tied_weights_keys`, `GenerationMixin`, and full native-`transformers` logit/argmax parity verification (`hf_export.py`'s `compare_native_transformers`) are all real, correct, already present. Most notably: MiniFrontier's hybrid-attention `layer_types` generation is *structurally identical* to Gemma3's own real convention in `configuration_gemma3.py` -- independent convergence on the same pattern, strong validation, not luck.
     - **Two genuinely new leads from Gemma3's real config**: (a) `attn_logit_softcapping` is a first-class field there -- a second, better HF-ecosystem-integrated primary source for [[MF-133]]'s already-recorded soft-capping lead (previously sourced only from grok-1's raw JAX), worth citing alongside grok-1 if that lead is ever scoped. (b) `query_pre_attn_scalar` (Gemma3 default 256) -- attention scaling as a fixed, config-set scalar *decoupled from head_dim*, replacing the standard `head_dim**-0.5` MiniFrontier's own `MiniFrontierAttention.scaling` uses. Not previously considered in this project's research; real, checkable, scale-independent.
  2. **`peft`/`LoRA-main`: no case for adoption (already correctly excluded from the core per `AGENTS.md`), but two things worth a short note.** (a) **A genuinely clean educational pattern, worth a mention in `introduction.md` or a lab, not code adoption**: Microsoft's original `loralib` zero-initializes one of its two low-rank matrices (`lora_B`) so the adapted delta `B @ A` is exactly zero at initialization -- the model's forward pass is byte-identical to the frozen base before any training happens, and the learned change grows from zero. Same design *spirit* as MiniFrontier's own `residual_std_damping` (damping a fresh random init) but applied to a delta on a frozen base rather than a fresh network -- a real, reusable "guarantee identical behavior at t=0" pattern worth explaining as a general idea. (b) **A real, useful quality-reference number**: LoRA's own published results (RoBERTa-base, DeBERTa-XXL, GPT-2 Medium on GLUE/E2E) show it matching or slightly *beating* full fine-tuning at ~0.2-0.6% of trainable parameters. Not a guarantee at MiniFrontier's own tiny scale, but real evidence that "full-fine-tune-only" SFT ([[MF-116]]/[[MF-123]]) isn't obviously leaving quality on the table by skipping a PEFT path -- the real thing it leaves on the table is storage/multi-task-switching efficiency, a different axis than quality (consistent with this session's own efficiency-vs-quality framing, see [[MF-066]]'s follow-up note).
     - **Deeper follow-up (2026-09-17), at the user's own direct request, into PEFT's other ~40 tuner methods and optimizer-adjacent code (real code read: `adalora/{layer,model}.py`, `ia3/layer.py`, `vera/layer.py`, `optimizers/{loraplus,lorafa}.py`) -- all correctly still out of adoption scope (same frozen-base-plus-adapter paradigm, doesn't map onto MiniFrontier's own full-fine-tune design), but two genuinely interesting educational finds and one confirmed non-finding.** (c) **AdaLoRA's adaptive-rank allocation** -- parameterizes the update as a real SVD triplet (`B @ diag(E) @ A`, with an orthogonality-regularization loss forcing the singular vectors to stay orthonormal), and tracks per-parameter importance via gradient-sensitivity scoring (`|weight * gradient|`, EMA-smoothed, plus an EMA of the score's own fluctuation as an uncertainty term) -- lowest-importance singular triplets get pruned as a rank budget shrinks over training, reallocating capacity toward layers that need it more. A real, well-motivated, self-contained "adaptive budget via gradient-sensitivity scoring" pattern with no equivalent anywhere in MiniFrontier's own code -- genuinely worth a paragraph in `introduction.md` or a lab as importance-driven allocation, distinct from anything this project currently teaches. (d) **VeRA -- the one method here that's a meaningfully different idea, not just another factorization**: freezes its two low-rank matrices as *shared, non-trainable buffers reused verbatim across every layer in the model* (one random basis generated once, globally), training only two tiny per-layer diagonal scaling vectors on top. Every other real method skimmed (BOFT/LoHa/LoKr/OFT, LoRA-FA) is confirmed to be a different mathematical factorization of the same "small trainable delta on a frozen weight" idea, not individually worth cataloguing -- VeRA's "share one frozen random structure everywhere, only learn how much to scale each dimension" framing is the real outlier, worth a brief mention alongside AdaLoRA if this ever becomes a lab topic. **Confirmed not actionable, recorded so it isn't re-investigated**: LoRA+'s differential A/B learning rates is a real, general principle (two matrices multiplying into one update can deserve different learning rates) but only a conceptual echo of something MiniFrontier's own Muon/AdamW parameter-partitioning already does for a different reason (matrix-shaped vs. vector-shaped weights, not fan-in/fan-out asymmetry) -- not a concrete action item. LoRA-FA's custom gradient-projection step is LoRA-specific plumbing with no scale-independent idea to extract.
  3. **`trl`: one correctness confirmation, one new data point, one real point already in MiniFrontier's favor.** TRL explicitly warns that a chat template can define a turn-end marker yet still exclude it from the loss mask, so "the model is never trained to stop" -- checked directly against `sft.py`: MiniFrontier already handles this correctly by design (`<|eot|>` is explicitly `True` in the loss mask per its own docstring, MF-103) -- confirmed already-correct, not a gap. TRL's own general `SFTConfig` default learning rate is `2e-5` (its own docs note this is lower than other trainers' typical `5e-5`) -- sits between MiniFrontier's own default (`1e-5`) and SmolLM2's specific recipe (`1e-3`, already on [[MF-137]]); doesn't change that item's existing bounded-test recommendation, just adds a data point. TRL's own default packing (`"bfd"`) truncates/discards overflow content that doesn't fit a sequence -- MiniFrontier's own ribbon packing concatenates continuously with zero waste, relying on [[MF-085]]'s document-boundary masking instead of truncation. A real point already in MiniFrontier's favor, not a gap (TRL's own `"bfd_split"` variant, not its default, is the closer analog to what MiniFrontier already does). **This pass only read `SFTTrainer` -- a deeper follow-up pass (2026-09-17) into TRL's `DPOTrainer`/`DistillationTrainer`/`RewardTrainer`/`GRPOTrainer` is recorded directly on [[MF-098]]**, since those findings (DPO defaults, a real vocab-matching blocking constraint for logit distillation, a third soft-capping source) are far more relevant to that task's own open scope decision than to this general reference-archive entry.
  4. **`unsloth`: one correctly out-of-scope confirmation, one genuinely new and potentially significant idea.** Its fused cross-entropy kernel is real, custom Triton -- correctly out of scope (`AGENTS.md` bans custom CUDA/Triton kernels from the core); it does support fused logit soft-capping, a small further corroboration of [[MF-133]]'s already-recorded lead, not new on its own. **The genuinely new, NOT kernel-requiring idea**: Unsloth implements **CPU-offloaded gradient checkpointing** as a distinct mode from standard recompute-based checkpointing -- pure orchestration (moving activations to CPU RAM during backward), no Triton or custom kernel involved, so nothing here is blocked by the core's own kernel ban. Their own code comment states a real, specific crossover heuristic: "below seq 512 the offloading overhead is not worth it and standard gc is faster." MiniFrontier's own activation checkpointing is recompute-based only, and its real, tight-VRAM recipes (150M/`head_dim=96`/`seq_len=2048`, already well past that 512-token threshold, and especially 350M's own outright VRAM NO-GO) are exactly the situations this lever targets. **Worth a real, bounded, measured test on this project's own actual hardware before trusting it helps** -- matches this project's own "measure before trusting" discipline (e.g. [[MF-108]]) -- but if it does, this is a real, previously-unexplored candidate for closing some of the gap behind 350M's own NO-GO decision, not just a marginal 150M optimization.
- **Acceptance:** N/A until scoped -- item 1's `query_pre_attn_scalar` idea would need a real primary-source read of Gemma3's own paper/report for its stated rationale before a bounded-ablation slot, matching this project's established bar; item 2 is documentation-only, no ablation needed; item 4 needs a real throwaway VRAM/throughput diagnostic (same shape as [[MF-108]]'s own probes) at both 150M's real recipe and 350M's own previously-rejected configuration, to see whether it changes either verdict.
- **Status note:** not scheduled; recorded per `AGENTS.md`'s "add newly discovered work to the backlog instead of hiding it" rule, at the user's own direct request. [[MF-070]]'s real run remains the current priority; item 4 (CPU-offloaded checkpointing) is the one worth revisiting first among everything in this entry, given its direct relevance to this project's own recurring VRAM ceiling.

### MF-143 — Reuse the KV cache across multi-turn chat instead of re-prefilling the whole conversation every turn (a single-user "RadixAttention-lite")

- **Priority / state:** P2 / Not started -- real, verified gap, cheap to fix, no new dependencies
- **Depends on:** none structurally; builds on already-existing primitives (`KVCache`, `model.forward(cache=..., position_start=...)`, [[MF-028]]/[[MF-029]])
- **Deliverable:** user asked directly whether SGLang (RadixAttention, continuous batching, structured decoding) has anything useful for MiniFrontier. RadixAttention's actual mechanism -- a radix-tree cache shared across many *concurrent* users/requests -- only pays off under continuous batching/multi-tenant serving, already correctly excluded from the core (`AGENTS.md` bans SGLang/vLLM from the core; matches the already-established treatment of grok-1's own continuous-batching `InferenceRunner`, [[MF-138]]). But checking whether the underlying *idea* -- not recomputing a shared prefix -- applies in single-user form surfaced a real, verified gap: **it does, and MiniFrontier doesn't have it.**
  - **Confirmed directly by reading the code, not assumed**: `chat.py`'s `generate_assistant` calls `fit_messages_to_context` on the *entire* `messages` list every single call, then `model.generate()` (`model.py:486-525`) builds a fresh KV cache and does a full prefill from scratch -- there is no parameter anywhere in `generate()`/`generation.py`'s `generate()` to pass in or return a cache from a previous call. A 10-turn conversation therefore re-prefills the same, growing shared prefix 10 separate times instead of extending one persisted cache with just each new turn's tokens.
  - **The fix needs no new machinery**: `model.forward()` already accepts `cache: KVCache | None` and `position_start: int | None` (the primitives [[MF-028]]/[[MF-029]] already built) -- the gap is purely that `generate()`/`generate_assistant()` never expose a way to thread a cache through across separate calls. A real implementation would have `generate_assistant` optionally accept and return a `KVCache`, extend it by only the new turn's tokens (system/user turns since the last call) rather than the whole history, and validate the cache's stored prefix still matches (in case history is edited/truncated between turns, which would require a fresh cache rather than corrupting a stale one).
  - **Real, honest scale caveat**: MiniFrontier's own real generation is currently CPU/small-batch/short-context by design (fixed-shape single-user educational scope, `MF-067`) -- so the actual wall-clock savings from this fix at MiniFrontier's own real scale (50-150M params, short conversations) may be modest compared to what RadixAttention buys a 30B+ multi-tenant server. Worth measuring the real savings on a realistic multi-turn conversation length before treating this as a big win, not just implementing on the assumption it obviously helps.
- **Acceptance:** a real multi-turn chat session (3+ turns) measurably avoids re-running the transformer over already-processed prefix tokens on turns after the first -- verified by either a real wall-clock/FLOP comparison (with vs. without cache reuse) or a direct instrumentation count of tokens actually run through the model per turn, not just code review. Cache-invalidation correctness (edited/truncated history) is tested explicitly, not assumed safe.
- **Status note:** not scheduled; recorded per `AGENTS.md`'s "add newly discovered work to the backlog instead of hiding it" rule, directly from the user's own SGLang question. A separate, lower-confidence, not-yet-scoped idea from the same question, recorded for completeness rather than as its own task: **structured/constrained decoding** (JSON-schema or grammar-constrained sampling) is a real capability MiniFrontier's own sampling toolkit (`generation.py`, min-p/repetition-control/degeneration metrics, [[MF-030]]/[[MF-091]]) does not have -- genuinely new, but priority relative to this task is unclear for an educational single-user project and not argued for here.

### MF-144 — Real Gemma4 architecture/recipe insights (arXiv, Hub config, local `reference/gemma-main.zip`, vLLM's serving recipe)

- **Priority / state:** P3 / Not started -- real, sourced findings, none scoped as a bounded task
- **Depends on:** none structurally; item 3 (split RoPE proportion) would join [[MF-081]]/[[MF-107]]'s own bounded-ablation slot if ever scoped; item 6 (DPO reference) is directly relevant to [[MF-098]]
- **Deliverable:** user-requested deep dive into Gemma4 specifically (arXiv `2607.02770`, `huggingface.co/google/gemma-4-12B/config.json`, the local `reference/gemma-main.zip` archive, and vLLM's `Google/Gemma4.md` recipe), continuing this session's research arc. All findings verified directly against primary sources, not secondhand summaries -- including one real correction to this project's own `more-context.md`.
  1. **Local:global attention ratio is scale-dependent, not a fixed 5:1 -- corrects a secondhand claim already recorded in `more-context.md`.** Verified directly in `gemma/gm/nn/gemma4/_gemma4.py`: the smallest real model (E2B, 35 layers) uses a **4 local : 1 global** repeating pattern; E4B (42 layers), the 31B dense model, and the 26B-A4B MoE variant all use **5:1**. `more-context.md`'s "Gemma 4 uses a 5:1 ratio" is true only for the larger variants -- the smallest, most MiniFrontier-comparable model is proportionally more global-heavy than that citation implies.
  2. **Genuinely new axis, not seen anywhere else in this project's research: local and global layers use different head dimensions.** `head_dim=256` for local/sliding layers, `_DEFAULT_GLOBAL_KEY_SIZE=512` for global layers -- literally double. MiniFrontier's own `head_dim_override` ([[MF-108]]) applies one uniform value across every layer regardless of local/global role; Gemma4 differentiates by attention type. Not previously considered here.
  3. **Split local/global RoPE goes further than theta alone -- a real, narrower variant of an already-rejected idea.** Confirmed `local_base_frequency=10_000` / `global_base_frequency=1_000_000` (matches MiniFrontier's own already-adopted `global_rope_theta` split, [[MF-081]] -- corroboration, not new). But **also** `local_rope_proportion=1.0` (full rotation) vs `global_rope_proportion=0.25` (partial rotation) -- partial RoPE applied *only* to global layers, never local. [[MF-107]]'s own bounded test applied partial RoPE uniformly across every layer and found it lost outright -- Gemma4's real design never tests that broad version; it isolates partial rotation to global layers specifically, a configuration MF-107's own test didn't isolate. Worth flagging as a real, more targeted variant of an already-rejected idea, not a reason to treat MF-107 as unsettled.
  4. **Global layers get extra, more aggressive GQA compression plus K=V weight tying, at larger scale only.** In the 31B/26B-A4B variants: `num_global_kv_heads` (4 and 2 respectively) is set lower than that layer's own `num_kv_heads` (16 and 8) -- global layers compress KV even further than local ones already do, plus `k_eq_v_global=True` ties global layers' K and V projections to the same weights for further real savings. Both are disabled (`None`/`False`) on the smaller E2B/E4B -- a real, size-gated technique, only adopted once the model is large enough to afford the extra complexity.
  5. **A real, novel KV-cache-layer-sharing mechanism, distinct from GQA and distinct from MiniFrontier's own ring cache ([[MF-050]]) or [[MF-143]]'s cross-turn reuse idea.** `KVCacheSharingConfig`/`frac_shared_layers`: some of the later layers don't compute their own KV at all -- they reuse an earlier layer's cache verbatim. Used only on the smaller models (E2B: 20 of 35 layers shared; E4B: 18 of 42) -- disabled (`frac_shared_layers=0.0`) on the 31B/26B-A4B variants. Genuinely new idea, real primary source, not yet tied to any other MF-number.
  6. **The local `gemma-main.zip` repo has real, working DPO/NPO training code -- directly relevant to [[MF-098]], and a correction to [[MF-139]]'s earlier finding (recorded there).** `examples/dpo.py`/`examples/npo.py` use `gm.nn.AnchoredPolicy` (a trainable policy plus a frozen reference-model copy -- the standard DPO mechanism) and `gm.ckpts.AnchoredPolicyLoader`. Starts from an already-pretrained checkpoint, not from-scratch pretraining, but is a real, concrete, primary-source DPO reference implementation for whenever [[MF-098]] gets real attention.
  7. **Gemma3's `attn_logit_softcapping` was dropped in Gemma4 -- recorded directly on [[MF-133]].** Every real Gemma4 factory config has `attn_logits_soft_cap=None`; only `final_logit_softcap=30.0` (applied once, to final output logits, not per-layer) remains. `query_pre_attn_scalar` ([[MF-137]]/[[MF-142]]'s other Gemma3 lead) has no equivalent named field anywhere in Gemma4's source -- plausibly superseded by the differentiated local/global head_dim itself (item 2).
  - **Already covered, confirmed unchanged**: QK-norm (`qk_norm_with_scale=True`) matches [[MF-137]]'s existing confirmation that MiniFrontier already has this on Modern.
  - **Explicitly out of scope**: vLLM's real quantization details (W4A16 compressed-tensors, mobile INT2 embeddings) are production multi-GPU serving formats, a different scheme family from GGUF/K-quants ([[MF-073]]/[[MF-074]]) -- not portable. The MoE variant (26B-A4B) and vision/audio encoders are also out of scope per `AGENTS.md`.
- **Acceptance:** N/A until scoped -- item 3 needs a real, narrower bounded-ablation design (global-layers-only partial RoPE, not the broad version [[MF-107]] already tested) before a slot is committed; item 6 is a real reference to read from when [[MF-098]] is actually picked up, not itself an implementation task.
- **Status note:** not scheduled; recorded per `AGENTS.md`'s "add newly discovered work to the backlog instead of hiding it" rule, at the user's own direct request. [[MF-070]]'s real run remains the current priority.

### MF-145 — Real Unsloth data-prep insights: synthetic-data quality filtering and a confirmed real gap in text cleaning

- **Priority / state:** P3 / Not started -- one confirmed real gap (item 3), two ideas relevant to [[MF-098]]'s eventual scope
- **Depends on:** none structurally; items 1-2 feed directly into [[MF-098]]'s "offline distillation" half if that task is ever scoped
- **Deliverable:** an earlier pass this session mined `reference/unsloth-main.zip` for its low-level training kernels only ([[MF-142]] item 4: fused cross-entropy, CPU-offloaded checkpointing). At the user's own direct follow-up request, a second pass went deep on `unsloth/dataprep/` specifically (`raw_text.py`, `synthetic.py`, `synthetic_configs.py`) -- real code read, not summarized from memory, cross-checked against MiniFrontier's own actual `data.py`/`shards.py`.
  1. **LLM-as-judge synthetic-data quality filtering -- genuinely new, directly relevant to [[MF-098]]'s "offline distillation from a stronger teacher" half.** `synthetic_configs.py`'s `qa_rating` prompt has the same (or a different) model rate each generated QA pair 1-10 on a strict JSON schema, then `cleanup_threshold` drops anything below the bar before it's used for training -- a real, self-contained generate-then-rate-then-filter loop, all via one local teacher model. Confirmed as a genuine gap by reading `data.py` directly: MiniFrontier's own quality filtering relies entirely on *upstream-provided* classifier scores (FineWeb-Edu's own field) -- `data.py`'s own comment states Cosmopedia v2 explicitly has "no quality-score field to filter on," and there is no self-generated judgment mechanism anywhere in this project. Directly usable if/when [[MF-098]]'s distillation half is ever scoped -- a real, cheap way to filter synthetic teacher output before training on it, rather than trusting every generation equally.
  2. **Generation-budget-aware chunking -- a small, concrete, correct detail worth remembering for the same eventual use case.** `SyntheticDataKit.prepare_qa_generation` computes `chunk_size = max_seq_length - max_generation_tokens*2 - 128`, deliberately reserving room in one context window for *both* the source-text chunk and the model's generated output, with a small safety margin.
